     name with =VALUE.  This sets the value of the variable is to VALUE
     while modifying the readonly attribute.

     The return status is zero unless an invalid option is supplied, one
     of the NAME arguments is not a valid shell variable or function
     name, or the â€˜-fâ€™ option is supplied with a name that is not a
     shell function.

â€˜returnâ€™
          return [N]

     Stop executing a shell function or sourced file and return the
     value N to its caller.  If N is not supplied, the return value is
     the exit status of the last command executed.  If â€˜returnâ€™ is
     executed by a trap handler, the last command used to determine the
     status is the last command executed before the trap handler.  If
     â€˜returnâ€™ is executed during a â€˜DEBUGâ€™ trap, the last command used
     to determine the status is the last command executed by the trap
     handler before â€˜returnâ€™ was invoked.

     When â€˜returnâ€™ is used to terminate execution of a script being
     executed with the â€˜.â€™ (â€˜sourceâ€™) builtin, it returns either N or
     the exit status of the last command executed within the script as
     the exit status of the script.  If N is supplied, the return value
     is its least significant 8 bits.

     Any command associated with the â€˜RETURNâ€™ trap is executed before
     execution resumes after the function or script.

     The return status is non-zero if â€˜returnâ€™ is supplied a non-numeric
     argument or is used outside a function and not during the execution
     of a script by â€˜.â€™ or â€˜sourceâ€™.

â€˜shiftâ€™
          shift [N]

     Shift the positional parameters to the left by N: the positional
     parameters from N+1 ... â€˜$#â€™ are renamed to â€˜$1â€™ ... â€˜$#â€™-N.
     Parameters represented by the numbers â€˜$#â€™ down to â€˜$#â€™-N+1 are
     unset.  N must be a non-negative number less than or equal to â€˜$#â€™.
     If N is not supplied, it is assumed to be 1.  If N is zero or
     greater than â€˜$#â€™, the positional parameters are not changed.  The
     return status is zero unless N is greater than â€˜$#â€™ or less than
     zero, non-zero otherwise.

â€˜testâ€™
â€˜[â€™
          test EXPR

     Evaluate a conditional expression EXPR and return a status of 0
     (true) or 1 (false).  Each operator and operand must be a separate
     argument.  Expressions are composed of the primaries described
     below in Bash Conditional Expressions.  â€˜testâ€™ does not accept any
     options, nor does it accept and ignore an argument of â€˜--â€™ as
     signifying the end of options.  When using the â€˜[â€™ form, the last
     argument to the command must be a â€˜]â€™.

     Expressions may be combined using the following operators, listed
     in decreasing order of precedence.  The evaluation depends on the
     number of arguments; see below.  â€˜testâ€™ uses operator precedence
     when there are five or more arguments.

     â€˜! EXPRâ€™
          True if EXPR is false.

     â€˜( EXPR )â€™
          Returns the value of EXPR.  This may be used to override
          normal operator precedence.

     â€˜EXPR1 -a EXPR2â€™
          True if both EXPR1 and EXPR2 are true.

     â€˜EXPR1 -o EXPR2â€™
          True if either EXPR1 or EXPR2 is true.

     The â€˜testâ€™ and â€˜[â€™ builtins evaluate conditional expressions using
     a set of rules based on the number of arguments.

     0 arguments
          The expression is false.

     1 argument
          The expression is true if, and only if, the argument is not
          null.

     2 arguments
          If the first argument is â€˜!â€™, the expression is true if and
          only if the second argument is null.  If the first argument is
          one of the unary conditional operators (see Bash Conditional
          Expressions), the expression is true if the unary test is
          true.  If the first argument is not a valid unary operator,
          the expression is false.

     3 arguments
          The following conditions are applied in the order listed.

            1. If the second argument is one of the binary conditional
               operators (see Bash Conditional Expressions), the result
               of the expression is the result of the binary test using
               the first and third arguments as operands.  The â€˜-aâ€™ and
               â€˜-oâ€™ operators are considered binary operators when there
               are three arguments.
            2. If the first argument is â€˜!â€™, the value is the negation
               of the two-argument test using the second and third
               arguments.
            3. If the first argument is exactly â€˜(â€™ and the third
               argument is exactly â€˜)â€™, the result is the one-argument
               test of the second argument.
            4. Otherwise, the expression is false.

     4 arguments
          The following conditions are applied in the order listed.

            1. If the first argument is â€˜!â€™, the result is the negation
               of the three-argument expression composed of the
               remaining arguments.
            2. If the first argument is exactly â€˜(â€™ and the fourth
               argument is exactly â€˜)â€™, the result is the two-argument
               test of the second and third arguments.
            3. Otherwise, the expression is parsed and evaluated
               according to precedence using the rules listed above.

     5 or more arguments
          The expression is parsed and evaluated according to precedence
          using the rules listed above.

     If the shell is in POSIX mode, or if the expression is part of the
     â€˜[[â€™ command, the â€˜<â€™ and â€˜>â€™ operators sort using the current
     locale.  If the shell is not in POSIX mode, the â€˜testâ€™ and â€˜[â€™
     commands sort lexicographically using ASCII ordering.

     The historical operator-precedence parsing with 4 or more arguments
     can lead to ambiguities when it encounters strings that look like
     primaries.  The POSIX standard has deprecated the â€˜-aâ€™ and â€˜-oâ€™
     primaries and enclosing expressions within parentheses.  Scripts
     should no longer use them.  It's much more reliable to restrict
     test invocations to a single primary, and to replace uses of â€˜-aâ€™
     and â€˜-oâ€™ with the shell's â€˜&&â€™ and â€˜||â€™ list operators.  For
     example, use

          test -n string1 && test -n string2

     instead of

          test -n string1 -a -n string2

â€˜timesâ€™
          times

     Print out the user and system times used by the shell and its
     children.  The return status is zero.

â€˜trapâ€™
          trap [-lpP] [ACTION] [SIGSPEC ...]

     The ACTION is a command that is read and executed when the shell
     receives any of the signals SIGSPEC.  If ACTION is absent (and
     there is a single SIGSPEC) or equal to â€˜-â€™, each specified
     SIGSPEC's disposition is reset to the value it had when the shell
     was started.  If ACTION is the null string, then the signal
     specified by each SIGSPEC is ignored by the shell and commands it
     invokes.

     If no arguments are supplied, â€˜trapâ€™ prints the actions associated
     with each trapped signal as a set of â€˜trapâ€™ commands that can be
     reused as shell input to restore the current signal dispositions.

     If ACTION is not present and â€˜-pâ€™ has been supplied, â€˜trapâ€™
     displays the trap commands associated with each SIGSPEC, or, if no
     SIGSPECs are supplied, for all trapped signals, as a set of â€˜trapâ€™
     commands that can be reused as shell input to restore the current
     signal dispositions.  The â€˜-Pâ€™ option behaves similarly, but
     displays only the actions associated with each SIGSPEC argument.
     â€˜-Pâ€™ requires at least one SIGSPEC argument.  The â€˜-Pâ€™ or â€˜-pâ€™
     options may be used in a subshell environment (e.g., command
     substitution) and, as long as they are used before â€˜trapâ€™ is used
     to change a signal's handling, will display the state of its
     parent's traps.

     The â€˜-lâ€™ option prints a list of signal names and their
     corresponding numbers.  Each SIGSPEC is either a signal name or a
     signal number.  Signal names are case insensitive and the â€˜SIGâ€™
     prefix is optional.  If â€˜-lâ€™ is supplied with no SIGSPEC arguments,
     it prints a list of valid signal names.

     If a SIGSPEC is â€˜0â€™ or â€˜EXITâ€™, ACTION is executed when the shell
     exits.  If a SIGSPEC is â€˜DEBUGâ€™, ACTION is executed before every
     simple command, â€˜forâ€™ command, â€˜caseâ€™ command, â€˜selectâ€™ command, ((
     arithmetic command, [[ conditional command, arithmetic â€˜forâ€™
     command, and before the first command executes in a shell function.
     Refer to the description of the â€˜extdebugâ€™ shell option (see The
     Shopt Builtin) for details of its effect on the â€˜DEBUGâ€™ trap.  If a
     SIGSPEC is â€˜RETURNâ€™, ACTION is executed each time a shell function
     or a script executed with the â€˜.â€™ or â€˜sourceâ€™ builtins finishes
     executing.

     If a SIGSPEC is â€˜ERRâ€™, ACTION is executed whenever a pipeline
     (which may consist of a single simple command), a list, or a
     compound command returns a non-zero exit status, subject to the
     following conditions.  The â€˜ERRâ€™ trap is not executed if the failed
     command is part of the command list immediately following an
     â€˜untilâ€™ or â€˜whileâ€™ reserved word, part of the test following the
     â€˜ifâ€™ or â€˜elifâ€™ reserved words, part of a command executed in a â€˜&&â€™
     or â€˜||â€™ list except the command following the final â€˜&&â€™ or â€˜||â€™,
     any command in a pipeline but the last, (subject to the state of
     the â€˜pipefailâ€™ shell option), or if the command's return status is
     being inverted using â€˜!â€™.  These are the same conditions obeyed by
     the â€˜errexitâ€™ (â€˜-eâ€™) option.

     When the shell is not interactive, signals ignored upon entry to a
     non-interactive shell cannot be trapped or reset.  Interactive
     shells permit trapping signals ignored on entry.  Trapped signals
     that are not being ignored are reset to their original values in a
     subshell or subshell environment when one is created.

     The return status is zero unless a SIGSPEC does not specify a valid
     signal; non-zero otherwise.

â€˜trueâ€™
          true

     Does nothing, returns a 0 status.

â€˜umaskâ€™
          umask [-p] [-S] [MODE]

     Set the shell process's file creation mask to MODE.  If MODE begins
     with a digit, it is interpreted as an octal number; if not, it is
     interpreted as a symbolic mode mask similar to that accepted by the
     â€˜chmodâ€™ command.  If MODE is omitted, â€˜umaskâ€™ prints the current
     value of the mask.  If the â€˜-Sâ€™ option is supplied without a MODE
     argument, â€˜umaskâ€™ prints the mask in a symbolic format; the default
     output is an octal number.  If the â€˜-pâ€™ option is supplied, and
     MODE is omitted, the output is in a form that may be reused as
     input.  The return status is zero if the mode is successfully
     changed or if no MODE argument is supplied, and non-zero otherwise.

     Note that when the mode is interpreted as an octal number, each
     number of the umask is subtracted from â€˜7â€™.  Thus, a umask of â€˜022â€™
     results in permissions of â€˜755â€™.

â€˜unsetâ€™
          unset [-fnv] [NAME]

     Remove each variable or function NAME.  If the â€˜-vâ€™ option is
     given, each NAME refers to a shell variable and that variable is
     removed.  If the â€˜-fâ€™ option is given, the NAMEs refer to shell
     functions, and the function definition is removed.  If the â€˜-nâ€™
     option is supplied, and NAME is a variable with the â€˜namerefâ€™
     attribute, NAME will be unset rather than the variable it
     references.  â€˜-nâ€™ has no effect if the â€˜-fâ€™ option is supplied.  If
     no options are supplied, each NAME refers to a variable; if there
     is no variable by that name, a function with that name, if any, is
     unset.  Readonly variables and functions may not be unset.  When
     variables or functions are removed, they are also removed from the
     environment passed to subsequent commands.  Some shell variables
     may not be unset.  Some shell variables lose their special behavior
     if they are unset; such behavior is noted in the description of the
     individual variables.  The return status is zero unless a NAME is
     readonly or may not be unset.

4.2 Bash Builtin Commands
=========================

This section describes builtin commands which are unique to or have been
extended in Bash.  Some of these commands are specified in the POSIX
standard.

â€˜aliasâ€™
          alias [-p] [NAME[=VALUE] ...]

     Without arguments or with the â€˜-pâ€™ option, â€˜aliasâ€™ prints the list
     of aliases on the standard output in a form that allows them to be
     reused as input.  If arguments are supplied, define an alias for
     each NAME whose VALUE is given.  If no VALUE is given, print the
     name and value of the alias NAME.  A trailing space in VALUE causes
     the next word to be checked for alias substitution when the alias
     is expanded during command parsing.  â€˜aliasâ€™ returns true unless a
     NAME is given (without a corresponding =VALUE) for which no alias
     has been defined.  Aliases are described in Aliases.

â€˜bindâ€™
          bind [-m KEYMAP] [-lsvSVX]
          bind [-m KEYMAP] [-q FUNCTION] [-u FUNCTION] [-r KEYSEQ]
          bind [-m KEYMAP] -f FILENAME
          bind [-m KEYMAP] -x KEYSEQ[: ]SHELL-COMMAND
          bind [-m KEYMAP] KEYSEQ:FUNCTION-NAME
          bind [-m KEYMAP] KEYSEQ:READLINE-COMMAND
          bind [-m KEYMAP] -p|-P [READLINE-COMMAND]
          bind READLINE-COMMAND-LINE

     Display current Readline (see Command Line Editing) key and
     function bindings, bind a key sequence to a Readline function or
     macro or to a shell command, or set a Readline variable.  Each
     non-option argument is a key binding or command as it would appear
     in a Readline initialization file (see Readline Init File), but
     each binding or command must be passed as a separate argument;
     e.g., â€˜"\C-x\C-r":re-read-init-fileâ€™.

     In the following descriptions, options that display output in a
     form available to be re-read format their output as commands that
     would appear in a Readline initialization file or that would be
     supplied as individual arguments to a â€˜bindâ€™ command.

     Options, if supplied, have the following meanings:

     â€˜-m KEYMAPâ€™
          Use KEYMAP as the keymap to be affected by the subsequent
          bindings.  Acceptable KEYMAP names are â€˜emacsâ€™,
          â€˜emacs-standardâ€™, â€˜emacs-metaâ€™, â€˜emacs-ctlxâ€™, â€˜viâ€™, â€˜vi-moveâ€™,
          â€˜vi-commandâ€™, and â€˜vi-insertâ€™.  â€˜viâ€™ is equivalent to
          â€˜vi-commandâ€™ (â€˜vi-moveâ€™ is also a synonym); â€˜emacsâ€™ is
          equivalent to â€˜emacs-standardâ€™.

     â€˜-lâ€™
          List the names of all Readline functions.

     â€˜-pâ€™
          Display Readline function names and bindings in such a way
          that they can be used as an argument to a subsequent â€˜bindâ€™
          command or in a Readline initialization file.  If arguments
          remain after option processing, â€˜bindâ€™ treats them as readline
          command names and restricts output to those names.

     â€˜-Pâ€™
          List current Readline function names and bindings.  If
          arguments remain after option processing, â€˜bindâ€™ treats them
          as readline command names and restricts output to those names.

     â€˜-sâ€™
          Display Readline key sequences bound to macros and the strings
          they output in such a way that they can be used as an argument
          to a subsequent â€˜bindâ€™ command or in a Readline initialization
          file.

     â€˜-Sâ€™
          Display Readline key sequences bound to macros and the strings
          they output.

     â€˜-vâ€™
          Display Readline variable names and values in such a way that
          they can be used as an argument to a subsequent â€˜bindâ€™ command
          or in a Readline initialization file.

     â€˜-Vâ€™
          List current Readline variable names and values.

     â€˜-f FILENAMEâ€™
          Read key bindings from FILENAME.

     â€˜-q FUNCTIONâ€™
          Display key sequences that invoke the named Readline FUNCTION.

     â€˜-u FUNCTIONâ€™
          Unbind all key sequences bound to the named Readline FUNCTION.

     â€˜-r KEYSEQâ€™
          Remove any current binding for KEYSEQ.

     â€˜-x KEYSEQ:SHELL-COMMANDâ€™
          Cause SHELL-COMMAND to be executed whenever KEYSEQ is entered.
          The separator between KEYSEQ and SHELL-COMMAND is either
          whitespace or a colon optionally followed by whitespace.  If
          the separator is whitespace, SHELL-COMMAND must be enclosed in
          double quotes and Readline expands any of its special
          backslash-escapes in SHELL-COMMAND before saving it.  If the
          separator is a colon, any enclosing double quotes are
          optional, and Readline does not expand the command string
          before saving it.  Since the entire key binding expression
          must be a single argument, it should be enclosed in single
          quotes.  When SHELL-COMMAND is executed, the shell sets the
          â€˜READLINE_LINEâ€™ variable to the contents of the Readline line
          buffer and the â€˜READLINE_POINTâ€™ and â€˜READLINE_MARKâ€™ variables
          to the current location of the insertion point and the saved
          insertion point (the MARK), respectively.  The shell assigns
          any numeric argument the user supplied to the
          â€˜READLINE_ARGUMENTâ€™ variable.  If there was no argument, that
          variable is not set.  If the executed command changes the
          value of any of â€˜READLINE_LINEâ€™, â€˜READLINE_POINTâ€™, or
          â€˜READLINE_MARKâ€™, those new values will be reflected in the
          editing state.

     â€˜-Xâ€™
          List all key sequences bound to shell commands and the
          associated commands in a format that can be reused as an
          argument to a subsequent â€˜bindâ€™ command.

     The return status is zero unless an invalid option is supplied or
     an error occurs.

â€˜builtinâ€™
          builtin [SHELL-BUILTIN [ARGS]]

     Execute the specified shell builtin SHELL-BUILTIN, passing it ARGS,
     and return its exit status.  This is useful when defining a shell
     function with the same name as a shell builtin, retaining the
     functionality of the builtin within the function.  The return
     status is non-zero if SHELL-BUILTIN is not a shell builtin command.

â€˜callerâ€™
          caller [EXPR]

     Returns the context of any active subroutine call (a shell function
     or a script executed with the â€˜.â€™ or â€˜sourceâ€™ builtins).

     Without EXPR, â€˜callerâ€™ displays the line number and source filename
     of the current subroutine call.  If a non-negative integer is
     supplied as EXPR, â€˜callerâ€™ displays the line number, subroutine
     name, and source file corresponding to that position in the current
     execution call stack.  This extra information may be used, for
     example, to print a stack trace.  The current frame is frame 0.

     The return value is 0 unless the shell is not executing a
     subroutine call or EXPR does not correspond to a valid position in
     the call stack.

â€˜commandâ€™
          command [-pVv] COMMAND [ARGUMENTS ...]

     The â€˜commandâ€™ builtin runs COMMAND with ARGUMENTS ignoring any
     shell function named COMMAND.  Only shell builtin commands or
     commands found by searching the â€˜PATHâ€™ are executed.  If there is a
     shell function named â€˜lsâ€™, running â€˜command lsâ€™ within the function
     will execute the external command â€˜lsâ€™ instead of calling the
     function recursively.  The â€˜-pâ€™ option means to use a default value
     for â€˜PATHâ€™ that is guaranteed to find all of the standard
     utilities.  The return status in this case is 127 if COMMAND cannot
     be found or an error occurred, and the exit status of COMMAND
     otherwise.

     If either the â€˜-Vâ€™ or â€˜-vâ€™ option is supplied, â€˜commandâ€™ prints a
     description of COMMAND.  The â€˜-vâ€™ option displays a single word
     indicating the command or file name used to invoke COMMAND; the
     â€˜-Vâ€™ option produces a more verbose description.  In this case, the
     return status is zero if COMMAND is found, and non-zero if not.

â€˜declareâ€™
          declare [-aAfFgiIlnrtux] [-p] [NAME[=VALUE] ...]

     Declare variables and give them attributes.  If no NAMEs are given,
     then display the values of variables or shell functions instead.

     The â€˜-pâ€™ option will display the attributes and values of each
     NAME.  When â€˜-pâ€™ is used with NAME arguments, additional options,
     other than â€˜-fâ€™ and â€˜-Fâ€™, are ignored.

     When â€˜-pâ€™ is supplied without NAME arguments, â€˜declareâ€™ will
     display the attributes and values of all variables having the
     attributes specified by the additional options.  If no other
     options are supplied with â€˜-pâ€™, â€˜declareâ€™ will display the
     attributes and values of all shell variables.  The â€˜-fâ€™ option
     restricts the display to shell functions.

     The â€˜-Fâ€™ option inhibits the display of function definitions; only
     the function name and attributes are printed.  If the â€˜extdebugâ€™
     shell option is enabled using â€˜shoptâ€™ (see The Shopt Builtin), the
     source file name and line number where each NAME is defined are
     displayed as well.  â€˜-Fâ€™ implies â€˜-fâ€™.

     The â€˜-gâ€™ option forces variables to be created or modified at the
     global scope, even when â€˜declareâ€™ is executed in a shell function.
     It is ignored in when â€˜declareâ€™ is not executed in a shell
     function.

     The â€˜-Iâ€™ option causes local variables to inherit the attributes
     (except the â€˜namerefâ€™ attribute) and value of any existing variable
     with the same NAME at a surrounding scope.  If there is no existing
     variable, the local variable is initially unset.

     The following options can be used to restrict output to variables
     with the specified attributes or to give variables attributes:

     â€˜-aâ€™
          Each NAME is an indexed array variable (see Arrays).

     â€˜-Aâ€™
          Each NAME is an associative array variable (see Arrays).

     â€˜-fâ€™
          Each NAME refers to a shell function.

     â€˜-iâ€™
          The variable is to be treated as an integer; arithmetic
          evaluation (see Shell Arithmetic) is performed when the
          variable is assigned a value.

     â€˜-lâ€™
          When the variable is assigned a value, all upper-case
          characters are converted to lower-case.  The upper-case
          attribute is disabled.

     â€˜-nâ€™
          Give each NAME the â€˜namerefâ€™ attribute, making it a name
          reference to another variable.  That other variable is defined
          by the value of NAME.  All references, assignments, and
          attribute modifications to NAME, except for those using or
          changing the â€˜-nâ€™ attribute itself, are performed on the
          variable referenced by NAME's value.  The nameref attribute
          cannot be applied to array variables.

     â€˜-râ€™
          Make NAMEs readonly.  These names cannot then be assigned
          values by subsequent assignment statements or unset.

     â€˜-tâ€™
          Give each NAME the â€˜traceâ€™ attribute.  Traced functions
          inherit the â€˜DEBUGâ€™ and â€˜RETURNâ€™ traps from the calling shell.
          The trace attribute has no special meaning for variables.

     â€˜-uâ€™
          When the variable is assigned a value, all lower-case
          characters are converted to upper-case.  The lower-case
          attribute is disabled.

     â€˜-xâ€™
          Mark each NAME for export to subsequent commands via the
          environment.

     Using â€˜+â€™ instead of â€˜-â€™ turns off the specified attribute instead,
     with the exceptions that â€˜+aâ€™ and â€˜+Aâ€™ may not be used to destroy
     array variables and â€˜+râ€™ will not remove the readonly attribute.

     When used in a function, â€˜declareâ€™ makes each NAME local, as with
     the â€˜localâ€™ command, unless the â€˜-gâ€™ option is supplied.  If a
     variable name is followed by =VALUE, the value of the variable is
     set to VALUE.

     When using â€˜-aâ€™ or â€˜-Aâ€™ and the compound assignment syntax to
     create array variables, additional attributes do not take effect
     until subsequent assignments.

     The return status is zero unless an invalid option is encountered,
     an attempt is made to define a function using â€˜-f foo=barâ€™, an
     attempt is made to assign a value to a readonly variable, an
     attempt is made to assign a value to an array variable without
     using the compound assignment syntax (see Arrays), one of the NAMEs
     is not a valid shell variable name, an attempt is made to turn off
     readonly status for a readonly variable, an attempt is made to turn
     off array status for an array variable, or an attempt is made to
     display a non-existent function with â€˜-fâ€™.

â€˜echoâ€™
          echo [-neE] [ARG ...]

     Output the ARGs, separated by spaces, terminated with a newline.
     The return status is 0 unless a write error occurs.  If â€˜-nâ€™ is
     specified, the trailing newline is not printed.

     If the â€˜-eâ€™ option is given, â€˜echoâ€™ interprets the following
     backslash-escaped characters.  The â€˜-Eâ€™ option disables
     interpretation of these escape characters, even on systems where
     they are interpreted by default.  The â€˜xpg_echoâ€™ shell option
     determines whether or not â€˜echoâ€™ interprets any options and expands
     these escape characters.  â€˜echoâ€™ does not interpret â€˜--â€™ to mean
     the end of options.

     â€˜echoâ€™ interprets the following escape sequences:
     â€˜\aâ€™
          alert (bell)
     â€˜\bâ€™
          backspace
     â€˜\câ€™
          suppress further output
     â€˜\eâ€™
     â€˜\Eâ€™
          escape
     â€˜\fâ€™
          form feed
     â€˜\nâ€™
          new line
     â€˜\râ€™
          carriage return
     â€˜\tâ€™
          horizontal tab
     â€˜\vâ€™
          vertical tab
     â€˜\\â€™
          backslash
     â€˜\0NNNâ€™
          The eight-bit character whose value is the octal value NNN
          (zero to three octal digits).
     â€˜\xHHâ€™
          The eight-bit character whose value is the hexadecimal value
          HH (one or two hex digits).
     â€˜\uHHHHâ€™
          The Unicode (ISO/IEC 10646) character whose value is the
          hexadecimal value HHHH (one to four hex digits).
     â€˜\UHHHHHHHHâ€™
          The Unicode (ISO/IEC 10646) character whose value is the
          hexadecimal value HHHHHHHH (one to eight hex digits).

     â€˜echoâ€™ writes any unrecognized backslash-escaped characters
     unchanged.

â€˜enableâ€™
          enable [-a] [-dnps] [-f FILENAME] [NAME ...]

     Enable and disable builtin shell commands.  Disabling a builtin
     allows an executable file which has the same name as a shell
     builtin to be executed without specifying a full pathname, even
     though the shell normally searches for builtins before files.

     If â€˜-nâ€™ is supplied, the NAMEs are disabled.  Otherwise NAMEs are
     enabled.  For example, to use the â€˜testâ€™ binary found using â€˜$PATHâ€™
     instead of the shell builtin version, type â€˜enable -n testâ€™.

     If the â€˜-pâ€™ option is supplied, or no NAME arguments are supplied,
     print a list of shell builtins.  With no other arguments, the list
     consists of all enabled shell builtins.  The â€˜-nâ€™ option means to
     print only disabled builtins.  The â€˜-aâ€™ option means to list each
     builtin with an indication of whether or not it is enabled.  The
     â€˜-sâ€™ option means to restrict â€˜enableâ€™ to the POSIX special
     builtins.

     The â€˜-fâ€™ option means to load the new builtin command NAME from
     shared object FILENAME, on systems that support dynamic loading.
     If FILENAME does not contain a slash.  Bash will use the value of
     the â€˜BASH_LOADABLES_PATHâ€™ variable as a colon-separated list of
     directories in which to search for FILENAME.  The default for
     â€˜BASH_LOADABLES_PATHâ€™ is system-dependent, and may include "."  to
     force a search of the current directory.  The â€˜-dâ€™ option will
     delete a builtin loaded with â€˜-fâ€™.  If â€˜-sâ€™ is used with â€˜-fâ€™, the
     new builtin becomes a POSIX special builtin (see Special Builtins).

     If no options are supplied and a NAME is not a shell builtin,
     â€˜enableâ€™ will attempt to load NAME from a shared object named NAME,
     as if the command were â€˜enable -f NAME NAMEâ€™.

     The return status is zero unless a NAME is not a shell builtin or
     there is an error loading a new builtin from a shared object.

â€˜helpâ€™
          help [-dms] [PATTERN]

     Display helpful information about builtin commands.  If PATTERN is
     specified, â€˜helpâ€™ gives detailed help on all commands matching
     PATTERN as described below; otherwise it displays a list of all
     builtins and shell compound commands.

     Options, if supplied, have the following meanings:

     â€˜-dâ€™
          Display a short description of each PATTERN
     â€˜-mâ€™
          Display the description of each PATTERN in a manpage-like
          format
     â€˜-sâ€™
          Display only a short usage synopsis for each PATTERN

     If PATTERN contains pattern matching characters (see Pattern
     Matching) it's treated as a shell pattern and â€˜helpâ€™ prints the
     description of each help topic matching PATTERN.

     If not, and PATTERN exactly matches the name of a help topic,
     â€˜helpâ€™ prints the description associated with that topic.
     Otherwise, â€˜helpâ€™ performs prefix matching and prints the
     descriptions of all matching help topics.

     The return status is zero unless no command matches PATTERN.

â€˜letâ€™
          let EXPRESSION [EXPRESSION ...]

     The â€˜letâ€™ builtin allows arithmetic to be performed on shell
     variables.  Each EXPRESSION is evaluated as an arithmetic
     expression according to the rules given below in Shell Arithmetic.
     If the last EXPRESSION evaluates to 0, â€˜letâ€™ returns 1; otherwise
     â€˜letâ€™ returns 0.

â€˜localâ€™
          local [OPTION] NAME[=VALUE] ...

     For each argument, create a local variable named NAME, and assign
     it VALUE.  The OPTION can be any of the options accepted by
     â€˜declareâ€™.  â€˜localâ€™ can only be used within a function; it makes
     the variable NAME have a visible scope restricted to that function
     and its children.  It is an error to use â€˜localâ€™ when not within a
     function.

     If NAME is â€˜-â€™, it makes the set of shell options local to the
     function in which â€˜localâ€™ is invoked: any shell options changed
     using the â€˜setâ€™ builtin inside the function after the call to
     â€˜localâ€™ are restored to their original values when the function
     returns.  The restore is performed as if a series of â€˜setâ€™ commands
     were executed to restore the values that were in place before the
     function.

     With no operands, â€˜localâ€™ writes a list of local variables to the
     standard output.

     The return status is zero unless â€˜localâ€™ is used outside a
     function, an invalid NAME is supplied, or NAME is a readonly
     variable.

â€˜logoutâ€™
          logout [N]

     Exit a login shell, returning a status of N to the shell's parent.

â€˜mapfileâ€™
          mapfile [-d DELIM] [-n COUNT] [-O ORIGIN] [-s COUNT]
              [-t] [-u FD] [-C CALLBACK] [-c QUANTUM] [ARRAY]

     Read lines from the standard input, or from file descriptor FD if
     the â€˜-uâ€™ option is supplied, into the indexed array variable ARRAY.
     The variable â€˜MAPFILEâ€™ is the default ARRAY.  Options, if supplied,
     have the following meanings:

     â€˜-dâ€™
          Use the first character of DELIM to terminate each input line,
          rather than newline.  If DELIM is the empty string, â€˜mapfileâ€™
          will terminate a line when it reads a NUL character.
     â€˜-nâ€™
          Copy at most COUNT lines.  If COUNT is 0, copy all lines.
     â€˜-Oâ€™
          Begin assigning to ARRAY at index ORIGIN.  The default index
          is 0.
     â€˜-sâ€™
          Discard the first COUNT lines read.
     â€˜-tâ€™
          Remove a trailing DELIM (default newline) from each line read.
     â€˜-uâ€™
          Read lines from file descriptor FD instead of the standard
          input.
     â€˜-Câ€™
          Evaluate CALLBACK each time QUANTUM lines are read.  The â€˜-câ€™
          option specifies QUANTUM.
     â€˜-câ€™
          Specify the number of lines read between each call to
          CALLBACK.

     If â€˜-Câ€™ is specified without â€˜-câ€™, the default quantum is 5000.
     When CALLBACK is evaluated, it is supplied the index of the next
     array element to be assigned and the line to be assigned to that
     element as additional arguments.  CALLBACK is evaluated after the
     line is read but before the array element is assigned.

     If not supplied with an explicit origin, â€˜mapfileâ€™ will clear ARRAY
     before assigning to it.

     â€˜mapfileâ€™ returns zero unless an invalid option or option argument
     is supplied, ARRAY is invalid or unassignable, or if ARRAY is not
     an indexed array.

â€˜printfâ€™
          printf [-v VAR] FORMAT [ARGUMENTS]

     Write the formatted ARGUMENTS to the standard output under the
     control of the FORMAT.  The â€˜-vâ€™ option assigns the output to the
     variable VAR rather than printing it to the standard output.

     The FORMAT is a character string which contains three types of
     objects: plain characters, which are simply copied to standard
     output, character escape sequences, which are converted and copied
     to the standard output, and format specifications, each of which
     causes printing of the next successive ARGUMENT.  In addition to
     the standard â€˜printf(3)â€™ format characters â€˜cCsSndiouxXeEfFgGaAâ€™,
     â€˜printfâ€™ interprets the following additional format specifiers:

     â€˜%bâ€™
          Causes â€˜printfâ€™ to expand backslash escape sequences in the
          corresponding ARGUMENT in the same way as â€˜echo -eâ€™ (see Bash
          Builtins).
     â€˜%qâ€™
          Causes â€˜printfâ€™ to output the corresponding ARGUMENT in a
          format that can be reused as shell input.  â€˜%qâ€™ and â€˜%Qâ€™P use
          the ANSI-C quoting style (see ANSI-C Quoting) if any
          characters in the argument string require it, and backslash
          quoting otherwise.  If the format string uses the â€˜printfâ€™
          _alternate form_, these two formats quote the argument string
          using single quotes.

     â€˜%Qâ€™
          like â€˜%qâ€™, but applies any supplied precision to the ARGUMENT
          before quoting it.

     â€˜%(DATEFMT)Tâ€™
          Causes â€˜printfâ€™ to output the date-time string resulting from
          using DATEFMT as a format string for â€˜strftimeâ€™(3).  The
          corresponding ARGUMENT is an integer representing the number
          of seconds since the epoch.  This format specifier recognizes
          Two special argument values: -1 represents the current time,
          and -2 represents the time the shell was invoked.  If no
          argument is specified, conversion behaves as if -1 had been
          supplied.  This is an exception to the usual â€˜printfâ€™
          behavior.

     The %b, %q, and %T format specifiers all use the field width and
     precision arguments from the format specification and write that
     many bytes from (or use that wide a field for) the expanded
     argument, which usually contains more characters than the original.

     The %n format specifier accepts a corresponding argument that is
     treated as a shell variable name.

     The %s and %c format specifiers accept an l (long) modifier, which
     forces them to convert the argument string to a wide-character
     string and apply any supplied field width and precision in terms of
     characters, not bytes.  The %S and %C format specifiers are
     equivalent to %ls and %lc, respectively.

     Arguments to non-string format specifiers are treated as C language
     constants, except that a leading plus or minus sign is allowed, and
     if the leading character is a single or double quote, the value is
     the numeric value of the following character, using the current
     locale.

     The FORMAT is reused as necessary to consume all of the ARGUMENTS.
     If the FORMAT requires more ARGUMENTS than are supplied, the extra
     format specifications behave as if a zero value or null string, as
     appropriate, had been supplied.  The return value is zero on
     success, non-zero if an invalid option is supplied or a write or
     assignment error occurs.

â€˜readâ€™
          read [-Eers] [-a ANAME] [-d DELIM] [-i TEXT] [-n NCHARS]
              [-N NCHARS] [-p PROMPT] [-t TIMEOUT] [-u FD] [NAME ...]

     Read one line from the standard input, or from the file descriptor
     FD supplied as an argument to the â€˜-uâ€™ option, split it into words
     as described above in Word Splitting, and assign the first word to
     the first NAME, the second word to the second NAME, and so on.  If
     there are more words than names, the remaining words and their
     intervening delimiters are assigned to the last NAME.  If there are
     fewer words read from the input stream than names, the remaining
     names are assigned empty values.  The characters in the value of
     the â€˜IFSâ€™ variable are used to split the line into words using the
     same rules the shell uses for expansion (described above in Word
     Splitting).  The backslash character â€˜\â€™ removes any special
     meaning for the next character read and is used for line
     continuation.

     Options, if supplied, have the following meanings:

     â€˜-a ANAMEâ€™
          The words are assigned to sequential indices of the array
          variable ANAME, starting at 0.  All elements are removed from
          ANAME before the assignment.  Other NAME arguments are
          ignored.

     â€˜-d DELIMâ€™
          The first character of DELIM terminates the input line, rather
          than newline.  If DELIM is the empty string, â€˜readâ€™ will
          terminate a line when it reads a NUL character.

     â€˜-eâ€™
          If the standard input is coming from a terminal, â€˜readâ€™ uses
          Readline (see Command Line Editing) to obtain the line.
          Readline uses the current (or default, if line editing was not
          previously active) editing settings, but uses Readline's
          default filename completion.

     â€˜-Eâ€™
          If the standard input is coming from a terminal, â€˜readâ€™ uses
          Readline (see Command Line Editing) to obtain the line.
          Readline uses the current (or default, if line editing was not
          previously active) editing settings, but uses Bash's default
          completion, including programmable completion.

     â€˜-i TEXTâ€™
          If Readline is being used to read the line, â€˜readâ€™ places TEXT
          into the editing buffer before editing begins.

     â€˜-n NCHARSâ€™
          â€˜readâ€™ returns after reading NCHARS characters rather than
          waiting for a complete line of input, unless it encounters EOF
          or â€˜readâ€™ times out, but honors a delimiter if it reads fewer
          than NCHARS characters before the delimiter.

     â€˜-N NCHARSâ€™
          â€˜readâ€™ returns after reading exactly NCHARS characters rather
          than waiting for a complete line of input, unless it
          encounters EOF or â€˜readâ€™ times out.  Delimiter characters in
          the input are not treated specially and do not cause â€˜readâ€™ to
          return until it has read NCHARS characters.  The result is not
          split on the characters in â€˜IFSâ€™; the intent is that the
          variable is assigned exactly the characters read (with the
          exception of backslash; see the â€˜-râ€™ option below).

     â€˜-p PROMPTâ€™
          Display PROMPT, without a trailing newline, before attempting
          to read any input, but only if input is coming from a
          terminal.

     â€˜-râ€™
          If this option is given, backslash does not act as an escape
          character.  The backslash is considered to be part of the
          line.  In particular, a backslash-newline pair may not then be
          used as a line continuation.

     â€˜-sâ€™
          Silent mode.  If input is coming from a terminal, characters
          are not echoed.

     â€˜-t TIMEOUTâ€™
          Cause â€˜readâ€™ to time out and return failure if it does not
          read a complete line of input (or a specified number of
          characters) within TIMEOUT seconds.  TIMEOUT may be a decimal
          number with a fractional portion following the decimal point.
          This option is only effective if â€˜readâ€™ is reading input from
          a terminal, pipe, or other special file; it has no effect when
          reading from regular files.  If â€˜readâ€™ times out, it saves any
          partial input read into the specified variable NAME, and
          returns a status greater than 128.  If TIMEOUT is 0, â€˜readâ€™
          returns immediately, without trying to read any data.  In this
          case, the exit status is 0 if input is available on the
          specified file descriptor, or the read will return EOF,
          non-zero otherwise.

     â€˜-u FDâ€™
          Read input from file descriptor FD instead of the standard
          input.

     Other than the case where DELIM is the empty string, â€˜readâ€™ ignores
     any NUL characters in the input.

     If no NAMEs are supplied, â€˜readâ€™ assigns the line read, without the
     ending delimiter but otherwise unmodified, to the variable â€˜REPLYâ€™.

     The exit status is zero, unless end-of-file is encountered, â€˜readâ€™
     times out (in which case the status is greater than 128), a
     variable assignment error (such as assigning to a readonly
     variable) occurs, or an invalid file descriptor is supplied as the
     argument to â€˜-uâ€™.

â€˜readarrayâ€™
          readarray [-d DELIM] [-n COUNT] [-O ORIGIN] [-s COUNT]
              [-t] [-u FD] [-C CALLBACK] [-c QUANTUM] [ARRAY]

     Read lines from the standard input into the indexed array variable
     ARRAY, or from file descriptor FD if the â€˜-uâ€™ option is supplied.

     A synonym for â€˜mapfileâ€™.

â€˜sourceâ€™
          source [-p PATH] FILENAME [ARGUMENTS]

     A synonym for â€˜.â€™ (see Bourne Shell Builtins).

â€˜typeâ€™
          type [-afptP] [NAME ...]

     Indicate how each NAME would be interpreted if used as a command
     name.

     If the â€˜-tâ€™ option is used, â€˜typeâ€™ prints a single word which is
     one of â€˜aliasâ€™, â€˜keywordâ€™, â€˜functionâ€™, â€˜builtinâ€™, or â€˜fileâ€™, if
     NAME is an alias, shell reserved word, shell function, shell
     builtin, or executable file, respectively.  If the NAME is not
     found, â€˜typeâ€™ prints nothing and returns a failure status.

     If the â€˜-pâ€™ option is used, â€˜typeâ€™ either returns the name of the
     executable file that would be found by searching â€˜$PATHâ€™ for
     â€˜nameâ€™, or nothing if â€˜-tâ€™ would not return â€˜fileâ€™.

     The â€˜-Pâ€™ option forces a path search for each NAME, even if â€˜-tâ€™
     would not return â€˜fileâ€™.

     If a NAME is present in the table of hashed commands, options â€˜-pâ€™
     and â€˜-Pâ€™ print the hashed value, which is not necessarily the file
     that appears first in â€˜$PATHâ€™.

     If the â€˜-aâ€™ option is used, â€˜typeâ€™ returns all of the places that
     contain a command named NAME.  This includes aliases, reserved
     words, functions, and builtins, but the path search options (â€˜-pâ€™
     and â€˜-Pâ€™) can be supplied to restrict the output to executable
     files.  If â€˜-aâ€™ is supplied with â€˜-pâ€™, â€˜typeâ€™ does not look in the
     table of hashed commands, and only performs a â€˜PATHâ€™ search for
     NAME.

     If the â€˜-fâ€™ option is used, â€˜typeâ€™ does not attempt to find shell
     functions, as with the â€˜commandâ€™ builtin.

     The return status is zero if all of the NAMEs are found, non-zero
     if any are not found.

â€˜typesetâ€™
          typeset [-afFgrxilnrtux] [-p] [NAME[=VALUE] ...]

     The â€˜typesetâ€™ command is supplied for compatibility with the Korn
     shell.  It is a synonym for the â€˜declareâ€™ builtin command.

â€˜ulimitâ€™
          ulimit [-HS] -a
          ulimit [-HS] [-bcdefiklmnpqrstuvxPRT] [LIMIT]

     â€˜ulimitâ€™ provides control over the resources available to the shell
     and to processes it starts, on systems that allow such control.  If
     an option is given, it is interpreted as follows:

     â€˜-Sâ€™
          Change and report the soft limit associated with a resource.

     â€˜-Hâ€™
          Change and report the hard limit associated with a resource.

     â€˜-aâ€™
          Report all current limits; no limits are set.

     â€˜-bâ€™
          The maximum socket buffer size.

     â€˜-câ€™
          The maximum size of core files created.

     â€˜-dâ€™
          The maximum size of a process's data segment.

     â€˜-eâ€™
          The maximum scheduling priority ("nice").

     â€˜-fâ€™
          The maximum size of files written by the shell and its
          children.

     â€˜-iâ€™
          The maximum number of pending signals.

     â€˜-kâ€™
          The maximum number of kqueues that may be allocated.

     â€˜-lâ€™
          The maximum size that may be locked into memory.

     â€˜-mâ€™
          The maximum resident set size (many systems do not honor this
          limit).

     â€˜-nâ€™
          The maximum number of open file descriptors (most systems do
          not allow this value to be set).

     â€˜-pâ€™
          The pipe buffer size.

     â€˜-qâ€™
          The maximum number of bytes in POSIX message queues.

     â€˜-râ€™
          The maximum real-time scheduling priority.

     â€˜-sâ€™
          The maximum stack size.

     â€˜-tâ€™
          The maximum amount of cpu time in seconds.

     â€˜-uâ€™
          The maximum number of processes available to a single user.

     â€˜-vâ€™
          The maximum amount of virtual memory available to the shell,
          and, on some systems, to its children.

     â€˜-xâ€™
          The maximum number of file locks.

     â€˜-Pâ€™
          The maximum number of pseudoterminals.

     â€˜-Râ€™
          The maximum time a real-time process can run before blocking,
          in microseconds.

     â€˜-Tâ€™
          The maximum number of threads.

     If LIMIT is supplied, and the â€˜-aâ€™ option is not used, LIMIT is the
     new value of the specified resource.  The special LIMIT values
     â€˜hardâ€™, â€˜softâ€™, and â€˜unlimitedâ€™ stand for the current hard limit,
     the current soft limit, and no limit, respectively.  A hard limit
     cannot be increased by a non-root user once it is set; a soft limit
     may be increased up to the value of the hard limit.  Otherwise,
     â€˜ulimitâ€™ prints the current value of the soft limit for the
     specified resource, unless the â€˜-Hâ€™ option is supplied.  When more
     than one resource is specified, the limit name and unit, if
     appropriate, are printed before the value.  When setting new
     limits, if neither â€˜-Hâ€™ nor â€˜-Sâ€™ is supplied, â€˜ulimitâ€™ sets both
     the hard and soft limits.  If no option is supplied, then â€˜-fâ€™ is
     assumed.

     Values are in 1024-byte increments, except for â€˜-tâ€™, which is in
     seconds; â€˜-Râ€™, which is in microseconds; â€˜-pâ€™, which is in units of
     512-byte blocks; â€˜-Pâ€™, â€˜-Tâ€™, â€˜-bâ€™, â€˜-kâ€™, â€˜-nâ€™ and â€˜-uâ€™, which are
     unscaled values; and, when in POSIX mode (see Bash POSIX Mode),
     â€˜-câ€™ and â€˜-fâ€™, which are in 512-byte increments.

     The return status is zero unless an invalid option or argument is
     supplied, or an error occurs while setting a new limit.

â€˜unaliasâ€™
          unalias [-a] [NAME ... ]

     Remove each NAME from the list of aliases.  If â€˜-aâ€™ is supplied,
     remove all aliases.  The return value is true unless a supplied
     NAME is not a defined alias.  Aliases are described in Aliases.

4.3 Modifying Shell Behavior
============================

4.3.1 The Set Builtin
---------------------

This builtin is so complicated that it deserves its own section.  â€˜setâ€™
allows you to change the values of shell options and set the positional
parameters, or to display the names and values of shell variables.

â€˜setâ€™
          set [-abefhkmnptuvxBCEHPT] [-o OPTION-NAME] [--] [-] [ARGUMENT ...]
          set [+abefhkmnptuvxBCEHPT] [+o OPTION-NAME] [--] [-] [ARGUMENT ...]
          set -o
          set +o

     If no options or arguments are supplied, â€˜setâ€™ displays the names
     and values of all shell variables and functions, sorted according
     to the current locale, in a format that may be reused as input for
     setting or resetting the currently-set variables.  Read-only
     variables cannot be reset.  In POSIX mode, only shell variables are
     listed.

     When options are supplied, they set or unset shell attributes.  Any
     arguments remaining after option processing replace the positional
     parameters.

     Options, if specified, have the following meanings:

     â€˜-aâ€™
          Each variable or function that is created or modified is given
          the export attribute and marked for export to the environment
          of subsequent commands.

     â€˜-bâ€™
          Cause the status of terminated background jobs to be reported
          immediately, rather than before printing the next primary
          prompt or, under some circumstances, when a foreground command
          exits.  This is effective only when job control is enabled.

     â€˜-eâ€™
          Exit immediately if a pipeline (see Pipelines), which may
          consist of a single simple command (see Simple Commands), a
          list (see Lists), or a compound command (see Compound
          Commands) returns a non-zero status.  The shell does not exit
          if the command that fails is part of the command list
          immediately following a â€˜whileâ€™ or â€˜untilâ€™ reserved word, part
          of the test in an â€˜ifâ€™ statement, part of any command executed
          in a â€˜&&â€™ or â€˜||â€™ list except the command following the final
          â€˜&&â€™ or â€˜||â€™, any command in a pipeline but the last (subject
          to the state of the â€˜pipefailâ€™ shell option), or if the
          command's return status is being inverted with â€˜!â€™.  If a
          compound command other than a subshell returns a non-zero
          status because a command failed while â€˜-eâ€™ was being ignored,
          the shell does not exit.  A trap on â€˜ERRâ€™, if set, is executed
          before the shell exits.

          This option applies to the shell environment and each subshell
          environment separately (see Command Execution Environment),
          and may cause subshells to exit before executing all the
          commands in the subshell.

          If a compound command or shell function executes in a context
          where â€˜-eâ€™ is being ignored, none of the commands executed
          within the compound command or function body will be affected
          by the â€˜-eâ€™ setting, even if â€˜-eâ€™ is set and a command returns
          a failure status.  If a compound command or shell function
          sets â€˜-eâ€™ while executing in a context where â€˜-eâ€™ is ignored,
          that setting will not have any effect until the compound
          command or the command containing the function call completes.

     â€˜-fâ€™
          Disable filename expansion (globbing).

     â€˜-hâ€™
          Locate and remember (hash) commands as they are looked up for
          execution.  This option is enabled by default.

     â€˜-kâ€™
          All arguments in the form of assignment statements are placed
          in the environment for a command, not just those that precede
          the command name.

     â€˜-mâ€™
          Job control is enabled (see Job Control).  All processes run
          in a separate process group.  When a background job completes,
          the shell prints a line containing its exit status.

     â€˜-nâ€™
          Read commands but do not execute them.  This may be used to
          check a script for syntax errors.  This option is ignored by
          interactive shells.

     â€˜-o OPTION-NAMEâ€™

          Set the option corresponding to OPTION-NAME.  If â€˜-oâ€™ is
          supplied with no OPTION-NAME, â€˜setâ€™ prints the current shell
          options settings.  If â€˜+oâ€™ is supplied with no OPTION-NAME,
          â€˜setâ€™ prints a series of â€˜setâ€™ commands to recreate the
          current option settings on the standard output.  Valid option
          names are:

          â€˜allexportâ€™
               Same as â€˜-aâ€™.

          â€˜braceexpandâ€™
               Same as â€˜-Bâ€™.

          â€˜emacsâ€™
               Use an â€˜emacsâ€™-style line editing interface (see Command
               Line Editing).  This also affects the editing interface
               used for â€˜read -eâ€™.

          â€˜errexitâ€™
               Same as â€˜-eâ€™.

          â€˜errtraceâ€™
               Same as â€˜-Eâ€™.

          â€˜functraceâ€™
               Same as â€˜-Tâ€™.

          â€˜hashallâ€™
               Same as â€˜-hâ€™.

          â€˜histexpandâ€™
               Same as â€˜-Hâ€™.

          â€˜historyâ€™
               Enable command history, as described in Bash History
               Facilities.  This option is on by default in interactive
               shells.

          â€˜ignoreeofâ€™
               An interactive shell will not exit upon reading EOF.

          â€˜keywordâ€™
               Same as â€˜-kâ€™.

          â€˜monitorâ€™
               Same as â€˜-mâ€™.

          â€˜noclobberâ€™
               Same as â€˜-Câ€™.

          â€˜noexecâ€™
               Same as â€˜-nâ€™.

          â€˜noglobâ€™
               Same as â€˜-fâ€™.

          â€˜nologâ€™
               Currently ignored.

          â€˜notifyâ€™
               Same as â€˜-bâ€™.

          â€˜nounsetâ€™
               Same as â€˜-uâ€™.

          â€˜onecmdâ€™
               Same as â€˜-tâ€™.

          â€˜physicalâ€™
               Same as â€˜-Pâ€™.

          â€˜pipefailâ€™
               If set, the return value of a pipeline is the value of
               the last (rightmost) command to exit with a non-zero
               status, or zero if all commands in the pipeline exit
               successfully.  This option is disabled by default.

          â€˜posixâ€™
               Enable POSIX mode; change the behavior of Bash where the
               default operation differs from the POSIX standard to
               match the standard (see Bash POSIX Mode).  This is
               intended to make Bash behave as a strict superset of that
               standard.

          â€˜privilegedâ€™
               Same as â€˜-pâ€™.

          â€˜verboseâ€™
               Same as â€˜-vâ€™.

          â€˜viâ€™
               Use a â€˜viâ€™-style line editing interface.  This also
               affects the editing interface used for â€˜read -eâ€™.

          â€˜xtraceâ€™
               Same as â€˜-xâ€™.

     â€˜-pâ€™
          Turn on privileged mode.  In this mode, the â€˜$BASH_ENVâ€™ and
          â€˜$ENVâ€™ files are not processed, shell functions are not
          inherited from the environment, and the â€˜SHELLOPTSâ€™,
          â€˜BASHOPTSâ€™, â€˜CDPATHâ€™ and â€˜GLOBIGNOREâ€™ variables, if they
          appear in the environment, are ignored.  If the shell is
          started with the effective user (group) id not equal to the
          real user (group) id, and the â€˜-pâ€™ option is not supplied,
          these actions are taken and the effective user id is set to
          the real user id.  If the â€˜-pâ€™ option is supplied at startup,
          the effective user id is not reset.  Turning this option off
          causes the effective user and group ids to be set to the real
          user and group ids.

     â€˜-râ€™
          Enable restricted shell mode (see The Restricted Shell).  This
          option cannot be unset once it has been set.

     â€˜-tâ€™
          Exit after reading and executing one command.

     â€˜-uâ€™
          Treat unset variables and parameters other than the special
          parameters â€˜@â€™ or â€˜*â€™, or array variables subscripted with â€˜@â€™
          or â€˜*â€™, as an error when performing parameter expansion.  An
          error message will be written to the standard error, and a
          non-interactive shell will exit.

     â€˜-vâ€™
          Print shell input lines to standard error as they are read.

     â€˜-xâ€™
          Print a trace of simple commands, â€˜forâ€™ commands, â€˜caseâ€™
          commands, â€˜selectâ€™ commands, and arithmetic â€˜forâ€™ commands and
          their arguments or associated word lists to the standard error
          after they are expanded and before they are executed.  The
          shell prints the expanded value of the â€˜PS4â€™ variable before
          the command and its expanded arguments.

     â€˜-Bâ€™
          The shell will perform brace expansion (see Brace Expansion).
          This option is on by default.

     â€˜-Câ€™
          Prevent output redirection using â€˜>â€™, â€˜>&â€™, and â€˜<>â€™ from
          overwriting existing files.  Using the redirection operator
          â€˜>|â€™ instead of â€˜>â€™ will override this and force the creation
          of an output file.

     â€˜-Eâ€™
          If set, any trap on â€˜ERRâ€™ is inherited by shell functions,
          command substitutions, and commands executed in a subshell
          environment.  The â€˜ERRâ€™ trap is normally not inherited in such
          cases.

     â€˜-Hâ€™
          Enable â€˜!â€™ style history substitution (see History
          Interaction).  This option is on by default for interactive
          shells.

     â€˜-Pâ€™
          If set, Bash does not resolve symbolic links when executing
          commands such as â€˜cdâ€™ which change the current directory.  It
          uses the physical directory structure instead.  By default,
          Bash follows the logical chain of directories when performing
          commands which change the current directory.

          For example, if â€˜/usr/sysâ€™ is a symbolic link to
          â€˜/usr/local/sysâ€™ then:
               $ cd /usr/sys; echo $PWD
               /usr/sys
               $ cd ..; pwd
               /usr

          If â€˜set -Pâ€™ is on, then:
               $ cd /usr/sys; echo $PWD
               /usr/local/sys
               $ cd ..; pwd
               /usr/local

     â€˜-Tâ€™
          If set, any traps on â€˜DEBUGâ€™ and â€˜RETURNâ€™ are inherited by
          shell functions, command substitutions, and commands executed
          in a subshell environment.  The â€˜DEBUGâ€™ and â€˜RETURNâ€™ traps are
          normally not inherited in such cases.

     â€˜--â€™
          If no arguments follow this option, unset the positional
          parameters.  Otherwise, the positional parameters are set to
          the ARGUMENTS, even if some of them begin with a â€˜-â€™.

     â€˜-â€™
          Signal the end of options, and assign all remaining ARGUMENTS
          to the positional parameters.  The â€˜-xâ€™ and â€˜-vâ€™ options are
          turned off.  If there are no arguments, the positional
          parameters remain unchanged.

     Using â€˜+â€™ rather than â€˜-â€™ causes these options to be turned off.
     The options can also be used upon invocation of the shell.  The
     current set of options may be found in â€˜$-â€™.

     The remaining N ARGUMENTS are positional parameters and are
     assigned, in order, to â€˜$1â€™, â€˜$2â€™, ... â€˜$Nâ€™.  The special parameter
     â€˜#â€™ is set to N.

     The return status is always zero unless an invalid option is
     supplied.

4.3.2 The Shopt Builtin
-----------------------

This builtin allows you to change additional optional shell behavior.

â€˜shoptâ€™
          shopt [-pqsu] [-o] [OPTNAME ...]

     Toggle the values of settings controlling optional shell behavior.
     The settings can be either those listed below, or, if the â€˜-oâ€™
     option is used, those available with the â€˜-oâ€™ option to the â€˜setâ€™
     builtin command (see The Set Builtin).

     With no options, or with the â€˜-pâ€™ option, display a list of all
     settable options, with an indication of whether or not each is set;
     if any OPTNAMEs are supplied, the output is restricted to those
     options.  The â€˜-pâ€™ option displays output in a form that may be
     reused as input.

     Other options have the following meanings:

     â€˜-sâ€™
          Enable (set) each OPTNAME.

     â€˜-uâ€™
          Disable (unset) each OPTNAME.

     â€˜-qâ€™
          Suppresses normal output; the return status indicates whether
          the OPTNAME is set or unset.  If multiple OPTNAME arguments
          are supplied with â€˜-qâ€™, the return status is zero if all
          OPTNAMEs are enabled; non-zero otherwise.

     â€˜-oâ€™
          Restricts the values of OPTNAME to be those defined for the
          â€˜-oâ€™ option to the â€˜setâ€™ builtin (see The Set Builtin).

     If either â€˜-sâ€™ or â€˜-uâ€™ is used with no OPTNAME arguments, â€˜shoptâ€™
     shows only those options which are set or unset, respectively.

     Unless otherwise noted, the â€˜shoptâ€™ options are disabled (off) by
     default.

     The return status when listing options is zero if all OPTNAMEs are
     enabled, non-zero otherwise.  When setting or unsetting options,
     the return status is zero unless an OPTNAME is not a valid shell
     option.

     The list of â€˜shoptâ€™ options is:

     â€˜array_expand_onceâ€™
          If set, the shell suppresses multiple evaluation of
          associative and indexed array subscripts during arithmetic
          expression evaluation, while executing builtins that can
          perform variable assignments, and while executing builtins
          that perform array dereferencing.

     â€˜assoc_expand_onceâ€™
          Deprecated; a synonym for â€˜array_expand_onceâ€™.

     â€˜autocdâ€™
          If set, a command name that is the name of a directory is
          executed as if it were the argument to the â€˜cdâ€™ command.  This
          option is only used by interactive shells.

     â€˜bash_source_fullpathâ€™
          If set, filenames added to the â€˜BASH_SOURCEâ€™ array variable
          are converted to full pathnames (see Bash Variables).

     â€˜cdable_varsâ€™
          If this is set, an argument to the â€˜cdâ€™ builtin command that
          is not a directory is assumed to be the name of a variable
          whose value is the directory to change to.

     â€˜cdspellâ€™
          If set, the â€˜cdâ€™ command attempts to correct minor errors in
          the spelling of a directory component.  Minor errors include
          transposed characters, a missing character, and one extra
          character.  If â€˜cdâ€™ corrects the directory name, it prints the
          corrected filename, and the command proceeds.  This option is
          only used by interactive shells.

     â€˜checkhashâ€™
          If this is set, Bash checks that a command found in the hash
          table exists before trying to execute it.  If a hashed command
          no longer exists, Bash performs a normal path search.

     â€˜checkjobsâ€™
          If set, Bash lists the status of any stopped and running jobs
          before exiting an interactive shell.  If any jobs are running,
          Bash defers the exit until a second exit is attempted without
          an intervening command (see Job Control).  The shell always
          postpones exiting if any jobs are stopped.

     â€˜checkwinsizeâ€™
          If set, Bash checks the window size after each external
          (non-builtin) command and, if necessary, updates the values of
          â€˜LINESâ€™ and â€˜COLUMNSâ€™, using the file descriptor associated
          with stderr if it is a terminal.  This option is enabled by
          default.

     â€˜cmdhistâ€™
          If set, Bash attempts to save all lines of a multiple-line
          command in the same history entry.  This allows easy
          re-editing of multi-line commands.  This option is enabled by
          default, but only has an effect if command history is enabled
          (see Bash History Facilities).

     â€˜compat31â€™
     â€˜compat32â€™
     â€˜compat40â€™
     â€˜compat41â€™
     â€˜compat42â€™
     â€˜compat43â€™
     â€˜compat44â€™
          These control aspects of the shell's compatibility mode (see
          Shell Compatibility Mode).

     â€˜complete_fullquoteâ€™
          If set, Bash quotes all shell metacharacters in filenames and
          directory names when performing completion.  If not set, Bash
          removes metacharacters such as the dollar sign from the set of
          characters that will be quoted in completed filenames when
          these metacharacters appear in shell variable references in
          words to be completed.  This means that dollar signs in
          variable names that expand to directories will not be quoted;
          however, any dollar signs appearing in filenames will not be
          quoted, either.  This is active only when Bash is using
          backslashes to quote completed filenames.  This variable is
          set by default, which is the default Bash behavior in versions
          through 4.2.

     â€˜direxpandâ€™
          If set, Bash replaces directory names with the results of word
          expansion when performing filename completion.  This changes
          the contents of the Readline editing buffer.  If not set, Bash
          attempts to preserve what the user typed.

     â€˜dirspellâ€™
          If set, Bash attempts spelling correction on directory names
          during word completion if the directory name initially
          supplied does not exist.

     â€˜dotglobâ€™
          If set, Bash includes filenames beginning with a â€˜.â€™ in the
          results of filename expansion.  The filenames â€˜.â€™ and â€˜..â€™
          must always be matched explicitly, even if â€˜dotglobâ€™ is set.

     â€˜execfailâ€™
          If this is set, a non-interactive shell will not exit if it
          cannot execute the file specified as an argument to the â€˜execâ€™
          builtin.  An interactive shell does not exit if â€˜execâ€™ fails.

     â€˜expand_aliasesâ€™
          If set, aliases are expanded as described below under Aliases,
          Aliases.  This option is enabled by default for interactive
          shells.

     â€˜extdebugâ€™
          If set at shell invocation, or in a shell startup file,
          arrange to execute the debugger profile before the shell
          starts, identical to the â€˜--debuggerâ€™ option.  If set after
          invocation, behavior intended for use by debuggers is enabled:

            1. The â€˜-Fâ€™ option to the â€˜declareâ€™ builtin (see Bash
               Builtins) displays the source file name and line number
               corresponding to each function name supplied as an
               argument.

            2. If the command run by the â€˜DEBUGâ€™ trap returns a non-zero
               value, the next command is skipped and not executed.

            3. If the command run by the â€˜DEBUGâ€™ trap returns a value of
               2, and the shell is executing in a subroutine (a shell
               function or a shell script executed by the â€˜.â€™ or
               â€˜sourceâ€™ builtins), the shell simulates a call to
               â€˜returnâ€™.

            4. â€˜BASH_ARGCâ€™ and â€˜BASH_ARGVâ€™ are updated as described in
               their descriptions (see Bash Variables).

            5. Function tracing is enabled: command substitution, shell
               functions, and subshells invoked with â€˜( COMMAND )â€™
               inherit the â€˜DEBUGâ€™ and â€˜RETURNâ€™ traps.

            6. Error tracing is enabled: command substitution, shell
               functions, and subshells invoked with â€˜( COMMAND )â€™
               inherit the â€˜ERRâ€™ trap.

     â€˜extglobâ€™
          If set, enable the extended pattern matching features
          described above (see Pattern Matching).

     â€˜extquoteâ€™
          If set, â€˜$'STRING'â€™ and â€˜$"STRING"â€™ quoting is performed
          within â€˜${PARAMETER}â€™ expansions enclosed in double quotes.
          This option is enabled by default.

     â€˜failglobâ€™
          If set, patterns which fail to match filenames during filename
          expansion result in an expansion error.

     â€˜force_fignoreâ€™
          If set, the suffixes specified by the â€˜FIGNOREâ€™ shell variable
          cause words to be ignored when performing word completion even
          if the ignored words are the only possible completions.  See
          Bash Variables, for a description of â€˜FIGNOREâ€™.  This option
          is enabled by default.

     â€˜globasciirangesâ€™
          If set, range expressions used in pattern matching bracket
          expressions (see Pattern Matching) behave as if in the
          traditional C locale when performing comparisons.  That is,
          pattern matching does not take the current locale's collating
          sequence into account, so â€˜bâ€™ will not collate between â€˜Aâ€™ and
          â€˜Bâ€™, and upper-case and lower-case ASCII characters will
          collate together.

     â€˜globskipdotsâ€™
          If set, filename expansion will never match the filenames â€˜.â€™
          and â€˜..â€™, even if the pattern begins with a â€˜.â€™.  This option
          is enabled by default.

     â€˜globstarâ€™
          If set, the pattern â€˜**â€™ used in a filename expansion context
          will match all files and zero or more directories and
          subdirectories.  If the pattern is followed by a â€˜/â€™, only
          directories and subdirectories match.

     â€˜gnu_errfmtâ€™
          If set, shell error messages are written in the standard GNU
          error message format.

     â€˜histappendâ€™
          If set, the history list is appended to the file named by the
          value of the â€˜HISTFILEâ€™ variable when the shell exits, rather
          than overwriting the file.

     â€˜histreeditâ€™
          If set, and Readline is being used, the user is given the
          opportunity to re-edit a failed history substitution.

     â€˜histverifyâ€™
          If set, and Readline is being used, the results of history
          substitution are not immediately passed to the shell parser.
          Instead, the resulting line is loaded into the Readline
          editing buffer, allowing further modification.

     â€˜hostcompleteâ€™
          If set, and Readline is being used, Bash will attempt to
          perform hostname completion when a word containing a â€˜@â€™ is
          being completed (see Commands For Completion).  This option is
          enabled by default.

     â€˜huponexitâ€™
          If set, Bash will send â€˜SIGHUPâ€™ to all jobs when an
          interactive login shell exits (see Signals).

     â€˜inherit_errexitâ€™
          If set, command substitution inherits the value of the
          â€˜errexitâ€™ option, instead of unsetting it in the subshell
          environment.  This option is enabled when POSIX mode is
          enabled.

     â€˜interactive_commentsâ€™
          In an interactive shell, a word beginning with â€˜#â€™ causes that
          word and all remaining characters on that line to be ignored,
          as in a non-interactive shell.  This option is enabled by
          default.

     â€˜lastpipeâ€™
          If set, and job control is not active, the shell runs the last
          command of a pipeline not executed in the background in the
          current shell environment.

     â€˜lithistâ€™
          If enabled, and the â€˜cmdhistâ€™ option is enabled, multi-line
          commands are saved to the history with embedded newlines
          rather than using semicolon separators where possible.

     â€˜localvar_inheritâ€™
          If set, local variables inherit the value and attributes of a
          variable of the same name that exists at a previous scope
          before any new value is assigned.  The â€˜namerefâ€™ attribute is
          not inherited.

     â€˜localvar_unsetâ€™
          If set, calling â€˜unsetâ€™ on local variables in previous
          function scopes marks them so subsequent lookups find them
          unset until that function returns.  This is identical to the
          behavior of unsetting local variables at the current function
          scope.

     â€˜login_shellâ€™
          The shell sets this option if it is started as a login shell
          (see Invoking Bash).  The value may not be changed.

     â€˜mailwarnâ€™
          If set, and a file that Bash is checking for mail has been
          accessed since the last time it was checked, Bash displays the
          message â€˜"The mail in MAILFILE has been read"â€™.

     â€˜no_empty_cmd_completionâ€™
          If set, and Readline is being used, Bash does not search the
          â€˜PATHâ€™ for possible completions when completion is attempted
          on an empty line.

     â€˜nocaseglobâ€™
          If set, Bash matches filenames in a case-insensitive fashion
          when performing filename expansion.

     â€˜nocasematchâ€™
          If set, Bash matches patterns in a case-insensitive fashion
          when performing matching while executing â€˜caseâ€™ or â€˜[[â€™
          conditional commands (see Conditional Constructs, when
          performing pattern substitution word expansions, or when
          filtering possible completions as part of programmable
          completion.

     â€˜noexpand_translationâ€™
          If set, Bash encloses the translated results of $"..." quoting
          in single quotes instead of double quotes.  If the string is
          not translated, this has no effect.

     â€˜nullglobâ€™
          If set, filename expansion patterns which match no files (see
          Filename Expansion) expand to nothing and are removed, rather
          than expanding to themselves.

     â€˜patsub_replacementâ€™
          If set, Bash expands occurrences of â€˜&â€™ in the replacement
          string of pattern substitution to the text matched by the
          pattern, as described above (see Shell Parameter Expansion).
          This option is enabled by default.

     â€˜progcompâ€™
          If set, enable the programmable completion facilities (see
          Programmable Completion).  This option is enabled by default.

     â€˜progcomp_aliasâ€™
          If set, and programmable completion is enabled, Bash treats a
          command name that doesn't have any completions as a possible
          alias and attempts alias expansion.  If it has an alias, Bash
          attempts programmable completion using the command word
          resulting from the expanded alias.

     â€˜promptvarsâ€™
          If set, prompt strings undergo parameter expansion, command
          substitution, arithmetic expansion, and quote removal after
          being expanded as described below (see Controlling the
          Prompt).  This option is enabled by default.

     â€˜restricted_shellâ€™
          The shell sets this option if it is started in restricted mode
          (see The Restricted Shell).  The value may not be changed.
          This is not reset when the startup files are executed,
          allowing the startup files to discover whether or not a shell
          is restricted.

     â€˜shift_verboseâ€™
          If this is set, the â€˜shiftâ€™ builtin prints an error message
          when the shift count exceeds the number of positional
          parameters.

     â€˜sourcepathâ€™
          If set, the â€˜.â€™ (â€˜sourceâ€™) builtin uses the value of â€˜PATHâ€™ to
          find the directory containing the file supplied as an argument
          when the â€˜-pâ€™ option is not supplied.  This option is enabled
          by default.

     â€˜varredir_closeâ€™
          If set, the shell automatically closes file descriptors
          assigned using the â€˜{varname}â€™ redirection syntax (see
          Redirections) instead of leaving them open when the command
          completes.

     â€˜xpg_echoâ€™
          If set, the â€˜echoâ€™ builtin expands backslash-escape sequences
          by default.  If the â€˜posixâ€™ shell option (see The Set Builtin)
          is also enabled, â€˜echoâ€™ does not interpret any options.

4.4 Special Builtins
====================

For historical reasons, the POSIX standard has classified several
