builtin commands as _special_.  When Bash is executing in POSIX mode,
the special builtins differ from other builtin commands in three
respects:

  1. Special builtins are found before shell functions during command
     lookup.

  2. If a special builtin returns an error status, a non-interactive
     shell exits.

  3. Assignment statements preceding the command stay in effect in the
     shell environment after the command completes.

   When Bash is not executing in POSIX mode, these builtins behave no
differently than the rest of the Bash builtin commands.  The Bash POSIX
mode is described in Bash POSIX Mode.

   These are the POSIX special builtins:
     break : . source continue eval exec exit export readonly return set
     shift times trap unset

5 Shell Variables
*****************

This chapter describes the shell variables that Bash uses.  Bash
automatically assigns default values to a number of variables.

5.1 Bourne Shell Variables
==========================

Bash uses certain shell variables in the same way as the Bourne shell.
In some cases, Bash assigns a default value to the variable.

â€˜CDPATHâ€™
     A colon-separated list of directories used as a search path for the
     â€˜cdâ€™ builtin command.

â€˜HOMEâ€™
     The current user's home directory; the default for the â€˜cdâ€™ builtin
     command.  The value of this variable is also used by tilde
     expansion (see Tilde Expansion).

â€˜IFSâ€™
     A list of characters that separate fields; used when the shell
     splits words as part of expansion and by the â€˜readâ€™ builtin to
     split lines into words.  See Word Splitting, for a description of
     word splitting.

â€˜MAILâ€™
     If the value is set to a filename or directory name and the
     â€˜MAILPATHâ€™ variable is not set, Bash informs the user of the
     arrival of mail in the specified file or Maildir-format directory.

â€˜MAILPATHâ€™
     A colon-separated list of filenames which the shell periodically
     checks for new mail.  Each list entry can specify the message that
     is printed when new mail arrives in the mail file by separating the
     filename from the message with a â€˜?â€™.  When used in the text of the
     message, â€˜$_â€™ expands to the name of the current mail file.

â€˜OPTARGâ€™
     The value of the last option argument processed by the â€˜getoptsâ€™
     builtin.

â€˜OPTINDâ€™
     The index of the next argument to be processed by the â€˜getoptsâ€™
     builtin.

â€˜PATHâ€™
     A colon-separated list of directories in which the shell looks for
     commands.  A zero-length (null) directory name in the value of
     â€˜PATHâ€™ indicates the current directory.  A null directory name may
     appear as two adjacent colons, or as an initial or trailing colon.
     The default path is system-dependent, and is set by the
     administrator who installs â€˜bashâ€™.  A common value is
     "/usr/local/bin:/usr/local/sbin:/usr/bin:/usr/sbin:/bin:/sbin".

â€˜PS1â€™
     The primary prompt string.  The default value is â€˜\s-\v\$ â€™.  See
     Controlling the Prompt, for the complete list of escape sequences
     that are expanded before â€˜PS1â€™ is displayed.

â€˜PS2â€™
     The secondary prompt string.  The default value is â€˜> â€™.  â€˜PS2â€™ is
     expanded in the same way as â€˜PS1â€™ before being displayed.

5.2 Bash Variables
==================

These variables are set or used by Bash, but other shells do not
normally treat them specially.

   A few variables used by Bash are described in different chapters:
variables for controlling the job control facilities (see Job Control
Variables).

â€˜_â€™
     ($_, an underscore.)  This has a number of meanings depending on
     context.  At shell startup, $_ set to the pathname used to invoke
     the shell or shell script being executed as passed in the
     environment or argument list.  Subsequently, it expands to the last
     argument to the previous simple command executed in the foreground,
     after expansion.  It is also set to the full pathname used to
     invoke each command executed and placed in the environment exported
     to that command.  When checking mail, $_ expands to the name of the
     mail file.

â€˜BASHâ€™
     The full pathname used to execute the current instance of Bash.

â€˜BASHOPTSâ€™
     A colon-separated list of enabled shell options.  Each word in the
     list is a valid argument for the â€˜-sâ€™ option to the â€˜shoptâ€™ builtin
     command (see The Shopt Builtin).  The options appearing in
     â€˜BASHOPTSâ€™ are those reported as â€˜onâ€™ by â€˜shoptâ€™.  If this variable
     is in the environment when Bash starts up, the shell enables each
     option in the list before reading any startup files.  If this
     variable is exported, child shells will enable each option in the
     list.  This variable is readonly.

â€˜BASHPIDâ€™
     Expands to the process ID of the current Bash process.  This
     differs from â€˜$$â€™ under certain circumstances, such as subshells
     that do not require Bash to be re-initialized.  Assignments to
     â€˜BASHPIDâ€™ have no effect.  If â€˜BASHPIDâ€™ is unset, it loses its
     special properties, even if it is subsequently reset.

â€˜BASH_ALIASESâ€™
     An associative array variable whose members correspond to the
     internal list of aliases as maintained by the â€˜aliasâ€™ builtin.
     (see Bourne Shell Builtins).  Elements added to this array appear
     in the alias list; however, unsetting array elements currently does
     not cause aliases to be removed from the alias list.  If
     â€˜BASH_ALIASESâ€™ is unset, it loses its special properties, even if
     it is subsequently reset.

â€˜BASH_ARGCâ€™
     An array variable whose values are the number of parameters in each
     frame of the current Bash execution call stack.  The number of
     parameters to the current subroutine (shell function or script
     executed with â€˜.â€™ or â€˜sourceâ€™) is at the top of the stack.  When a
     subroutine is executed, the number of parameters passed is pushed
     onto â€˜BASH_ARGCâ€™.  The shell sets â€˜BASH_ARGCâ€™ only when in extended
     debugging mode (see The Shopt Builtin for a description of the
     â€˜extdebugâ€™ option to the â€˜shoptâ€™ builtin).  Setting â€˜extdebugâ€™
     after the shell has started to execute a subroutine, or referencing
     this variable when â€˜extdebugâ€™ is not set, may result in
     inconsistent values.  Assignments to â€˜BASH_ARGCâ€™ have no effect,
     and it may not be unset.

â€˜BASH_ARGVâ€™
     An array variable containing all of the parameters in the current
     Bash execution call stack.  The final parameter of the last
     subroutine call is at the top of the stack; the first parameter of
     the initial call is at the bottom.  When a subroutine is executed,
     the shell pushes the supplied parameters onto â€˜BASH_ARGVâ€™.  The
     shell sets â€˜BASH_ARGVâ€™ only when in extended debugging mode (see
     The Shopt Builtin for a description of the â€˜extdebugâ€™ option to the
     â€˜shoptâ€™ builtin).  Setting â€˜extdebugâ€™ after the shell has started
     to execute a script, or referencing this variable when â€˜extdebugâ€™
     is not set, may result in inconsistent values.  Assignments to
     â€˜BASH_ARGVâ€™ have no effect, and it may not be unset.

â€˜BASH_ARGV0â€™
     When referenced, this variable expands to the name of the shell or
     shell script (identical to â€˜$0â€™; See Special Parameters, for the
     description of special parameter 0).  Assigning a value to
     â€˜BASH_ARGV0â€™ sets â€˜$0â€™ to the same value.  If â€˜BASH_ARGV0â€™ is
     unset, it loses its special properties, even if it is subsequently
     reset.

â€˜BASH_CMDSâ€™
     An associative array variable whose members correspond to the
     internal hash table of commands as maintained by the â€˜hashâ€™ builtin
     (see Bourne Shell Builtins).  Adding elements to this array makes
     them appear in the hash table; however, unsetting array elements
     currently does not remove command names from the hash table.  If
     â€˜BASH_CMDSâ€™ is unset, it loses its special properties, even if it
     is subsequently reset.

â€˜BASH_COMMANDâ€™
     Expands to the command currently being executed or about to be
     executed, unless the shell is executing a command as the result of
     a trap, in which case it is the command executing at the time of
     the trap.  If â€˜BASH_COMMANDâ€™ is unset, it loses its special
     properties, even if it is subsequently reset.

â€˜BASH_COMPATâ€™
     The value is used to set the shell's compatibility level.  See
     Shell Compatibility Mode, for a description of the various
     compatibility levels and their effects.  The value may be a decimal
     number (e.g., 4.2) or an integer (e.g., 42) corresponding to the
     desired compatibility level.  If â€˜BASH_COMPATâ€™ is unset or set to
     the empty string, the compatibility level is set to the default for
     the current version.  If â€˜BASH_COMPATâ€™ is set to a value that is
     not one of the valid compatibility levels, the shell prints an
     error message and sets the compatibility level to the default for
     the current version.  A subset of the valid values correspond to
     the compatibility levels described below (see Shell Compatibility
     Mode).  For example, 4.2 and 42 are valid values that correspond to
     the â€˜compat42â€™ â€˜shoptâ€™ option and set the compatibility level to
     42.  The current version is also a valid value.

â€˜BASH_ENVâ€™
     If this variable is set when Bash is invoked to execute a shell
     script, its value is expanded and used as the name of a startup
     file to read before executing the script.  Bash does not use â€˜PATHâ€™
     to search for the resultant filename.  See Bash Startup Files.

â€˜BASH_EXECUTION_STRINGâ€™
     The command argument to the â€˜-câ€™ invocation option.

â€˜BASH_LINENOâ€™
     An array variable whose members are the line numbers in source
     files where each corresponding member of â€˜FUNCNAMEâ€™ was invoked.
     â€˜${BASH_LINENO[$i]}â€™ is the line number in the source file
     (â€˜${BASH_SOURCE[$i+1]}â€™) where â€˜${FUNCNAME[$i]}â€™ was called (or
     â€˜${BASH_LINENO[$i-1]}â€™ if referenced within another shell
     function).  Use â€˜LINENOâ€™ to obtain the current line number.
     Assignments to â€˜BASH_LINENOâ€™ have no effect, and it may not be
     unset.

â€˜BASH_LOADABLES_PATHâ€™
     A colon-separated list of directories in which the â€˜enableâ€™ command
     looks for dynamically loadable builtins.

â€˜BASH_MONOSECONDSâ€™
     Each time this variable is referenced, it expands to the value
     returned by the system's monotonic clock, if one is available.  If
     there is no monotonic clock, this is equivalent to â€˜EPOCHSECONDSâ€™.
     If â€˜BASH_MONOSECONDSâ€™ is unset, it loses its special properties,
     even if it is subsequently reset.

â€˜BASH_REMATCHâ€™
     An array variable whose members are assigned by the â€˜=~â€™ binary
     operator to the â€˜[[â€™ conditional command (see Conditional
     Constructs).  The element with index 0 is the portion of the string
     matching the entire regular expression.  The element with index N
     is the portion of the string matching the Nth parenthesized
     subexpression.

â€˜BASH_SOURCEâ€™
     An array variable whose members are the source filenames where the
     corresponding shell function names in the â€˜FUNCNAMEâ€™ array variable
     are defined.  The shell function â€˜${FUNCNAME[$i]}â€™ is defined in
     the file â€˜${BASH_SOURCE[$i]}â€™ and called from
     â€˜${BASH_SOURCE[$i+1]}â€™ Assignments to â€˜BASH_SOURCEâ€™ have no effect,
     and it may not be unset.

â€˜BASH_SUBSHELLâ€™
     Incremented by one within each subshell or subshell environment
     when the shell begins executing in that environment.  The initial
     value is 0.  If â€˜BASH_SUBSHELLâ€™ is unset, it loses its special
     properties, even if it is subsequently reset.

â€˜BASH_TRAPSIGâ€™
     Set to the signal number corresponding to the trap action being
     executed during its execution.  See the description of â€˜trapâ€™ (see
     Bourne Shell Builtins) for information about signal numbers and
     trap execution.

â€˜BASH_VERSINFOâ€™
     A readonly array variable (see Arrays) whose members hold version
     information for this instance of Bash.  The values assigned to the
     array members are as follows:

     â€˜BASH_VERSINFO[0]â€™
          The major version number (the â€œreleaseâ€).

     â€˜BASH_VERSINFO[1]â€™
          The minor version number (the â€œversionâ€).

     â€˜BASH_VERSINFO[2]â€™
          The patch level.

     â€˜BASH_VERSINFO[3]â€™
          The build version.

     â€˜BASH_VERSINFO[4]â€™
          The release status (e.g., â€˜betaâ€™).

     â€˜BASH_VERSINFO[5]â€™
          The value of â€˜MACHTYPEâ€™.

â€˜BASH_VERSIONâ€™
     Expands to a string describing the version of this instance of Bash
     (e.g., 5.2.37(3)-release).

â€˜BASH_XTRACEFDâ€™
     If set to an integer corresponding to a valid file descriptor, Bash
     writes the trace output generated when â€˜set -xâ€™ is enabled to that
     file descriptor, instead of the standard error.  This allows
     tracing output to be separated from diagnostic and error messages.
     The file descriptor is closed when â€˜BASH_XTRACEFDâ€™ is unset or
     assigned a new value.  Unsetting â€˜BASH_XTRACEFDâ€™ or assigning it
     the empty string causes the trace output to be sent to the standard
     error.  Note that setting â€˜BASH_XTRACEFDâ€™ to 2 (the standard error
     file descriptor) and then unsetting it will result in the standard
     error being closed.

â€˜CHILD_MAXâ€™
     Set the number of exited child status values for the shell to
     remember.  Bash will not allow this value to be decreased below a
     POSIX-mandated minimum, and there is a maximum value (currently
     8192) that this may not exceed.  The minimum value is
     system-dependent.

â€˜COLUMNSâ€™
     Used by the â€˜selectâ€™ command to determine the terminal width when
     printing selection lists.  Automatically set if the â€˜checkwinsizeâ€™
     option is enabled (see The Shopt Builtin), or in an interactive
     shell upon receipt of a â€˜SIGWINCHâ€™.

â€˜COMP_CWORDâ€™
     An index into â€˜${COMP_WORDS}â€™ of the word containing the current
     cursor position.  This variable is available only in shell
     functions invoked by the programmable completion facilities (see
     Programmable Completion).

â€˜COMP_KEYâ€™
     The key (or final key of a key sequence) used to invoke the current
     completion function.  This variable is available only in shell
     functions and external commands invoked by the programmable
     completion facilities (see Programmable Completion).

â€˜COMP_LINEâ€™
     The current command line.  This variable is available only in shell
     functions and external commands invoked by the programmable
     completion facilities (see Programmable Completion).

â€˜COMP_POINTâ€™
     The index of the current cursor position relative to the beginning
     of the current command.  If the current cursor position is at the
     end of the current command, the value of this variable is equal to
     â€˜${#COMP_LINE}â€™.  This variable is available only in shell
     functions and external commands invoked by the programmable
     completion facilities (see Programmable Completion).

â€˜COMP_TYPEâ€™
     Set to an integer value corresponding to the type of attempted
     completion that caused a completion function to be called: <TAB>,
     for normal completion, â€˜?â€™, for listing completions after
     successive tabs, â€˜!â€™, for listing alternatives on partial word
     completion, â€˜@â€™, to list completions if the word is not unmodified,
     or â€˜%â€™, for menu completion.  This variable is available only in
     shell functions and external commands invoked by the programmable
     completion facilities (see Programmable Completion).

â€˜COMP_WORDBREAKSâ€™
     The set of characters that the Readline library treats as word
     separators when performing word completion.  If â€˜COMP_WORDBREAKSâ€™
     is unset, it loses its special properties, even if it is
     subsequently reset.

â€˜COMP_WORDSâ€™
     An array variable consisting of the individual words in the current
     command line.  The line is split into words as Readline would split
     it, using â€˜COMP_WORDBREAKSâ€™ as described above.  This variable is
     available only in shell functions invoked by the programmable
     completion facilities (see Programmable Completion).

â€˜COMPREPLYâ€™
     An array variable from which Bash reads the possible completions
     generated by a shell function invoked by the programmable
     completion facility (see Programmable Completion).  Each array
     element contains one possible completion.

â€˜COPROCâ€™
     An array variable created to hold the file descriptors for output
     from and input to an unnamed coprocess (see Coprocesses).

â€˜DIRSTACKâ€™
     An array variable containing the current contents of the directory
     stack.  Directories appear in the stack in the order they are
     displayed by the â€˜dirsâ€™ builtin.  Assigning to members of this
     array variable may be used to modify directories already in the
     stack, but the â€˜pushdâ€™ and â€˜popdâ€™ builtins must be used to add and
     remove directories.  Assigning to this variable does not change the
     current directory.  If â€˜DIRSTACKâ€™ is unset, it loses its special
     properties, even if it is subsequently reset.

â€˜EMACSâ€™
     If Bash finds this variable in the environment when the shell
     starts, and its value is â€˜tâ€™, Bash assumes that the shell is
     running in an Emacs shell buffer and disables line editing.

â€˜ENVâ€™
     Expanded and executed similarly to â€˜BASH_ENVâ€™ (see Bash Startup
     Files) when an interactive shell is invoked in POSIX mode (see Bash
     POSIX Mode).

â€˜EPOCHREALTIMEâ€™
     Each time this parameter is referenced, it expands to the number of
     seconds since the Unix Epoch as a floating-point value with
     micro-second granularity (see the documentation for the C library
     function â€˜timeâ€™ for the definition of Epoch).  Assignments to
     â€˜EPOCHREALTIMEâ€™ are ignored.  If â€˜EPOCHREALTIMEâ€™ is unset, it loses
     its special properties, even if it is subsequently reset.

â€˜EPOCHSECONDSâ€™
     Each time this parameter is referenced, it expands to the number of
     seconds since the Unix Epoch (see the documentation for the C
     library function â€˜timeâ€™ for the definition of Epoch).  Assignments
     to â€˜EPOCHSECONDSâ€™ are ignored.  If â€˜EPOCHSECONDSâ€™ is unset, it
     loses its special properties, even if it is subsequently reset.

â€˜EUIDâ€™
     The numeric effective user id of the current user.  This variable
     is readonly.

â€˜EXECIGNOREâ€™
     A colon-separated list of shell patterns (see Pattern Matching)
     defining the set of filenames to be ignored by command search using
     â€˜PATHâ€™.  Files whose full pathnames match one of these patterns are
     not considered executable files for the purposes of completion and
     command execution via â€˜PATHâ€™ lookup.  This does not affect the
     behavior of the â€˜[â€™, â€˜testâ€™, and â€˜[[â€™ commands.  Full pathnames in
     the command hash table are not subject to â€˜EXECIGNOREâ€™.  Use this
     variable to ignore shared library files that have the executable
     bit set, but are not executable files.  The pattern matching honors
     the setting of the â€˜extglobâ€™ shell option.

â€˜FCEDITâ€™
     The editor used as a default by the â€˜fcâ€™ builtin command.

â€˜FIGNOREâ€™
     A colon-separated list of suffixes to ignore when performing
     filename completion.  A filename whose suffix matches one of the
     entries in â€˜FIGNOREâ€™ is excluded from the list of matched
     filenames.  A sample value is â€˜.o:~â€™

â€˜FUNCNAMEâ€™
     An array variable containing the names of all shell functions
     currently in the execution call stack.  The element with index 0 is
     the name of any currently-executing shell function.  The
     bottom-most element (the one with the highest index) is â€˜"main"â€™.
     This variable exists only when a shell function is executing.
     Assignments to â€˜FUNCNAMEâ€™ have no effect.  If â€˜FUNCNAMEâ€™ is unset,
     it loses its special properties, even if it is subsequently reset.

     This variable can be used with â€˜BASH_LINENOâ€™ and â€˜BASH_SOURCEâ€™.
     Each element of â€˜FUNCNAMEâ€™ has corresponding elements in
     â€˜BASH_LINENOâ€™ and â€˜BASH_SOURCEâ€™ to describe the call stack.  For
     instance, â€˜${FUNCNAME[$i]}â€™ was called from the file
     â€˜${BASH_SOURCE[$i+1]}â€™ at line number â€˜${BASH_LINENO[$i]}â€™.  The
     â€˜callerâ€™ builtin displays the current call stack using this
     information.

â€˜FUNCNESTâ€™
     A numeric value greater than 0 defines a maximum function nesting
     level.  Function invocations that exceed this nesting level cause
     the current command to abort.

â€˜GLOBIGNOREâ€™
     A colon-separated list of patterns defining the set of file names
     to be ignored by filename expansion.  If a file name matched by a
     filename expansion pattern also matches one of the patterns in
     â€˜GLOBIGNOREâ€™, it is removed from the list of matches.  The pattern
     matching honors the setting of the â€˜extglobâ€™ shell option.

â€˜GLOBSORTâ€™
     Controls how the results of filename expansion are sorted.  The
     value of this variable specifies the sort criteria and sort order
     for the results of filename expansion.  If this variable is unset
     or set to the null string, filename expansion uses the historical
     behavior of sorting by name, in ascending lexicographic order as
     determined by the â€˜LC_COLLATEâ€™ shell variable.

     If set, a valid value begins with an optional â€˜+â€™, which is
     ignored, or â€˜-â€™, which reverses the sort order from ascending to
     descending, followed by a sort specifier.  The valid sort
     specifiers are â€˜nameâ€™, â€˜numericâ€™, â€˜sizeâ€™, â€˜mtimeâ€™, â€˜atimeâ€™,
     â€˜ctimeâ€™, and â€˜blocksâ€™, which sort the files on name, names in
     numeric rather than lexicographic order, file size, modification
     time, access time, inode change time, and number of blocks,
     respectively.  If any of the non-name keys compare as equal (e.g.,
     if two files are the same size), sorting uses the name as a
     secondary sort key.

     For example, a value of â€˜-mtimeâ€™ sorts the results in descending
     order by modification time (newest first).

     The â€˜numericâ€™ specifier treats names consisting solely of digits as
     numbers and sorts them using their numeric value (so "2" sorts
     before "10", for example).  When using â€˜numericâ€™, names containing
     non-digits sort after all the all-digit names and are sorted by
     name using the traditional behavior.

     A sort specifier of â€˜nosortâ€™ disables sorting completely; Bash
     returns the results in the order they are read from the file
     system, ignoring any leading â€˜-â€™.

     If the sort specifier is missing, it defaults to NAME, so a value
     of â€˜+â€™ is equivalent to the null string, and a value of â€˜-â€™ sorts
     by name in descending order.

     Any invalid value restores the historical sorting behavior.

â€˜GROUPSâ€™
     An array variable containing the list of groups of which the
     current user is a member.  Assignments to â€˜GROUPSâ€™ have no effect.
     If â€˜GROUPSâ€™ is unset, it loses its special properties, even if it
     is subsequently reset.

â€˜histcharsâ€™
     The two or three characters which control history expansion, quick
     substitution, and tokenization (see History Interaction).  The
     first character is the â€œhistory expansionâ€ character, the character
     which begins a history expansion, normally â€˜!â€™.  The second
     character is the â€œquick substitutionâ€ character, normally â€˜^â€™.
     When it appears as the first character on the line, history
     substitution repeats the previous command, replacing one string
     with another.  The optional third character is the â€œhistory
     commentâ€ character, normally â€˜#â€™, which indicates that the
     remainder of the line is a comment when it appears as the first
     character of a word.  The history comment character disables
     history substitution for the remaining words on the line.  It does
     not necessarily cause the shell parser to treat the rest of the
     line as a comment.

â€˜HISTCMDâ€™
     The history number, or index in the history list, of the current
     command.  Assignments to â€˜HISTCMDâ€™ have no effect.  If â€˜HISTCMDâ€™ is
     unset, it loses its special properties, even if it is subsequently
     reset.

â€˜HISTCONTROLâ€™
     A colon-separated list of values controlling how commands are saved
     on the history list.  If the list of values includes â€˜ignorespaceâ€™,
     lines which begin with a space character are not saved in the
     history list.  A value of â€˜ignoredupsâ€™ causes lines which match the
     previous history entry not to be saved.  A value of â€˜ignorebothâ€™ is
     shorthand for â€˜ignorespaceâ€™ and â€˜ignoredupsâ€™.  A value of
     â€˜erasedupsâ€™ causes all previous lines matching the current line to
     be removed from the history list before that line is saved.  Any
     value not in the above list is ignored.  If â€˜HISTCONTROLâ€™ is unset,
     or does not include a valid value, Bash saves all lines read by the
     shell parser on the history list, subject to the value of
     â€˜HISTIGNOREâ€™.  If the first line of a multi-line compound command
     was saved, the second and subsequent lines are not tested, and are
     added to the history regardless of the value of â€˜HISTCONTROLâ€™.  If
     the first line was not saved, the second and subsequent lines of
     the command are not saved either.

â€˜HISTFILEâ€™
     The name of the file to which the command history is saved.  Bash
     assigns a default value of â€˜~/.bash_historyâ€™.  If â€˜HISTFILEâ€™ is
     unset or null, the shell does not save the command history when it
     exits.

â€˜HISTFILESIZEâ€™
     The maximum number of lines contained in the history file.  When
     this variable is assigned a value, the history file is truncated,
     if necessary, to contain no more than the number of history entries
     that total no more than that number of lines by removing the oldest
     entries.  If the history list contains multi-line entries, the
     history file may contain more lines than this maximum to avoid
     leaving partial history entries.  The history file is also
     truncated to this size after writing it when a shell exits or by
     the â€˜historyâ€™ builtin.  If the value is 0, the history file is
     truncated to zero size.  Non-numeric values and numeric values less
     than zero inhibit truncation.  The shell sets the default value to
     the value of â€˜HISTSIZEâ€™ after reading any startup files.

â€˜HISTIGNOREâ€™
     A colon-separated list of patterns used to decide which command
     lines should be saved on the history list.  If a command line
     matches one of the patterns in the value of â€˜HISTIGNOREâ€™, it is not
     saved on the history list.  Each pattern is anchored at the
     beginning of the line and must match the complete line (Bash does
     not implicitly append a â€˜*â€™).  Each pattern is tested against the
     line after the checks specified by â€˜HISTCONTROLâ€™ are applied.  In
     addition to the normal shell pattern matching characters, â€˜&â€™
     matches the previous history line.  A backslash escapes the â€˜&â€™;
     the backslash is removed before attempting a match.  If the first
     line of a multi-line compound command was saved, the second and
     subsequent lines are not tested, and are added to the history
     regardless of the value of â€˜HISTIGNOREâ€™.  If the first line was not
     saved, the second and subsequent lines of the command are not saved
     either.  The pattern matching honors the setting of the â€˜extglobâ€™
     shell option.

     â€˜HISTIGNOREâ€™ subsumes some of the function of â€˜HISTCONTROLâ€™.  A
     pattern of â€˜&â€™ is identical to â€˜ignoredupsâ€™, and a pattern of â€˜[
     ]*â€™ is identical to â€˜ignorespaceâ€™.  Combining these two patterns,
     separating them with a colon, provides the functionality of
     â€˜ignorebothâ€™.

â€˜HISTSIZEâ€™
     The maximum number of commands to remember on the history list.  If
     the value is 0, commands are not saved in the history list.
     Numeric values less than zero result in every command being saved
     on the history list (there is no limit).  The shell sets the
     default value to 500 after reading any startup files.

â€˜HISTTIMEFORMATâ€™
     If this variable is set and not null, its value is used as a format
     string for â€˜strftimeâ€™(3) to print the time stamp associated with
     each history entry displayed by the â€˜historyâ€™ builtin.  If this
     variable is set, the shell writes time stamps to the history file
     so they may be preserved across shell sessions.  This uses the
     history comment character to distinguish timestamps from other
     history lines.

â€˜HOSTFILEâ€™
     Contains the name of a file in the same format as â€˜/etc/hostsâ€™ that
     should be read when the shell needs to complete a hostname.  The
     list of possible hostname completions may be changed while the
     shell is running; the next time hostname completion is attempted
     after the value is changed, Bash adds the contents of the new file
     to the existing list.  If â€˜HOSTFILEâ€™ is set, but has no value, or
     does not name a readable file, Bash attempts to read â€˜/etc/hostsâ€™
     to obtain the list of possible hostname completions.  When
     â€˜HOSTFILEâ€™ is unset, Bash clears the hostname list.

â€˜HOSTNAMEâ€™
     The name of the current host.

â€˜HOSTTYPEâ€™
     A string describing the machine Bash is running on.

â€˜IGNOREEOFâ€™
     Controls the action of the shell on receipt of an â€˜EOFâ€™ character
     as the sole input.  If set, the value is the number of consecutive
     â€˜EOFâ€™ characters that can be read as the first character on an
     input line before Bash exits.  If the variable is set but does not
     have a numeric value, or the value is null, then the default is 10.
     If the variable is unset, then â€˜EOFâ€™ signifies the end of input to
     the shell.  This is only in effect for interactive shells.

â€˜INPUTRCâ€™
     The name of the Readline initialization file, overriding the
     default of â€˜~/.inputrcâ€™.

â€˜INSIDE_EMACSâ€™
     If Bash finds this variable in the environment when the shell
     starts, it assumes that the shell is running in an Emacs shell
     buffer and may disable line editing depending on the value of
     â€˜TERMâ€™.

â€˜LANGâ€™
     Used to determine the locale category for any category not
     specifically selected with a variable starting with â€˜LC_â€™.

â€˜LC_ALLâ€™
     This variable overrides the value of â€˜LANGâ€™ and any other â€˜LC_â€™
     variable specifying a locale category.

â€˜LC_COLLATEâ€™
     This variable determines the collation order used when sorting the
     results of filename expansion, and determines the behavior of range
     expressions, equivalence classes, and collating sequences within
     filename expansion and pattern matching (see Filename Expansion).

â€˜LC_CTYPEâ€™
     This variable determines the interpretation of characters and the
     behavior of character classes within filename expansion and pattern
     matching (see Filename Expansion).

â€˜LC_MESSAGESâ€™
     This variable determines the locale used to translate double-quoted
     strings preceded by a â€˜$â€™ (see Locale Translation).

â€˜LC_NUMERICâ€™
     This variable determines the locale category used for number
     formatting.

â€˜LC_TIMEâ€™
     This variable determines the locale category used for data and time
     formatting.

â€˜LINENOâ€™
     The line number in the script or shell function currently
     executing.  Line numbers start with 1.  When not in a script or
     function, the value is not guaranteed to be meaningful.  If
     â€˜LINENOâ€™ is unset, it loses its special properties, even if it is
     subsequently reset.

â€˜LINESâ€™
     Used by the â€˜selectâ€™ command to determine the column length for
     printing selection lists.  Automatically set if the â€˜checkwinsizeâ€™
     option is enabled (see The Shopt Builtin), or in an interactive
     shell upon receipt of a â€˜SIGWINCHâ€™.

â€˜MACHTYPEâ€™
     A string that fully describes the system type on which Bash is
     executing, in the standard GNU CPU-COMPANY-SYSTEM format.

â€˜MAILCHECKâ€™
     How often (in seconds) that the shell should check for mail in the
     files specified in the â€˜MAILPATHâ€™ or â€˜MAILâ€™ variables.  The default
     is 60 seconds.  When it is time to check for mail, the shell does
     so before displaying the primary prompt.  If this variable is
     unset, or set to a value that is not a number greater than or equal
     to zero, the shell disables mail checking.

â€˜MAPFILEâ€™
     An array variable created to hold the text read by the â€˜mapfileâ€™
     builtin when no variable name is supplied.

â€˜OLDPWDâ€™
     The previous working directory as set by the â€˜cdâ€™ builtin.

â€˜OPTERRâ€™
     If set to the value 1, Bash displays error messages generated by
     the â€˜getoptsâ€™ builtin command.  â€˜OPTERRâ€™ is initialized to 1 each
     time the shell is invoked.

â€˜OSTYPEâ€™
     A string describing the operating system Bash is running on.

â€˜PIPESTATUSâ€™
     An array variable (see Arrays) containing a list of exit status
     values from the commands in the most-recently-executed foreground
     pipeline, which may consist of only a simple command (see Shell
     Commands).  Bash sets â€˜PIPESTATUSâ€™ after executing multi-element
     pipelines, timed and negated pipelines, simple commands, subshells
     created with the â€˜(â€™ operator, the â€˜[[â€™ and â€˜((â€™ compound commands,
     and after error conditions that result in the shell aborting
     command execution.

â€˜POSIXLY_CORRECTâ€™
     If this variable is in the environment when Bash starts, the shell
     enters POSIX mode (see Bash POSIX Mode) before reading the startup
     files, as if the â€˜--posixâ€™ invocation option had been supplied.  If
     it is set while the shell is running, Bash enables POSIX mode, as
     if the command
          set -o posix
     had been executed.  When the shell enters POSIX mode, it sets this
     variable if it was not already set.

â€˜PPIDâ€™
     The process ID of the shell's parent process.  This variable is
     readonly.

â€˜PROMPT_COMMANDâ€™
     If this variable is set, and is an array, the value of each set
     element is interpreted as a command to execute before printing the
     primary prompt (â€˜$PS1â€™).  If this is set but not an array variable,
     its value is used as a command to execute instead.

â€˜PROMPT_DIRTRIMâ€™
     If set to a number greater than zero, the value is used as the
     number of trailing directory components to retain when expanding
     the â€˜\wâ€™ and â€˜\Wâ€™ prompt string escapes (see Controlling the
     Prompt).  Characters removed are replaced with an ellipsis.

â€˜PS0â€™
     The value of this parameter is expanded like â€˜PS1â€™ and displayed by
     interactive shells after reading a command and before the command
     is executed.

â€˜PS3â€™
     The value of this variable is used as the prompt for the â€˜selectâ€™
     command.  If this variable is not set, the â€˜selectâ€™ command prompts
     with â€˜#? â€™

â€˜PS4â€™
     The value of this parameter is expanded like â€˜PS1â€™ and the expanded
     value is the prompt printed before the command line is echoed when
     the â€˜-xâ€™ option is set (see The Set Builtin).  The first character
     of the expanded value is replicated multiple times, as necessary,
     to indicate multiple levels of indirection.  The default is â€˜+ â€™.

â€˜PWDâ€™
     The current working directory as set by the â€˜cdâ€™ builtin.

â€˜RANDOMâ€™
     Each time this parameter is referenced, it expands to a random
     integer between 0 and 32767.  Assigning a value to â€˜RANDOMâ€™
     initializes (seeds) the sequence of random numbers.  Seeding the
     random number generator with the same constant value produces the
     same sequence of values.  If â€˜RANDOMâ€™ is unset, it loses its
     special properties, even if it is subsequently reset.

â€˜READLINE_ARGUMENTâ€™
     Any numeric argument given to a Readline command that was defined
     using â€˜bind -xâ€™ (see Bash Builtins when it was invoked.

â€˜READLINE_LINEâ€™
     The contents of the Readline line buffer, for use with â€˜bind -xâ€™
     (see Bash Builtins).

â€˜READLINE_MARKâ€™
     The position of the â€œmarkâ€ (saved insertion point) in the Readline
     line buffer, for use with â€˜bind -xâ€™ (see Bash Builtins).  The
     characters between the insertion point and the mark are often
     called the â€œregionâ€.

â€˜READLINE_POINTâ€™
     The position of the insertion point in the Readline line buffer,
     for use with â€˜bind -xâ€™ (see Bash Builtins).

â€˜REPLYâ€™
     The default variable for the â€˜readâ€™ builtin; set to the line read
     when â€˜readâ€™ is not supplied a variable name argument.

â€˜SECONDSâ€™
     This variable expands to the number of seconds since the shell was
     started.  Assignment to this variable resets the count to the value
     assigned, and the expanded value becomes the value assigned plus
     the number of seconds since the assignment.  The number of seconds
     at shell invocation and the current time are always determined by
     querying the system clock at one-second resolution.  If â€˜SECONDSâ€™
     is unset, it loses its special properties, even if it is
     subsequently reset.

â€˜SHELLâ€™
     This environment variable expands to the full pathname to the
     shell.  If it is not set when the shell starts, Bash assigns to it
     the full pathname of the current user's login shell.

â€˜SHELLOPTSâ€™
     A colon-separated list of enabled shell options.  Each word in the
     list is a valid argument for the â€˜-oâ€™ option to the â€˜setâ€™ builtin
     command (see The Set Builtin).  The options appearing in
     â€˜SHELLOPTSâ€™ are those reported as â€˜onâ€™ by â€˜set -oâ€™.  If this
     variable is in the environment when Bash starts up, the shell
     enables each option in the list before reading any startup files.
     If this variable is exported, child shells will enable each option
     in the list.  This variable is readonly.

â€˜SHLVLâ€™
     Incremented by one each time a new instance of Bash is started.
     This is intended to be a count of how deeply your Bash shells are
     nested.

â€˜SRANDOMâ€™
     This variable expands to a 32-bit pseudo-random number each time it
     is referenced.  The random number generator is not linear on
     systems that support â€˜/dev/urandomâ€™ or â€˜arc4randomâ€™, so each
     returned number has no relationship to the numbers preceding it.
     The random number generator cannot be seeded, so assignments to
     this variable have no effect.  If â€˜SRANDOMâ€™ is unset, it loses its
     special properties, even if it is subsequently reset.

â€˜TIMEFORMATâ€™
     The value of this parameter is used as a format string specifying
     how the timing information for pipelines prefixed with the â€˜timeâ€™
     reserved word should be displayed.  The â€˜%â€™ character introduces an
     escape sequence that is expanded to a time value or other
     information.  The escape sequences and their meanings are as
     follows; the brackets denote optional portions.

     â€˜%%â€™
          A literal â€˜%â€™.

     â€˜%[P][l]Râ€™
          The elapsed time in seconds.

     â€˜%[P][l]Uâ€™
          The number of CPU seconds spent in user mode.

     â€˜%[P][l]Sâ€™
          The number of CPU seconds spent in system mode.

     â€˜%Pâ€™
          The CPU percentage, computed as (%U + %S) / %R.

     The optional P is a digit specifying the precision, the number of
     fractional digits after a decimal point.  A value of 0 causes no
     decimal point or fraction to be output.  â€˜timeâ€™ prints at most six
     digits after the decimal point; values of P greater than 6 are
     changed to 6.  If P is not specified, â€˜timeâ€™ prints three digits
     after the decimal point.

     The optional â€˜lâ€™ specifies a longer format, including minutes, of
     the form MMmSS.FFs.  The value of P determines whether or not the
     fraction is included.

     If this variable is not set, Bash acts as if it had the value
          $'\nreal\t%3lR\nuser\t%3lU\nsys\t%3lS'
     If the value is null, Bash does not display any timing information.
     A trailing newline is added when the format string is displayed.

â€˜TMOUTâ€™
     If set to a value greater than zero, the â€˜readâ€™ builtin uses the
     value as its default timeout (see Bash Builtins).  The â€˜selectâ€™
     command (see Conditional Constructs) terminates if input does not
     arrive after â€˜TMOUTâ€™ seconds when input is coming from a terminal.

     In an interactive shell, the value is interpreted as the number of
     seconds to wait for a line of input after issuing the primary
     prompt.  Bash terminates after waiting for that number of seconds
     if a complete line of input does not arrive.

â€˜TMPDIRâ€™
     If set, Bash uses its value as the name of a directory in which
     Bash creates temporary files for the shell's use.

â€˜UIDâ€™
     The numeric real user id of the current user.  This variable is
     readonly.

6 Bash Features
***************

This chapter describes features unique to Bash.

6.1 Invoking Bash
=================

     bash [long-opt] [-ir] [-abefhkmnptuvxdBCDHP] [-o OPTION]
         [-O SHOPT_OPTION] [ARGUMENT ...]
     bash [long-opt] [-abefhkmnptuvxdBCDHP] [-o OPTION]
         [-O SHOPT_OPTION] -c STRING [ARGUMENT ...]
     bash [long-opt] -s [-abefhkmnptuvxdBCDHP] [-o OPTION]
         [-O SHOPT_OPTION] [ARGUMENT ...]

   All of the single-character options used with the â€˜setâ€™ builtin (see
The Set Builtin) can be used as options when the shell is invoked.  In
addition, there are several multi-character options that you can use.
These options must appear on the command line before the
single-character options to be recognized.

â€˜--debuggerâ€™
     Arrange for the debugger profile to be executed before the shell
     starts.  Turns on extended debugging mode (see The Shopt Builtin
     for a description of the â€˜extdebugâ€™ option to the â€˜shoptâ€™ builtin).

â€˜--dump-po-stringsâ€™
     Print a list of all double-quoted strings preceded by â€˜$â€™ on the
     standard output in the GNU â€˜gettextâ€™ PO (portable object) file
     format.  Equivalent to â€˜-Dâ€™ except for the output format.

â€˜--dump-stringsâ€™
     Equivalent to â€˜-Dâ€™.

â€˜--helpâ€™
     Display a usage message on standard output and exit successfully.

â€˜--init-file FILENAMEâ€™
â€˜--rcfile FILENAMEâ€™
     Execute commands from FILENAME (instead of â€˜~/.bashrcâ€™) in an
     interactive shell.

â€˜--loginâ€™
     Equivalent to â€˜-lâ€™.

â€˜--noeditingâ€™
     Do not use the GNU Readline library (see Command Line Editing) to
     read command lines when the shell is interactive.

â€˜--noprofileâ€™
     Don't load the system-wide startup file â€˜/etc/profileâ€™ or any of
     the personal initialization files â€˜~/.bash_profileâ€™,
     â€˜~/.bash_loginâ€™, or â€˜~/.profileâ€™ when Bash is invoked as a login
     shell.

â€˜--norcâ€™
     Don't read the â€˜~/.bashrcâ€™ initialization file in an interactive
     shell.  This is on by default if the shell is invoked as â€˜shâ€™.

â€˜--posixâ€™
     Enable POSIX mode; change the behavior of Bash where the default
     operation differs from the POSIX standard to match the standard.
     This is intended to make Bash behave as a strict superset of that
     standard.  See Bash POSIX Mode, for a description of the Bash POSIX
     mode.

â€˜--restrictedâ€™
     Equivalent to â€˜-râ€™.  Make the shell a restricted shell (see The
     Restricted Shell).

â€˜--verboseâ€™
     Equivalent to â€˜-vâ€™.  Print shell input lines as they're read.

â€˜--versionâ€™
     Show version information for this instance of Bash on the standard
     output and exit successfully.

   There are several single-character options that may be supplied at
invocation which are not available with the â€˜setâ€™ builtin.

â€˜-câ€™
     Read and execute commands from the first non-option argument
     COMMAND_STRING, then exit.  If there are arguments after the
     COMMAND_STRING, the first argument is assigned to â€˜$0â€™ and any
     remaining arguments are assigned to the positional parameters.  The
     assignment to â€˜$0â€™ sets the name of the shell, which is used in
     warning and error messages.

â€˜-iâ€™
     Force the shell to run interactively.  Interactive shells are
     described in Interactive Shells.

â€˜-lâ€™
     Make this shell act as if it had been directly invoked by login.
     When the shell is interactive, this is equivalent to starting a
     login shell with â€˜exec -l bashâ€™.  When the shell is not
     interactive, it will read and execute the login shell startup
     files.  â€˜exec bash -lâ€™ or â€˜exec bash --loginâ€™ will replace the
     current shell with a Bash login shell.  See Bash Startup Files, for
     a description of the special behavior of a login shell.

â€˜-râ€™
     Make the shell a restricted shell (see The Restricted Shell).

â€˜-sâ€™
     If this option is present, or if no arguments remain after option
     processing, then Bash reads commands from the standard input.  This
     option allows the positional parameters to be set when invoking an
     interactive shell or when reading input through a pipe.

â€˜-Dâ€™
     Print a list of all double-quoted strings preceded by â€˜$â€™ on the
     standard output.  These are the strings that are subject to
     language translation when the current locale is not â€˜Câ€™ or â€˜POSIXâ€™
     (see Locale Translation).  This implies the â€˜-nâ€™ option; no
     commands will be executed.

â€˜[-+]O [SHOPT_OPTION]â€™
     SHOPT_OPTION is one of the shell options accepted by the â€˜shoptâ€™
     builtin (see The Shopt Builtin).  If SHOPT_OPTION is present, â€˜-Oâ€™
     sets the value of that option; â€˜+Oâ€™ unsets it.  If SHOPT_OPTION is
     not supplied, Bash prints the names and values of the shell options
     accepted by â€˜shoptâ€™ on the standard output.  If the invocation
     option is â€˜+Oâ€™, the output is displayed in a format that may be
     reused as input.

â€˜--â€™
     A â€˜--â€™ signals the end of options and disables further option
     processing.  Any arguments after the â€˜--â€™ are treated as a shell
     script filename (see Shell Scripts) and arguments passed to that
     script.

â€˜-â€™
     Equivalent to â€˜--â€™.

   A â€œlogin shellâ€ is one whose first character of argument zero is â€˜-â€™,
or one invoked with the â€˜--loginâ€™ option.

   An â€œinteractive shellâ€ is one started without non-option arguments,
unless â€˜-sâ€™ is specified, without specifying the â€˜-câ€™ option, and whose
standard input and standard error are both connected to terminals (as
determined by isatty(3)), or one started with the â€˜-iâ€™ option.  See
Interactive Shells, for more information.

   If arguments remain after option processing, and neither the â€˜-câ€™ nor
the â€˜-sâ€™ option has been supplied, the first argument is treated as the
name of a file containing shell commands (see Shell Scripts).  When Bash
is invoked in this fashion, â€˜$0â€™ is set to the name of the file, and the
positional parameters are set to the remaining arguments.  Bash reads
and executes commands from this file, then exits.  Bash's exit status is
the exit status of the last command executed in the script.  If no
commands are executed, the exit status is 0.  Bash first attempts to
open the file in the current directory, and, if no file is found,
searches the directories in â€˜PATHâ€™ for the script.

6.2 Bash Startup Files
======================

This section describes how Bash executes its startup files.  If any of
the files exist but cannot be read, Bash reports an error.  Tildes are
expanded in filenames as described above under Tilde Expansion (see
Tilde Expansion).

   Interactive shells are described in Interactive Shells.

Invoked as an interactive login shell, or with â€˜--loginâ€™
........................................................

When Bash is invoked as an interactive login shell, or as a
non-interactive shell with the â€˜--loginâ€™ option, it first reads and
executes commands from the file â€˜/etc/profileâ€™, if that file exists.
After reading that file, it looks for â€˜~/.bash_profileâ€™,
â€˜~/.bash_loginâ€™, and â€˜~/.profileâ€™, in that order, and reads and executes
commands from the first one that exists and is readable.  The
â€˜--noprofileâ€™ option inhibits this behavior.

   When an interactive login shell exits, or a non-interactive login
shell executes the â€˜exitâ€™ builtin command, Bash reads and executes
commands from the file â€˜~/.bash_logoutâ€™, if it exists.

Invoked as an interactive non-login shell
.........................................

When Bash runs as an interactive shell that is not a login shell, it
reads and executes commands from â€˜~/.bashrcâ€™, if that file exists.  The
â€˜--norcâ€™ option inhibits this behavior.  The â€˜--rcfile FILEâ€™ option
causes Bash to use FILE instead of â€˜~/.bashrcâ€™.

   So, typically, your â€˜~/.bash_profileâ€™ contains the line
     if [ -f ~/.bashrc ]; then . ~/.bashrc; fi
after (or before) any login-specific initializations.

Invoked non-interactively
.........................

When Bash is started non-interactively, to run a shell script, for
example, it looks for the variable â€˜BASH_ENVâ€™ in the environment,
expands its value if it appears there, and uses the expanded value as
the name of a file to read and execute.  Bash behaves as if the
following command were executed:
     if [ -n "$BASH_ENV" ]; then . "$BASH_ENV"; fi
but does not the value of the â€˜PATHâ€™ variable to search for the
filename.

   As noted above, if a non-interactive shell is invoked with the
â€˜--loginâ€™ option, Bash attempts to read and execute commands from the
login shell startup files.

Invoked with name â€˜shâ€™
......................

If Bash is invoked with the name â€˜shâ€™, it tries to mimic the startup
behavior of historical versions of â€˜shâ€™ as closely as possible, while
conforming to the POSIX standard as well.

   When invoked as an interactive login shell, or as a non-interactive
shell with the â€˜--loginâ€™ option, it first attempts to read and execute
commands from â€˜/etc/profileâ€™ and â€˜~/.profileâ€™, in that order.  The
â€˜--noprofileâ€™ option inhibits this behavior.

   When invoked as an interactive shell with the name â€˜shâ€™, Bash looks
for the variable â€˜ENVâ€™, expands its value if it is defined, and uses the
expanded value as the name of a file to read and execute.  Since a shell
invoked as â€˜shâ€™ does not attempt to read and execute commands from any
other startup files, the â€˜--rcfileâ€™ option has no effect.

   A non-interactive shell invoked with the name â€˜shâ€™ does not attempt
to read any other startup files.

   When invoked as â€˜shâ€™, Bash enters POSIX mode after reading the
startup files.

Invoked in POSIX mode
.....................

When Bash is started in POSIX mode, as with the â€˜--posixâ€™ command line
option, it follows the POSIX standard for startup files.  In this mode,
interactive shells expand the â€˜ENVâ€™ variable and read and execute
commands from the file whose name is the expanded value.  No other
startup files are read.

Invoked by remote shell daemon
..............................

Bash attempts to determine when it is being run with its standard input
connected to a network connection, as when executed by the historical
and rarely-seen remote shell daemon, usually â€˜rshdâ€™, or the secure shell
daemon â€˜sshdâ€™.  If Bash determines it is being run non-interactively in
this fashion, it reads and executes commands from â€˜~/.bashrcâ€™, if that
file exists and is readable.  Bash does not read this file if invoked as
â€˜shâ€™.  The â€˜--norcâ€™ option inhibits this behavior, and the â€˜--rcfileâ€™
option makes Bash use a different file instead of â€˜~/.bashrcâ€™, but
neither â€˜rshdâ€™ nor â€˜sshdâ€™ generally invoke the shell with those options
or allow them to be specified.

Invoked with unequal effective and real UID/GIDs
................................................

If Bash is started with the effective user (group) id not equal to the
real user (group) id, and the â€˜-pâ€™ option is not supplied, no startup
files are read, shell functions are not inherited from the environment,
the â€˜SHELLOPTSâ€™, â€˜BASHOPTSâ€™, â€˜CDPATHâ€™, and â€˜GLOBIGNOREâ€™ variables, if
they appear in the environment, are ignored, and the effective user id
is set to the real user id.  If the â€˜-pâ€™ option is supplied at
invocation, the startup behavior is the same, but the effective user id
is not reset.

6.3 Interactive Shells
======================

6.3.1 What is an Interactive Shell?
-----------------------------------

An interactive shell is one started without non-option arguments (unless
â€˜-sâ€™ is specified) and without specifying the â€˜-câ€™ option, whose input
and error output are both connected to terminals (as determined by
â€˜isatty(3)â€™), or one started with the â€˜-iâ€™ option.

   An interactive shell generally reads from and writes to a user's
terminal.

   The â€˜-sâ€™ invocation option may be used to set the positional
parameters when an interactive shell starts.

6.3.2 Is this Shell Interactive?
--------------------------------

To determine within a startup script whether or not Bash is running
interactively, test the value of the â€˜-â€™ special parameter.  It contains
â€˜iâ€™ when the shell is interactive.  For example:

     case "$-" in
     *i*)	echo This shell is interactive ;;
     *)	echo This shell is not interactive ;;
     esac

   Alternatively, startup scripts may examine the variable â€˜PS1â€™; it is
unset in non-interactive shells, and set in interactive shells.  Thus:

     if [ -z "$PS1" ]; then
             echo This shell is not interactive
     else
             echo This shell is interactive
     fi

6.3.3 Interactive Shell Behavior
--------------------------------

When the shell is running interactively, it changes its behavior in
several ways.

  1. Bash reads and executes startup files as described in Bash Startup
     Files.

  2. Job Control (see Job Control) is enabled by default.  When job
     control is in effect, Bash ignores the keyboard-generated job
     control signals â€˜SIGTTINâ€™, â€˜SIGTTOUâ€™, and â€˜SIGTSTPâ€™.

  3. Bash executes the values of the set elements of the
     â€˜PROMPT_COMMANDâ€™ array variable as commands before printing the
     primary prompt, â€˜$PS1â€™ (see Bash Variables).

  4. Bash expands and displays â€˜PS1â€™ before reading the first line of a
     command, and expands and displays â€˜PS2â€™ before reading the second
     and subsequent lines of a multi-line command.  Bash expands and
     displays â€˜PS0â€™ after it reads a command but before executing it.
     See Controlling the Prompt, for a complete list of prompt string
     escape sequences.

  5. Bash uses Readline (see Command Line Editing) to read commands from
     the user's terminal.

  6. Bash inspects the value of the â€˜ignoreeofâ€™ option to â€˜set -oâ€™
     instead of exiting immediately when it receives an â€˜EOFâ€™ on its
     standard input when reading a command (see The Set Builtin).

  7. Bash enables Command history (see Bash History Facilities) and
     history expansion (see History Interaction) by default.  When a
     shell with history enabled exits, Bash saves the command history to
     the file named by â€˜$HISTFILEâ€™.

  8. Alias expansion (see Aliases) is performed by default.

  9. In the absence of any traps, Bash ignores â€˜SIGTERMâ€™ (see Signals).

  10. In the absence of any traps, â€˜SIGINTâ€™ is caught and handled (see
     Signals).  â€˜SIGINTâ€™ will interrupt some shell builtins.

  11. An interactive login shell sends a â€˜SIGHUPâ€™ to all jobs on exit if
     the â€˜huponexitâ€™ shell option has been enabled (see Signals).

  12. The â€˜-nâ€™ option has no effect, whether at invocation or when using
     â€˜set -nâ€™ (see The Set Builtin).

  13. Bash will check for mail periodically, depending on the values of
     the â€˜MAILâ€™, â€˜MAILPATHâ€™, and â€˜MAILCHECKâ€™ shell variables (see Bash
     Variables).

  14. The shell will not exit on expansion errors due to references to
     unbound shell variables after â€˜set -uâ€™ has been enabled (see The
     Set Builtin).

  15. The shell will not exit on expansion errors caused by VAR being
     unset or null in â€˜${VAR:?WORD}â€™ expansions (see Shell Parameter
     Expansion).

  16. Redirection errors encountered by shell builtins will not cause
     the shell to exit.

  17. When running in POSIX mode, a special builtin returning an error
     status will not cause the shell to exit (see Bash POSIX Mode).

  18. A failed â€˜execâ€™ will not cause the shell to exit (see Bourne Shell
     Builtins).

  19. Parser syntax errors will not cause the shell to exit.

  20. If the â€˜cdspellâ€™ shell option is enabled, the shell will attempt
     simple spelling correction for directory arguments to the â€˜cdâ€™
     builtin (see the description of the â€˜cdspellâ€™ option to the â€˜shoptâ€™
     builtin in The Shopt Builtin).  The â€˜cdspellâ€™ option is only
     effective in interactive shells.

  21. The shell will check the value of the â€˜TMOUTâ€™ variable and exit if
     a command is not read within the specified number of seconds after
     printing â€˜$PS1â€™ (see Bash Variables).

6.4 Bash Conditional Expressions
================================

Conditional expressions are used by the â€˜[[â€™ compound command (see
Conditional Constructs) and the â€˜testâ€™ and â€˜[â€™ builtin commands (see
Bourne Shell Builtins).  The â€˜testâ€™ and â€˜[â€™ commands determine their
behavior based on the number of arguments; see the descriptions of those
commands for any other command-specific actions.

   Expressions may be unary or binary, and are formed from the primaries
listed below.  Unary expressions are often used to examine the status of
a file or shell variable.  Binary operators are used for string,
numeric, and file attribute comparisons.

   Bash handles several filenames specially when they are used in
expressions.  If the operating system on which Bash is running provides
these special files, Bash uses them; otherwise it emulates them
internally with this behavior: If the FILE argument to one of the
primaries is of the form â€˜/dev/fd/Nâ€™, then Bash checks file descriptor
N.  If the FILE argument to one of the primaries is one of â€˜/dev/stdinâ€™,
â€˜/dev/stdoutâ€™, or â€˜/dev/stderrâ€™, Bash checks file descriptor 0, 1, or 2,
respectively.

   When used with â€˜[[â€™, the â€˜<â€™ and â€˜>â€™ operators sort lexicographically
using the current locale.  The â€˜testâ€™ command uses ASCII ordering.

   Unless otherwise specified, primaries that operate on files follow
symbolic links and operate on the target of the link, rather than the
link itself.

â€˜-a FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists.

â€˜-b FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is a block special file.

â€˜-c FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is a character special file.

â€˜-d FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is a directory.

â€˜-e FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists.

â€˜-f FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is a regular file.

â€˜-g FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and its set-group-id bit is set.

â€˜-h FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is a symbolic link.

â€˜-k FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and its "sticky" bit is set.

â€˜-p FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is a named pipe (FIFO).

â€˜-r FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is readable.

â€˜-s FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and has a size greater than zero.

â€˜-t FDâ€™
     True if file descriptor FD is open and refers to a terminal.

â€˜-u FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and its set-user-id bit is set.

â€˜-w FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is writable.

â€˜-x FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is executable.

â€˜-G FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is owned by the effective group id.

â€˜-L FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is a symbolic link.

â€˜-N FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and has been modified since it was last
     accessed.

â€˜-O FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is owned by the effective user id.

â€˜-S FILEâ€™
     True if FILE exists and is a socket.

â€˜FILE1 -ef FILE2â€™
     True if FILE1 and FILE2 refer to the same device and inode numbers.

â€˜FILE1 -nt FILE2â€™
     True if FILE1 is newer (according to modification date) than FILE2,
     or if FILE1 exists and FILE2 does not.

â€˜FILE1 -ot FILE2â€™
     True if FILE1 is older than FILE2, or if FILE2 exists and FILE1
     does not.

â€˜-o OPTNAMEâ€™
     True if the shell option OPTNAME is enabled.  The list of options
     appears in the description of the â€˜-oâ€™ option to the â€˜setâ€™ builtin
     (see The Set Builtin).

â€˜-v VARNAMEâ€™
     True if the shell variable VARNAME is set (has been assigned a
     value).  If VARNAME is an indexed array variable name subscripted
     by â€˜@â€™ or â€˜*â€™, this returns true if the array has any set elements.
     If VARNAME is an associative array variable name subscripted by â€˜@â€™
     or â€˜*â€™, this returns true if an element with that key is set.

â€˜-R VARNAMEâ€™
     True if the shell variable VARNAME is set and is a name reference.

â€˜-z STRINGâ€™
     True if the length of STRING is zero.

â€˜-n STRINGâ€™
â€˜STRINGâ€™
     True if the length of STRING is non-zero.

â€˜STRING1 == STRING2â€™
â€˜STRING1 = STRING2â€™
     True if the strings are equal.  When used with the â€˜[[â€™ command,
     this performs pattern matching as described above (see Conditional
     Constructs).

     â€˜=â€™ should be used with the â€˜testâ€™ command for POSIX conformance.

â€˜STRING1 != STRING2â€™
     True if the strings are not equal.

â€˜STRING1 < STRING2â€™
     True if STRING1 sorts before STRING2 lexicographically.

â€˜STRING1 > STRING2â€™
     True if STRING1 sorts after STRING2 lexicographically.

â€˜ARG1 OP ARG2â€™
     â€˜OPâ€™ is one of â€˜-eqâ€™, â€˜-neâ€™, â€˜-ltâ€™, â€˜-leâ€™, â€˜-gtâ€™, or â€˜-geâ€™.  These
     arithmetic binary operators return true if ARG1 is equal to, not
     equal to, less than, less than or equal to, greater than, or
     greater than or equal to ARG2, respectively.  ARG1 and ARG2 may be
     positive or negative integers.  When used with the â€˜[[â€™ command,
     ARG1 and ARG2 are evaluated as arithmetic expressions (see Shell
     Arithmetic).  Since the expansions the â€˜[[â€™ command performs on
     ARG1 and ARG2 can potentially result in empty strings, arithmetic
     expression evaluation treats those as expressions that evaluate to
     0.

6.5 Shell Arithmetic
====================

The shell allows arithmetic expressions to be evaluated, as one of the
shell expansions or by using the â€˜((â€™ compound command, the â€˜letâ€™ and
â€˜declareâ€™ builtins, the arithmetic â€˜forâ€™ command, the â€˜[[â€™ conditional
command, or the â€˜-iâ€™ option to the â€˜declareâ€™ builtin.

   Evaluation is done in the largest fixed-width integers available,
with no check for overflow, though division by 0 is trapped and flagged
as an error.  The operators and their precedence, associativity, and
values are the same as in the C language.  The following list of
operators is grouped into levels of equal-precedence operators.  The
levels are listed in order of decreasing precedence.

â€˜ID++ ID--â€™
     variable post-increment and post-decrement

â€˜++ID --IDâ€™
     variable pre-increment and pre-decrement

â€˜- +â€™
     unary minus and plus

â€˜! ~â€™
     logical and bitwise negation

â€˜**â€™
     exponentiation

â€˜* / %â€™
     multiplication, division, remainder

â€˜+ -â€™
     addition, subtraction

â€˜<< >>â€™
     left and right bitwise shifts

â€˜<= >= < >â€™
     comparison

â€˜== !=â€™
     equality and inequality

â€˜&â€™
     bitwise AND

â€˜^â€™
     bitwise exclusive OR

â€˜|â€™
     bitwise OR

â€˜&&â€™
     logical AND

â€˜||â€™
     logical OR

â€˜expr ? if-true-expr : if-false-exprâ€™
     conditional operator

â€˜= *= /= %= += -= <<= >>= &= ^= |=â€™
     assignment

â€˜expr1 , expr2â€™
     comma

   Shell variables are allowed as operands; parameter expansion is
performed before the expression is evaluated.  Within an expression,
shell variables may also be referenced by name without using the
parameter expansion syntax.  This means you can use X, where X is a
shell variable name, in an arithmetic expression, and the shell will
evaluate its value as an expression and use the result.  A shell
variable that is null or unset evaluates to 0 when referenced by name in
an expression.

   The value of a variable is evaluated as an arithmetic expression when
it is referenced, or when a variable which has been given the â€˜integerâ€™
attribute using â€˜declare -iâ€™ is assigned a value.  A null value
evaluates to 0.  A shell variable need not have its â€˜integerâ€™ attribute
turned on to be used in an expression.

   Integer constants follow the C language definition, without suffixes
or character constants.  Constants with a leading 0 are interpreted as
octal numbers.  A leading â€˜0xâ€™ or â€˜0Xâ€™ denotes hexadecimal.  Otherwise,
numbers take the form [BASEâ€˜#â€™]N, where the optional BASE is a decimal
number between 2 and 64 representing the arithmetic base, and N is a
number in that base.  If BASEâ€˜#â€™ is omitted, then base 10 is used.  When
specifying N, if a non-digit is required, the digits greater than 9 are
represented by the lowercase letters, the uppercase letters, â€˜@â€™, and
â€˜_â€™, in that order.  If BASE is less than or equal to 36, lowercase and
uppercase letters may be used interchangeably to represent numbers
between 10 and 35.

   Operators are evaluated in precedence order.  Sub-expressions in
parentheses are evaluated first and may override the precedence rules
above.

6.6 Aliases
===========

â€œAliasesâ€ allow a string to be substituted for a word that is in a
position in the input where it can be the first word of a simple
command.  Aliases have names and corresponding values that are set and
unset using the â€˜aliasâ€™ and â€˜unaliasâ€™ builtin commands (see Shell
Builtin Commands).

   If the shell reads an unquoted word in the right position, it checks
the word to see if it matches an alias name.  If it matches, the shell
replaces the word with the alias value, and reads that value as if it
had been read instead of the word.  The shell doesn't look at any
characters following the word before attempting alias substitution.

   The characters â€˜/â€™, â€˜$â€™, â€˜`â€™, â€˜=â€™ and any of the shell metacharacters
or quoting characters listed above may not appear in an alias name.  The
replacement text may contain any valid shell input, including shell
metacharacters.  The first word of the replacement text is tested for
aliases, but a word that is identical to an alias being expanded is not
expanded a second time.  This means that one may alias â€˜lsâ€™ to â€˜"ls
-F"â€™, for instance, and Bash does not try to recursively expand the
replacement text.

   If the last character of the alias value is a â€˜blankâ€™, then the shell
checks the next command word following the alias for alias expansion.

   Aliases are created and listed with the â€˜aliasâ€™ command, and removed
with the â€˜unaliasâ€™ command.

   There is no mechanism for using arguments in the replacement text, as
in â€˜cshâ€™.  If arguments are needed, use a shell function (see Shell
Functions) instead.

   Aliases are not expanded when the shell is not interactive, unless
the â€˜expand_aliasesâ€™ shell option is set using â€˜shoptâ€™ (see The Shopt
Builtin).

   The rules concerning the definition and use of aliases are somewhat
confusing.  Bash always reads at least one complete line of input, and
all lines that make up a compound command, before executing any of the
commands on that line or the compound command.  Aliases are expanded
when a command is read, not when it is executed.  Therefore, an alias
definition appearing on the same line as another command does not take
effect until the shell reads the next line of input, and an alias
definition in a compound command does not take effect until the shell
parses and executes the entire compound command.  The commands following
the alias definition on that line, or in the rest of a compound command,
are not affected by the new alias.  This behavior is also an issue when
functions are executed.  Aliases are expanded when a function definition
is read, not when the function is executed, because a function
definition is itself a command.  As a consequence, aliases defined in a
function are not available until after that function is executed.  To be
safe, always put alias definitions on a separate line, and do not use
â€˜aliasâ€™ in compound commands.

   For almost every purpose, shell functions are preferable to aliases.

6.7 Arrays
==========

Bash provides one-dimensional indexed and associative array variables.
Any variable may be used as an indexed array; the â€˜declareâ€™ builtin
explicitly declares an array.  There is no maximum limit on the size of
an array, nor any requirement that members be indexed or assigned
contiguously.  Indexed arrays are referenced using arithmetic
expressions that must expand to an integer (see Shell Arithmetic)) and
are zero-based; associative arrays use arbitrary strings.  Unless
otherwise noted, indexed array indices must be non-negative integers.

   The shell performs parameter and variable expansion, arithmetic
expansion, command substitution, and quote removal on indexed array
subscripts.  Since this can potentially result in empty strings,
subscript indexing treats those as expressions that evaluate to 0.

   The shell performs tilde expansion, parameter and variable expansion,
arithmetic expansion, command substitution, and quote removal on
associative array subscripts.  Empty strings cannot be used as
associative array keys.

   Bash automatically creates an indexed array if any variable is
assigned to using the syntax
     NAME[SUBSCRIPT]=VALUE

The SUBSCRIPT is treated as an arithmetic expression that must evaluate
to a number greater than or equal to zero.  To explicitly declare an
indexed array, use
     declare -a NAME
(see Bash Builtins).  The syntax
     declare -a NAME[SUBSCRIPT]
is also accepted; the SUBSCRIPT is ignored.

Associative arrays are created using
     declare -A NAME

   Attributes may be specified for an array variable using the â€˜declareâ€™
and â€˜readonlyâ€™ builtins.  Each attribute applies to all members of an
array.

   Arrays are assigned using compound assignments of the form
     NAME=(VALUE1 VALUE2 ... )
where each VALUE may be of the form â€˜[SUBSCRIPT]=â€™STRING.  Indexed array
assignments do not require anything but STRING.

   Each VALUE in the list undergoes the shell expansions described above
(see Shell Expansions), but VALUEs that are valid variable assignments
including the brackets and subscript do not undergo brace expansion and
word splitting, as with individual variable assignments.

   When assigning to indexed arrays, if the optional subscript is
supplied, that index is assigned to; otherwise the index of the element
assigned is the last index assigned to by the statement plus one.
Indexing starts at zero.

   When assigning to an associative array, the words in a compound
assignment may be either assignment statements, for which the subscript
is required, or a list of words that is interpreted as a sequence of
alternating keys and values: NAME=(KEY1 VALUE1 KEY2 VALUE2 ... ).  These
are treated identically to NAME=( [KEY1]=VALUE1 [KEY2]=VALUE2 ... ).
The first word in the list determines how the remaining words are
interpreted; all assignments in a list must be of the same type.  When
using key/value pairs, the keys may not be missing or empty; a final
missing value is treated like the empty string.

   This syntax is also accepted by the â€˜declareâ€™ builtin.  Individual
array elements may be assigned to using the â€˜NAME[SUBSCRIPT]=VALUEâ€™
syntax introduced above.

   When assigning to an indexed array, if NAME is subscripted by a
negative number, that number is interpreted as relative to one greater
than the maximum index of NAME, so negative indices count back from the
end of the array, and an index of -1 references the last element.

   The â€˜+=â€™ operator appends to an array variable when assigning using
the compound assignment syntax; see Shell Parameters above.

   An array element is referenced using â€˜${NAME[SUBSCRIPT]}â€™.  The
braces are required to avoid conflicts with the shell's filename
expansion operators.  If the SUBSCRIPT is â€˜@â€™ or â€˜*â€™, the word expands
to all members of the array NAME, unless otherwise noted in the
description of a builtin or word expansion.  These subscripts differ
only when the word appears within double quotes.  If the word is
double-quoted, â€˜${NAME[*]}â€™ expands to a single word with the value of
each array member separated by the first character of the â€˜IFSâ€™
variable, and â€˜${NAME[@]}â€™ expands each element of NAME to a separate
word.  When there are no array members, â€˜${NAME[@]}â€™ expands to nothing.
If the double-quoted expansion occurs within a word, the expansion of
the first parameter is joined with the beginning part of the expansion
of the original word, and the expansion of the last parameter is joined
with the last part of the expansion of the original word.  This is
analogous to the expansion of the special parameters â€˜@â€™ and â€˜*â€™.

   â€˜${#NAME[SUBSCRIPT]}â€™ expands to the length of â€˜${NAME[SUBSCRIPT]}â€™.
If SUBSCRIPT is â€˜@â€™ or â€˜*â€™, the expansion is the number of elements in
the array.

   If the SUBSCRIPT used to reference an element of an indexed array
evaluates to a number less than zero, it is interpreted as relative to
one greater than the maximum index of the array, so negative indices
count back from the end of the array, and an index of -1 refers to the
last element.

   Referencing an array variable without a subscript is equivalent to
referencing with a subscript of 0.  Any reference to a variable using a
valid subscript is valid; Bash creates an array if necessary.

   An array variable is considered set if a subscript has been assigned
a value.  The null string is a valid value.

   It is possible to obtain the keys (indices) of an array as well as
the values.  ${!NAME[@]} and ${!NAME[*]} expand to the indices assigned
in array variable NAME.  The treatment when in double quotes is similar
to the expansion of the special parameters â€˜@â€™ and â€˜*â€™ within double
quotes.

   The â€˜unsetâ€™ builtin is used to destroy arrays.  â€˜unset
NAME[SUBSCRIPT]â€™ unsets the array element at index SUBSCRIPT.  Negative
subscripts to indexed arrays are interpreted as described above.
Unsetting the last element of an array variable does not unset the
variable.  â€˜unset NAMEâ€™, where NAME is an array, removes the entire
array.  â€˜unset NAME[SUBSCRIPT]â€™ behaves differently depending on the
array type when SUBSCRIPT is â€˜*â€™ or â€˜@â€™.  When NAME is an associative
array, it removes the element with key â€˜*â€™ or â€˜@â€™.  If NAME is an
indexed array, â€˜unsetâ€™ removes all of the elements, but does not remove
the array itself.

   When using a variable name with a subscript as an argument to a
command, such as with â€˜unsetâ€™, without using the word expansion syntax
described above (e.g., unset a[4]), the argument is subject to the
shell's filename expansion.  Quote the argument if pathname expansion is
not desired (e.g., unset 'a[4]').

   The â€˜declareâ€™, â€˜localâ€™, and â€˜readonlyâ€™ builtins each accept a â€˜-aâ€™
option to specify an indexed array and a â€˜-Aâ€™ option to specify an
associative array.  If both options are supplied, â€˜-Aâ€™ takes precedence.
The â€˜readâ€™ builtin accepts a â€˜-aâ€™ option to assign a list of words read
from the standard input to an array, and can read values from the
standard input into individual array elements.  The â€˜setâ€™ and â€˜declareâ€™
builtins display array values in a way that allows them to be reused as
input.  Other builtins accept array name arguments as well (e.g.,
â€˜mapfileâ€™); see the descriptions of individual builtins for details.
The shell provides a number of builtin array variables.

6.8 The Directory Stack
=======================

The directory stack is a list of recently-visited directories.  The
â€˜pushdâ€™ builtin adds directories to the stack as it changes the current
directory, and the â€˜popdâ€™ builtin removes specified directories from the
stack and changes the current directory to the directory removed.  The
â€˜dirsâ€™ builtin displays the contents of the directory stack.  The
current directory is always the "top" of the directory stack.

   The contents of the directory stack are also visible as the value of
the â€˜DIRSTACKâ€™ shell variable.

6.8.1 Directory Stack Builtins
------------------------------
