          commands.  Built-in â€˜keymapâ€™ names are â€˜emacsâ€™,
          â€˜emacs-standardâ€™, â€˜emacs-metaâ€™, â€˜emacs-ctlxâ€™, â€˜viâ€™, â€˜vi-moveâ€™,
          â€˜vi-commandâ€™, and â€˜vi-insertâ€™.  â€˜viâ€™ is equivalent to
          â€˜vi-commandâ€™ (â€˜vi-moveâ€™ is also a synonym); â€˜emacsâ€™ is
          equivalent to â€˜emacs-standardâ€™.  Applications may add
          additional names.  The default value is â€˜emacsâ€™; the value of
          the â€˜editing-modeâ€™ variable also affects the default keymap.

     â€˜keyseq-timeoutâ€™
          Specifies the duration Readline will wait for a character when
          reading an ambiguous key sequence (one that can form a
          complete key sequence using the input read so far, or can take
          additional input to complete a longer key sequence).  If
          Readline doesn't receive any input within the timeout, it uses
          the shorter but complete key sequence.  Readline uses this
          value to determine whether or not input is available on the
          current input source (â€˜rl_instreamâ€™ by default).  The value is
          specified in milliseconds, so a value of 1000 means that
          Readline will wait one second for additional input.  If this
          variable is set to a value less than or equal to zero, or to a
          non-numeric value, Readline waits until another key is pressed
          to decide which key sequence to complete.  The default value
          is â€˜500â€™.

     â€˜mark-directoriesâ€™
          If set to â€˜onâ€™, completed directory names have a slash
          appended.  The default is â€˜onâ€™.

     â€˜mark-modified-linesâ€™
          When this variable is set to â€˜onâ€™, Readline displays an
          asterisk (â€˜*â€™) at the start of history lines which have been
          modified.  This variable is â€˜offâ€™ by default.

     â€˜mark-symlinked-directoriesâ€™
          If set to â€˜onâ€™, completed names which are symbolic links to
          directories have a slash appended, subject to the value of
          â€˜mark-directoriesâ€™.  The default is â€˜offâ€™.

     â€˜match-hidden-filesâ€™
          This variable, when set to â€˜onâ€™, forces Readline to match
          files whose names begin with a â€˜.â€™ (hidden files) when
          performing filename completion.  If set to â€˜offâ€™, the user
          must include the leading â€˜.â€™ in the filename to be completed.
          This variable is â€˜onâ€™ by default.

     â€˜menu-complete-display-prefixâ€™
          If set to â€˜onâ€™, menu completion displays the common prefix of
          the list of possible completions (which may be empty) before
          cycling through the list.  The default is â€˜offâ€™.

     â€˜output-metaâ€™
          If set to â€˜onâ€™, Readline displays characters with the eighth
          bit set directly rather than as a meta-prefixed escape
          sequence.  The default is â€˜offâ€™, but Readline sets it to â€˜onâ€™
          if the locale contains characters whose encodings may include
          bytes with the eighth bit set.  This variable is dependent on
          the â€˜LC_CTYPEâ€™ locale category, and its value may change if
          the locale changes.

     â€˜page-completionsâ€™
          If set to â€˜onâ€™, Readline uses an internal pager resembling
          more(1) to display a screenful of possible completions at a
          time.  This variable is â€˜onâ€™ by default.

     â€˜prefer-visible-bellâ€™
          See â€˜bell-styleâ€™.

     â€˜print-completions-horizontallyâ€™
          If set to â€˜onâ€™, Readline displays completions with matches
          sorted horizontally in alphabetical order, rather than down
          the screen.  The default is â€˜offâ€™.

     â€˜revert-all-at-newlineâ€™
          If set to â€˜onâ€™, Readline will undo all changes to history
          lines before returning when executing â€˜accept-lineâ€™.  By
          default, history lines may be modified and retain individual
          undo lists across calls to â€˜readline()â€™.  The default is
          â€˜offâ€™.

     â€˜search-ignore-caseâ€™
          If set to â€˜onâ€™, Readline performs incremental and
          non-incremental history list searches in a case-insensitive
          fashion.  The default value is â€˜offâ€™.

     â€˜show-all-if-ambiguousâ€™
          This alters the default behavior of the completion functions.
          If set to â€˜onâ€™, words which have more than one possible
          completion cause the matches to be listed immediately instead
          of ringing the bell.  The default value is â€˜offâ€™.

     â€˜show-all-if-unmodifiedâ€™
          This alters the default behavior of the completion functions
          in a fashion similar to SHOW-ALL-IF-AMBIGUOUS.  If set to
          â€˜onâ€™, words which have more than one possible completion
          without any possible partial completion (the possible
          completions don't share a common prefix) cause the matches to
          be listed immediately instead of ringing the bell.  The
          default value is â€˜offâ€™.

     â€˜show-mode-in-promptâ€™
          If set to â€˜onâ€™, add a string to the beginning of the prompt
          indicating the editing mode: emacs, vi command, or vi
          insertion.  The mode strings are user-settable (e.g.,
          EMACS-MODE-STRING).  The default value is â€˜offâ€™.

     â€˜skip-completed-textâ€™
          If set to â€˜onâ€™, this alters the default completion behavior
          when inserting a single match into the line.  It's only active
          when performing completion in the middle of a word.  If
          enabled, Readline does not insert characters from the
          completion that match characters after point in the word being
          completed, so portions of the word following the cursor are
          not duplicated.  For instance, if this is enabled, attempting
          completion when the cursor is after the first â€˜eâ€™ in
          â€˜Makefileâ€™ will result in â€˜Makefileâ€™ rather than
          â€˜Makefilefileâ€™, assuming there is a single possible
          completion.  The default value is â€˜offâ€™.

     â€˜vi-cmd-mode-stringâ€™
          If the SHOW-MODE-IN-PROMPT variable is enabled, this string is
          displayed immediately before the last line of the primary
          prompt when vi editing mode is active and in command mode.
          The value is expanded like a key binding, so the standard set
          of meta- and control- prefixes and backslash escape sequences
          is available.  The â€˜\1â€™ and â€˜\2â€™ escapes begin and end
          sequences of non-printing characters, which can be used to
          embed a terminal control sequence into the mode string.  The
          default is â€˜(cmd)â€™.

     â€˜vi-ins-mode-stringâ€™
          If the SHOW-MODE-IN-PROMPT variable is enabled, this string is
          displayed immediately before the last line of the primary
          prompt when vi editing mode is active and in insertion mode.
          The value is expanded like a key binding, so the standard set
          of meta- and control- prefixes and backslash escape sequences
          is available.  The â€˜\1â€™ and â€˜\2â€™ escapes begin and end
          sequences of non-printing characters, which can be used to
          embed a terminal control sequence into the mode string.  The
          default is â€˜(ins)â€™.

     â€˜visible-statsâ€™
          If set to â€˜onâ€™, a character denoting a file's type is appended
          to the filename when listing possible completions.  The
          default is â€˜offâ€™.

Key Bindings
     The syntax for controlling key bindings in the init file is simple.
     First you need to find the name of the command that you want to
     change.  The following sections contain tables of the command name,
     the default keybinding, if any, and a short description of what the
     command does.

     Once you know the name of the command, simply place on a line in
     the init file the name of the key you wish to bind the command to,
     a colon, and then the name of the command.  There can be no space
     between the key name and the colon - that will be interpreted as
     part of the key name.  The name of the key can be expressed in
     different ways, depending on what you find most comfortable.

     In addition to command names, Readline allows keys to be bound to a
     string that is inserted when the key is pressed (a MACRO).  The
     difference between a macro and a command is that a macro is
     enclosed in single or double quotes.

     The â€˜bind -pâ€™ command displays Readline function names and bindings
     in a format that can be put directly into an initialization file.
     See Bash Builtins.

     KEYNAME: FUNCTION-NAME or MACRO
          KEYNAME is the name of a key spelled out in English.  For
          example:
               Control-u: universal-argument
               Meta-Rubout: backward-kill-word
               Control-o: "> output"

          In the example above, â€˜C-uâ€™ is bound to the function
          â€˜universal-argumentâ€™, â€˜M-DELâ€™ is bound to the function
          â€˜backward-kill-wordâ€™, and â€˜C-oâ€™ is bound to run the macro
          expressed on the right hand side (that is, to insert the text
          â€˜> outputâ€™ into the line).

          This key binding syntax recognizes a number of symbolic
          character names: DEL, ESC, ESCAPE, LFD, NEWLINE, RET, RETURN,
          RUBOUT (a destructive backspace), SPACE, SPC, and TAB.

     "KEYSEQ": FUNCTION-NAME or MACRO
          KEYSEQ differs from KEYNAME above in that strings denoting an
          entire key sequence can be specified, by placing the key
          sequence in double quotes.  Some GNU Emacs style key escapes
          can be used, as in the following example, but none of the
          special character names are recognized.

               "\C-u": universal-argument
               "\C-x\C-r": re-read-init-file
               "\e[11~": "Function Key 1"

          In the above example, â€˜C-uâ€™ is again bound to the function
          â€˜universal-argumentâ€™ (just as it was in the first example),
          â€˜â€˜C-xâ€™ â€˜C-râ€™â€™ is bound to the function â€˜re-read-init-fileâ€™,
          and â€˜<ESC> <[> <1> <1> <~>â€™ is bound to insert the text
          â€˜Function Key 1â€™.

     The following GNU Emacs style escape sequences are available when
     specifying key sequences:

     â€˜\C-â€™
          A control prefix.
     â€˜\M-â€™
          Adding the meta prefix or converting the following character
          to a meta character, as described above under
          â€˜force-meta-prefixâ€™ (see â€˜Variable Settingsâ€™ in Readline Init
          File Syntax).
     â€˜\eâ€™
          An escape character.
     â€˜\\â€™
          Backslash.
     â€˜\"â€™
          <">, a double quotation mark.
     â€˜\'â€™
          <'>, a single quote or apostrophe.

     In addition to the GNU Emacs style escape sequences, a second set
     of backslash escapes is available:

     â€˜\aâ€™
          alert (bell)
     â€˜\bâ€™
          backspace
     â€˜\dâ€™
          delete
     â€˜\fâ€™
          form feed
     â€˜\nâ€™
          newline
     â€˜\râ€™
          carriage return
     â€˜\tâ€™
          horizontal tab
     â€˜\vâ€™
          vertical tab
     â€˜\NNNâ€™
          The eight-bit character whose value is the octal value NNN
          (one to three digits).
     â€˜\xHHâ€™
          The eight-bit character whose value is the hexadecimal value
          HH (one or two hex digits).

     When entering the text of a macro, single or double quotes must be
     used to indicate a macro definition.  Unquoted text is assumed to
     be a function name.  The backslash escapes described above are
     expanded in the macro body.  Backslash will quote any other
     character in the macro text, including â€˜"â€™ and â€˜'â€™.  For example,
     the following binding will make â€˜â€˜C-xâ€™ \â€™ insert a single â€˜\â€™ into
     the line:
          "\C-x\\": "\\"

8.3.2 Conditional Init Constructs
---------------------------------

Readline implements a facility similar in spirit to the conditional
compilation features of the C preprocessor which allows key bindings and
variable settings to be performed as the result of tests.  There are
four parser directives available.

â€˜$ifâ€™
     The â€˜$ifâ€™ construct allows bindings to be made based on the editing
     mode, the terminal being used, or the application using Readline.
     The text of the test, after any comparison operator, extends to the
     end of the line; unless otherwise noted, no characters are required
     to isolate it.

     â€˜modeâ€™
          The â€˜mode=â€™ form of the â€˜$ifâ€™ directive is used to test
          whether Readline is in â€˜emacsâ€™ or â€˜viâ€™ mode.  This may be used
          in conjunction with the â€˜set keymapâ€™ command, for instance, to
          set bindings in the â€˜emacs-standardâ€™ and â€˜emacs-ctlxâ€™ keymaps
          only if Readline is starting out in â€˜emacsâ€™ mode.

     â€˜termâ€™
          The â€˜term=â€™ form may be used to include terminal-specific key
          bindings, perhaps to bind the key sequences output by the
          terminal's function keys.  The word on the right side of the
          â€˜=â€™ is tested against both the full name of the terminal and
          the portion of the terminal name before the first â€˜-â€™.  This
          allows â€˜xtermâ€™ to match both â€˜xtermâ€™ and â€˜xterm-256colorâ€™, for
          instance.

     â€˜versionâ€™
          The â€˜versionâ€™ test may be used to perform comparisons against
          specific Readline versions.  The â€˜versionâ€™ expands to the
          current Readline version.  The set of comparison operators
          includes â€˜=â€™ (and â€˜==â€™), â€˜!=â€™, â€˜<=â€™, â€˜>=â€™, â€˜<â€™, and â€˜>â€™.  The
          version number supplied on the right side of the operator
          consists of a major version number, an optional decimal point,
          and an optional minor version (e.g., â€˜7.1â€™).  If the minor
          version is omitted, it defaults to â€˜0â€™.  The operator may be
          separated from the string â€˜versionâ€™ and from the version
          number argument by whitespace.  The following example sets a
          variable if the Readline version being used is 7.0 or newer:
               $if version >= 7.0
               set show-mode-in-prompt on
               $endif

     â€˜applicationâ€™
          The APPLICATION construct is used to include
          application-specific settings.  Each program using the
          Readline library sets the APPLICATION NAME, and you can test
          for a particular value.  This could be used to bind key
          sequences to functions useful for a specific program.  For
          instance, the following command adds a key sequence that
          quotes the current or previous word in Bash:
               $if Bash
               # Quote the current or previous word
               "\C-xq": "\eb\"\ef\""
               $endif

     â€˜variableâ€™
          The VARIABLE construct provides simple equality tests for
          Readline variables and values.  The permitted comparison
          operators are â€˜=â€™, â€˜==â€™, and â€˜!=â€™.  The variable name must be
          separated from the comparison operator by whitespace; the
          operator may be separated from the value on the right hand
          side by whitespace.  String and boolean variables may be
          tested.  Boolean variables must be tested against the values
          ON and OFF.  The following example is equivalent to the
          â€˜mode=emacsâ€™ test described above:
               $if editing-mode == emacs
               set show-mode-in-prompt on
               $endif

â€˜$elseâ€™
     Commands in this branch of the â€˜$ifâ€™ directive are executed if the
     test fails.

â€˜$endifâ€™
     This command, as seen in the previous example, terminates an â€˜$ifâ€™
     command.

â€˜$includeâ€™
     This directive takes a single filename as an argument and reads
     commands and key bindings from that file.  For example, the
     following directive reads from â€˜/etc/inputrcâ€™:
          $include /etc/inputrc

8.3.3 Sample Init File
----------------------

Here is an example of an INPUTRC file.  This illustrates key binding,
variable assignment, and conditional syntax.

     # This file controls the behavior of line input editing for
     # programs that use the GNU Readline library.  Existing
     # programs include FTP, Bash, and GDB.
     #
     # You can re-read the inputrc file with C-x C-r.
     # Lines beginning with '#' are comments.
     #
     # First, include any system-wide bindings and variable
     # assignments from /etc/Inputrc
     $include /etc/Inputrc

     #
     # Set various bindings for emacs mode.

     set editing-mode emacs

     $if mode=emacs

     Meta-Control-h:	backward-kill-word	Text after the function name is ignored

     #
     # Arrow keys in keypad mode
     #
     #"\M-OD":        backward-char
     #"\M-OC":        forward-char
     #"\M-OA":        previous-history
     #"\M-OB":        next-history
     #
     # Arrow keys in ANSI mode
     #
     "\M-[D":        backward-char
     "\M-[C":        forward-char
     "\M-[A":        previous-history
     "\M-[B":        next-history
     #
     # Arrow keys in 8 bit keypad mode
     #
     #"\M-\C-OD":       backward-char
     #"\M-\C-OC":       forward-char
     #"\M-\C-OA":       previous-history
     #"\M-\C-OB":       next-history
     #
     # Arrow keys in 8 bit ANSI mode
     #
     #"\M-\C-[D":       backward-char
     #"\M-\C-[C":       forward-char
     #"\M-\C-[A":       previous-history
     #"\M-\C-[B":       next-history

     C-q: quoted-insert

     $endif

     # An old-style binding.  This happens to be the default.
     TAB: complete

     # Macros that are convenient for shell interaction
     $if Bash
     # edit the path
     "\C-xp": "PATH=${PATH}\e\C-e\C-a\ef\C-f"
     # prepare to type a quoted word --
     # insert open and close double quotes
     # and move to just after the open quote
     "\C-x\"": "\"\"\C-b"
     # insert a backslash (testing backslash escapes
     # in sequences and macros)
     "\C-x\\": "\\"
     # Quote the current or previous word
     "\C-xq": "\eb\"\ef\""
     # Add a binding to refresh the line, which is unbound
     "\C-xr": redraw-current-line
     # Edit variable on current line.
     "\M-\C-v": "\C-a\C-k$\C-y\M-\C-e\C-a\C-y="
     $endif

     # use a visible bell if one is available
     set bell-style visible

     # don't strip characters to 7 bits when reading
     set input-meta on

     # allow iso-latin1 characters to be inserted rather
     # than converted to prefix-meta sequences
     set convert-meta off

     # display characters with the eighth bit set directly
     # rather than as meta-prefixed characters
     set output-meta on

     # if there are 150 or more possible completions for a word,
     # ask whether or not the user wants to see all of them
     set completion-query-items 150

     # For FTP
     $if Ftp
     "\C-xg": "get \M-?"
     "\C-xt": "put \M-?"
     "\M-.": yank-last-arg
     $endif

8.4 Bindable Readline Commands
==============================

This section describes Readline commands that may be bound to key
sequences.  You can list your key bindings by executing â€˜bind -Pâ€™ or,
for a more terse format, suitable for an INPUTRC file, â€˜bind -pâ€™.  (See
Bash Builtins.)  Command names without an accompanying key sequence are
unbound by default.

   In the following descriptions, â€œpointâ€ refers to the current cursor
position, and â€œmarkâ€ refers to a cursor position saved by the â€˜set-markâ€™
command.  The text between the point and mark is referred to as the
â€œregionâ€.  Readline has the concept of an _active region_: when the
region is active, Readline redisplay highlights the region using the
value of the â€˜active-region-start-colorâ€™ variable.  The
â€˜enable-active-regionâ€™ variable turns this on and off.  Several commands
set the region to active; those are noted below.

8.4.1 Commands For Moving
-------------------------

â€˜beginning-of-line (C-a)â€™
     Move to the start of the current line.  This may also be bound to
     the Home key on some keyboards.

â€˜end-of-line (C-e)â€™
     Move to the end of the line.  This may also be bound to the End key
     on some keyboards.

â€˜forward-char (C-f)â€™
     Move forward a character.  This may also be bound to the right
     arrow key on some keyboards.

â€˜backward-char (C-b)â€™
     Move back a character.  This may also be bound to the left arrow
     key on some keyboards.

â€˜forward-word (M-f)â€™
     Move forward to the end of the next word.  Words are composed of
     letters and digits.

â€˜backward-word (M-b)â€™
     Move back to the start of the current or previous word.  Words are
     composed of letters and digits.

â€˜shell-forward-word (M-C-f)â€™
     Move forward to the end of the next word.  Words are delimited by
     non-quoted shell metacharacters.

â€˜shell-backward-word (M-C-b)â€™
     Move back to the start of the current or previous word.  Words are
     delimited by non-quoted shell metacharacters.

â€˜previous-screen-line ()â€™
     Attempt to move point to the same physical screen column on the
     previous physical screen line.  This will not have the desired
     effect if the current Readline line does not take up more than one
     physical line or if point is not greater than the length of the
     prompt plus the screen width.

â€˜next-screen-line ()â€™
     Attempt to move point to the same physical screen column on the
     next physical screen line.  This will not have the desired effect
     if the current Readline line does not take up more than one
     physical line or if the length of the current Readline line is not
     greater than the length of the prompt plus the screen width.

â€˜clear-display (M-C-l)â€™
     Clear the screen and, if possible, the terminal's scrollback
     buffer, then redraw the current line, leaving the current line at
     the top of the screen.

â€˜clear-screen (C-l)â€™
     Clear the screen, then redraw the current line, leaving the current
     line at the top of the screen.  If given a numeric argument, this
     refreshes the current line without clearing the screen.

â€˜redraw-current-line ()â€™
     Refresh the current line.  By default, this is unbound.

8.4.2 Commands For Manipulating The History
-------------------------------------------

â€˜accept-line (Newline or Return)â€™
     Accept the line regardless of where the cursor is.  If this line is
     non-empty, add it to the history list according to the setting of
     the â€˜HISTCONTROLâ€™ and â€˜HISTIGNOREâ€™ variables.  If this line is a
     modified history line, then restore the history line to its
     original state.

â€˜previous-history (C-p)â€™
     Move 'back' through the history list, fetching the previous
     command.  This may also be bound to the up arrow key on some
     keyboards.

â€˜next-history (C-n)â€™
     Move 'forward' through the history list, fetching the next command.
     This may also be bound to the down arrow key on some keyboards.

â€˜beginning-of-history (M-<)â€™
     Move to the first line in the history.

â€˜end-of-history (M->)â€™
     Move to the end of the input history, i.e., the line currently
     being entered.

â€˜reverse-search-history (C-r)â€™
     Search backward starting at the current line and moving 'up'
     through the history as necessary.  This is an incremental search.
     This command sets the region to the matched text and activates the
     region.

â€˜forward-search-history (C-s)â€™
     Search forward starting at the current line and moving 'down'
     through the history as necessary.  This is an incremental search.
     This command sets the region to the matched text and activates the
     region.

â€˜non-incremental-reverse-search-history (M-p)â€™
     Search backward starting at the current line and moving 'up'
     through the history as necessary using a non-incremental search for
     a string supplied by the user.  The search string may match
     anywhere in a history line.

â€˜non-incremental-forward-search-history (M-n)â€™
     Search forward starting at the current line and moving 'down'
     through the history as necessary using a non-incremental search for
     a string supplied by the user.  The search string may match
     anywhere in a history line.

â€˜history-search-backward ()â€™
     Search backward through the history for the string of characters
     between the start of the current line and the point.  The search
     string must match at the beginning of a history line.  This is a
     non-incremental search.  By default, this command is unbound, but
     may be bound to the Page Down key on some keyboards.

â€˜history-search-forward ()â€™
     Search forward through the history for the string of characters
     between the start of the current line and the point.  The search
     string must match at the beginning of a history line.  This is a
     non-incremental search.  By default, this command is unbound, but
     may be bound to the Page Up key on some keyboards.

â€˜history-substring-search-backward ()â€™
     Search backward through the history for the string of characters
     between the start of the current line and the point.  The search
     string may match anywhere in a history line.  This is a
     non-incremental search.  By default, this command is unbound.

â€˜history-substring-search-forward ()â€™
     Search forward through the history for the string of characters
     between the start of the current line and the point.  The search
     string may match anywhere in a history line.  This is a
     non-incremental search.  By default, this command is unbound.

â€˜yank-nth-arg (M-C-y)â€™
     Insert the first argument to the previous command (usually the
     second word on the previous line) at point.  With an argument N,
     insert the Nth word from the previous command (the words in the
     previous command begin with word 0).  A negative argument inserts
     the Nth word from the end of the previous command.  Once the
     argument N is computed, this uses the history expansion facilities
     to extract the Nth word, as if the â€˜!Nâ€™ history expansion had been
     specified.

â€˜yank-last-arg (M-. or M-_)â€™
     Insert last argument to the previous command (the last word of the
     previous history entry).  With a numeric argument, behave exactly
     like â€˜yank-nth-argâ€™.  Successive calls to â€˜yank-last-argâ€™ move back
     through the history list, inserting the last word (or the word
     specified by the argument to the first call) of each line in turn.
     Any numeric argument supplied to these successive calls determines
     the direction to move through the history.  A negative argument
     switches the direction through the history (back or forward).  This
     uses the history expansion facilities to extract the last word, as
     if the â€˜!$â€™ history expansion had been specified.

â€˜operate-and-get-next (C-o)â€™
     Accept the current line for return to the calling application as if
     a newline had been entered, and fetch the next line relative to the
     current line from the history for editing.  A numeric argument, if
     supplied, specifies the history entry to use instead of the current
     line.

â€˜fetch-history ()â€™
     With a numeric argument, fetch that entry from the history list and
     make it the current line.  Without an argument, move back to the
     first entry in the history list.

8.4.3 Commands For Changing Text
--------------------------------

â€˜end-of-file (usually C-d)â€™
     The character indicating end-of-file as set, for example, by
     â€˜sttyâ€™.  If this character is read when there are no characters on
     the line, and point is at the beginning of the line, Readline
     interprets it as the end of input and returns EOF.

â€˜delete-char (C-d)â€™
     Delete the character at point.  If this function is bound to the
     same character as the tty EOF character, as â€˜C-dâ€™ commonly is, see
     above for the effects.  This may also be bound to the Delete key on
     some keyboards.

â€˜backward-delete-char (Rubout)â€™
     Delete the character behind the cursor.  A numeric argument means
     to kill the characters, saving them on the kill ring, instead of
     deleting them.

â€˜forward-backward-delete-char ()â€™
     Delete the character under the cursor, unless the cursor is at the
     end of the line, in which case the character behind the cursor is
     deleted.  By default, this is not bound to a key.

â€˜quoted-insert (C-q or C-v)â€™
     Add the next character typed to the line verbatim.  This is how to
     insert key sequences like â€˜C-qâ€™, for example.

â€˜self-insert (a, b, A, 1, !, ...)â€™
     Insert the character typed.

â€˜bracketed-paste-begin ()â€™
     This function is intended to be bound to the "bracketed paste"
     escape sequence sent by some terminals, and such a binding is
     assigned by default.  It allows Readline to insert the pasted text
     as a single unit without treating each character as if it had been
     read from the keyboard.  The characters are inserted as if each one
     was bound to â€˜self-insertâ€™ instead of executing any editing
     commands.

     Bracketed paste sets the region (the characters between point and
     the mark) to the inserted text.  It sets the _active region_.

â€˜transpose-chars (C-t)â€™
     Drag the character before the cursor forward over the character at
     the cursor, moving the cursor forward as well.  If the insertion
     point is at the end of the line, then this transposes the last two
     characters of the line.  Negative arguments have no effect.

â€˜transpose-words (M-t)â€™
     Drag the word before point past the word after point, moving point
     past that word as well.  If the insertion point is at the end of
     the line, this transposes the last two words on the line.

â€˜shell-transpose-words (M-C-t)â€™
     Drag the word before point past the word after point, moving point
     past that word as well.  If the insertion point is at the end of
     the line, this transposes the last two words on the line.  Word
     boundaries are the same as â€˜shell-forward-wordâ€™ and
     â€˜shell-backward-wordâ€™.

â€˜upcase-word (M-u)â€™
     Uppercase the current (or following) word.  With a negative
     argument, uppercase the previous word, but do not move the cursor.

â€˜downcase-word (M-l)â€™
     Lowercase the current (or following) word.  With a negative
     argument, lowercase the previous word, but do not move the cursor.

â€˜capitalize-word (M-c)â€™
     Capitalize the current (or following) word.  With a negative
     argument, capitalize the previous word, but do not move the cursor.

â€˜overwrite-mode ()â€™
     Toggle overwrite mode.  With an explicit positive numeric argument,
     switches to overwrite mode.  With an explicit non-positive numeric
     argument, switches to insert mode.  This command affects only
     â€˜emacsâ€™ mode; â€˜viâ€™ mode does overwrite differently.  Each call to
     â€˜readline()â€™ starts in insert mode.

     In overwrite mode, characters bound to â€˜self-insertâ€™ replace the
     text at point rather than pushing the text to the right.
     Characters bound to â€˜backward-delete-charâ€™ replace the character
     before point with a space.

     By default, this command is unbound, but may be bound to the Insert
     key on some keyboards.

8.4.4 Killing And Yanking
-------------------------

â€˜kill-line (C-k)â€™
     Kill the text from point to the end of the current line.  With a
     negative numeric argument, kill backward from the cursor to the
     beginning of the line.

â€˜backward-kill-line (C-x Rubout)â€™
     Kill backward from the cursor to the beginning of the current line.
     With a negative numeric argument, kill forward from the cursor to
     the end of the line.

â€˜unix-line-discard (C-u)â€™
     Kill backward from the cursor to the beginning of the current line.

â€˜kill-whole-line ()â€™
     Kill all characters on the current line, no matter where point is.
     By default, this is unbound.

â€˜kill-word (M-d)â€™
     Kill from point to the end of the current word, or if between
     words, to the end of the next word.  Word boundaries are the same
     as â€˜forward-wordâ€™.

â€˜backward-kill-word (M-<DEL>)â€™
     Kill the word behind point.  Word boundaries are the same as
     â€˜backward-wordâ€™.

â€˜shell-kill-word (M-C-d)â€™
     Kill from point to the end of the current word, or if between
     words, to the end of the next word.  Word boundaries are the same
     as â€˜shell-forward-wordâ€™.

â€˜shell-backward-kill-word ()â€™
     Kill the word behind point.  Word boundaries are the same as
     â€˜shell-backward-wordâ€™.

â€˜unix-word-rubout (C-w)â€™
     Kill the word behind point, using white space as a word boundary,
     saving the killed text on the kill-ring.

â€˜unix-filename-rubout ()â€™
     Kill the word behind point, using white space and the slash
     character as the word boundaries, saving the killed text on the
     kill-ring.

â€˜delete-horizontal-space ()â€™
     Delete all spaces and tabs around point.  By default, this is
     unbound.

â€˜kill-region ()â€™
     Kill the text in the current region.  By default, this command is
     unbound.

â€˜copy-region-as-kill ()â€™
     Copy the text in the region to the kill buffer, so it can be yanked
     right away.  By default, this command is unbound.

â€˜copy-backward-word ()â€™
     Copy the word before point to the kill buffer.  The word boundaries
     are the same as â€˜backward-wordâ€™.  By default, this command is
     unbound.

â€˜copy-forward-word ()â€™
     Copy the word following point to the kill buffer.  The word
     boundaries are the same as â€˜forward-wordâ€™.  By default, this
     command is unbound.

â€˜yank (C-y)â€™
     Yank the top of the kill ring into the buffer at point.

â€˜yank-pop (M-y)â€™
     Rotate the kill-ring, and yank the new top.  You can only do this
     if the prior command is â€˜yankâ€™ or â€˜yank-popâ€™.

8.4.5 Specifying Numeric Arguments
----------------------------------

â€˜digit-argument (M-0, M-1, ... M--)â€™
     Add this digit to the argument already accumulating, or start a new
     argument.  â€˜M--â€™ starts a negative argument.

â€˜universal-argument ()â€™
     This is another way to specify an argument.  If this command is
     followed by one or more digits, optionally with a leading minus
     sign, those digits define the argument.  If the command is followed
     by digits, executing â€˜universal-argumentâ€™ again ends the numeric
     argument, but is otherwise ignored.  As a special case, if this
     command is immediately followed by a character that is neither a
     digit nor minus sign, the argument count for the next command is
     multiplied by four.  The argument count is initially one, so
     executing this function the first time makes the argument count
     four, a second time makes the argument count sixteen, and so on.
     By default, this is not bound to a key.

8.4.6 Letting Readline Type For You
-----------------------------------

â€˜complete (<TAB>)â€™
     Attempt to perform completion on the text before point.  The actual
     completion performed is application-specific.  Bash attempts
     completion by first checking for any programmable completions for
     the command word (see Programmable Completion), otherwise treating
     the text as a variable (if the text begins with â€˜$â€™), username (if
     the text begins with â€˜~â€™), hostname (if the text begins with â€˜@â€™),
     or command (including aliases, functions, and builtins) in turn.
     If none of these produces a match, it falls back to filename
     completion.

â€˜possible-completions (M-?)â€™
     List the possible completions of the text before point.  When
     displaying completions, Readline sets the number of columns used
     for display to the value of â€˜completion-display-widthâ€™, the value
     of the environment variable â€˜COLUMNSâ€™, or the screen width, in that
     order.

â€˜insert-completions (M-*)â€™
     Insert all completions of the text before point that would have
     been generated by â€˜possible-completionsâ€™, separated by a space.

â€˜menu-complete ()â€™
     Similar to â€˜completeâ€™, but replaces the word to be completed with a
     single match from the list of possible completions.  Repeatedly
     executing â€˜menu-completeâ€™ steps through the list of possible
     completions, inserting each match in turn.  At the end of the list
     of completions, â€˜menu-completeâ€™ rings the bell (subject to the
     setting of â€˜bell-styleâ€™) and restores the original text.  An
     argument of N moves N positions forward in the list of matches; a
     negative argument moves backward through the list.  This command is
     intended to be bound to <TAB>, but is unbound by default.

â€˜menu-complete-backward ()â€™
     Identical to â€˜menu-completeâ€™, but moves backward through the list
     of possible completions, as if â€˜menu-completeâ€™ had been given a
     negative argument.  This command is unbound by default.

â€˜export-completions ()â€™
     Perform completion on the word before point as described above and
     write the list of possible completions to Readline's output stream
     using the following format, writing information on separate lines:

        â€¢ the number of matches N;
        â€¢ the word being completed;
        â€¢ S:E, where S and E are the start and end offsets of the word
          in the Readline line buffer; then
        â€¢ each match, one per line

     If there are no matches, the first line will be "0", and this
     command does not print any output after the S:E.  If there is only
     a single match, this prints a single line containing it.  If there
     is more than one match, this prints the common prefix of the
     matches, which may be empty, on the first line after the S:E, then
     the matches on subsequent lines.  In this case, N will include the
     first line with the common prefix.

     The user or application should be able to accommodate the
     possibility of a blank line.  The intent is that the user or
     application reads N lines after the line containing S:E to obtain
     the match list.  This command is unbound by default.

â€˜delete-char-or-list ()â€™
     Deletes the character under the cursor if not at the beginning or
     end of the line (like â€˜delete-charâ€™).  At the end of the line, it
     behaves identically to â€˜possible-completionsâ€™.  This command is
     unbound by default.

â€˜complete-filename (M-/)â€™
     Attempt filename completion on the text before point.

â€˜possible-filename-completions (C-x /)â€™
     List the possible completions of the text before point, treating it
     as a filename.

â€˜complete-username (M-~)â€™
     Attempt completion on the text before point, treating it as a
     username.

â€˜possible-username-completions (C-x ~)â€™
     List the possible completions of the text before point, treating it
     as a username.

â€˜complete-variable (M-$)â€™
     Attempt completion on the text before point, treating it as a shell
     variable.

â€˜possible-variable-completions (C-x $)â€™
     List the possible completions of the text before point, treating it
     as a shell variable.

â€˜complete-hostname (M-@)â€™
     Attempt completion on the text before point, treating it as a
     hostname.

â€˜possible-hostname-completions (C-x @)â€™
     List the possible completions of the text before point, treating it
     as a hostname.

â€˜complete-command (M-!)â€™
     Attempt completion on the text before point, treating it as a
     command name.  Command completion attempts to match the text
     against aliases, reserved words, shell functions, shell builtins,
     and finally executable filenames, in that order.

â€˜possible-command-completions (C-x !)â€™
     List the possible completions of the text before point, treating it
     as a command name.

â€˜dynamic-complete-history (M-<TAB>)â€™
     Attempt completion on the text before point, comparing the text
     against history list entries for possible completion matches.

â€˜dabbrev-expand ()â€™
     Attempt menu completion on the text before point, comparing the
     text against lines from the history list for possible completion
     matches.

â€˜complete-into-braces (M-{)â€™
     Perform filename completion and insert the list of possible
     completions enclosed within braces so the list is available to the
     shell (see Brace Expansion).

8.4.7 Keyboard Macros
---------------------

â€˜start-kbd-macro (C-x ()â€™
     Begin saving the characters typed into the current keyboard macro.

â€˜end-kbd-macro (C-x ))â€™
     Stop saving the characters typed into the current keyboard macro
     and save the definition.

â€˜call-last-kbd-macro (C-x e)â€™
     Re-execute the last keyboard macro defined, by making the
     characters in the macro appear as if typed at the keyboard.

â€˜print-last-kbd-macro ()â€™
     Print the last keyboard macro defined in a format suitable for the
     INPUTRC file.

8.4.8 Some Miscellaneous Commands
---------------------------------

â€˜re-read-init-file (C-x C-r)â€™
     Read in the contents of the INPUTRC file, and incorporate any
     bindings or variable assignments found there.

â€˜abort (C-g)â€™
     Abort the current editing command and ring the terminal's bell
     (subject to the setting of â€˜bell-styleâ€™).

â€˜do-lowercase-version (M-A, M-B, M-X, ...)â€™
     If the metafied character X is upper case, run the command that is
     bound to the corresponding metafied lower case character.  The
     behavior is undefined if X is already lower case.

â€˜prefix-meta (<ESC>)â€™
     Metafy the next character typed.  Typing â€˜<ESC> fâ€™ is equivalent to
     typing â€˜M-fâ€™.

â€˜undo (C-_ or C-x C-u)â€™
     Incremental undo, separately remembered for each line.

â€˜revert-line (M-r)â€™
     Undo all changes made to this line.  This is like executing the
     â€˜undoâ€™ command enough times to get back to the initial state.

â€˜tilde-expand (M-&)â€™
     Perform tilde expansion on the current word.

â€˜set-mark (C-@)â€™
     Set the mark to the point.  If a numeric argument is supplied, set
     the mark to that position.

â€˜exchange-point-and-mark (C-x C-x)â€™
     Swap the point with the mark.  Set the current cursor position to
     the saved position, then set the mark to the old cursor position.

â€˜character-search (C-])â€™
     Read a character and move point to the next occurrence of that
     character.  A negative argument searches for previous occurrences.

â€˜character-search-backward (M-C-])â€™
     Read a character and move point to the previous occurrence of that
     character.  A negative argument searches for subsequent
     occurrences.

â€˜skip-csi-sequence ()â€™
     Read enough characters to consume a multi-key sequence such as
     those defined for keys like Home and End.  CSI sequences begin with
     a Control Sequence Indicator (CSI), usually â€˜ESC [â€™.  If this
     sequence is bound to "\e[", keys producing CSI sequences have no
     effect unless explicitly bound to a Readline command, instead of
     inserting stray characters into the editing buffer.  This is
     unbound by default, but usually bound to â€˜ESC [â€™.

â€˜insert-comment (M-#)â€™
     Without a numeric argument, insert the value of the â€˜comment-beginâ€™
     variable at the beginning of the current line.  If a numeric
     argument is supplied, this command acts as a toggle: if the
     characters at the beginning of the line do not match the value of
     â€˜comment-beginâ€™, insert the value; otherwise delete the characters
     in â€˜comment-beginâ€™ from the beginning of the line.  In either case,
     the line is accepted as if a newline had been typed.  The default
     value of â€˜comment-beginâ€™ causes this command to make the current
     line a shell comment.  If a numeric argument causes the comment
     character to be removed, the line will be executed by the shell.

â€˜dump-functions ()â€™
     Print all of the functions and their key bindings to the Readline
     output stream.  If a numeric argument is supplied, the output is
     formatted in such a way that it can be made part of an INPUTRC
     file.  This command is unbound by default.

â€˜dump-variables ()â€™
     Print all of the settable variables and their values to the
     Readline output stream.  If a numeric argument is supplied, the
     output is formatted in such a way that it can be made part of an
     INPUTRC file.  This command is unbound by default.

â€˜dump-macros ()â€™
     Print all of the Readline key sequences bound to macros and the
     strings they output to the Readline output stream.  If a numeric
     argument is supplied, the output is formatted in such a way that it
     can be made part of an INPUTRC file.  This command is unbound by
     default.

â€˜execute-named-command (M-x)â€™
     Read a bindable Readline command name from the input and execute
     the function to which it's bound, as if the key sequence to which
     it was bound appeared in the input.  If this function is supplied
     with a numeric argument, it passes that argument to the function it
     executes.

â€˜spell-correct-word (C-x s)â€™
     Perform spelling correction on the current word, treating it as a
     directory or filename, in the same way as the â€˜cdspellâ€™ shell
     option.  Word boundaries are the same as those used by
     â€˜shell-forward-wordâ€™.

â€˜glob-complete-word (M-g)â€™
     Treat the word before point as a pattern for pathname expansion,
     with an asterisk implicitly appended, then use the pattern to
     generate a list of matching file names for possible completions.

â€˜glob-expand-word (C-x *)â€™
     Treat the word before point as a pattern for pathname expansion,
     and insert the list of matching file names, replacing the word.  If
     a numeric argument is supplied, append a â€˜*â€™ before pathname
     expansion.

â€˜glob-list-expansions (C-x g)â€™
     Display the list of expansions that would have been generated by
     â€˜glob-expand-wordâ€™, and redisplay the line.  If a numeric argument
     is supplied, append a â€˜*â€™ before pathname expansion.

â€˜shell-expand-line (M-C-e)â€™
     Expand the line by performing shell word expansions.  This performs
     alias and history expansion, $'STRING' and $"STRING" quoting, tilde
     expansion, parameter and variable expansion, arithmetic expansion,
     command and process substitution, word splitting, and quote
     removal.  An explicit argument suppresses command and process
     substitution.

â€˜history-expand-line (M-^)â€™
     Perform history expansion on the current line.

â€˜magic-space ()â€™
     Perform history expansion on the current line and insert a space
     (see History Interaction).

â€˜alias-expand-line ()â€™
     Perform alias expansion on the current line (see Aliases).

â€˜history-and-alias-expand-line ()â€™
     Perform history and alias expansion on the current line.

â€˜insert-last-argument (M-. or M-_)â€™
     A synonym for â€˜yank-last-argâ€™.

â€˜edit-and-execute-command (C-x C-e)â€™
     Invoke an editor on the current command line, and execute the
     result as shell commands.  Bash attempts to invoke â€˜$VISUALâ€™,
     â€˜$EDITORâ€™, and â€˜emacsâ€™ as the editor, in that order.

â€˜display-shell-version (C-x C-v)â€™
     Display version information about the current instance of Bash.

8.5 Readline vi Mode
====================

While the Readline library does not have a full set of â€˜viâ€™ editing
functions, it does contain enough to allow simple editing of the line.
The Readline â€˜viâ€™ mode behaves as specified in the â€˜shâ€™ description in
the POSIX standard.

   You can use the â€˜set -o emacsâ€™ and â€˜set -o viâ€™ commands (see The Set
Builtin) to switch interactively between â€˜emacsâ€™ and â€˜viâ€™ editing modes,
The Readline default is â€˜emacsâ€™ mode.

   When you enter a line in â€˜viâ€™ mode, you are already placed in
'insertion' mode, as if you had typed an â€˜iâ€™.  Pressing <ESC> switches
you into 'command' mode, where you can edit the text of the line with
the standard â€˜viâ€™ movement keys, move to previous history lines with â€˜kâ€™
and subsequent lines with â€˜jâ€™, and so forth.

8.6 Programmable Completion
===========================

When the user attempts word completion for a command or an argument to a
command for which a completion specification (a â€œcompspecâ€) has been
defined using the â€˜completeâ€™ builtin (see Programmable Completion
Builtins), Readline invokes the programmable completion facilities.

   First, Bash identifies the command name.  If a compspec has been
defined for that command, the compspec is used to generate the list of
possible completions for the word.  If the command word is the empty
string (completion attempted at the beginning of an empty line), Bash
uses any compspec defined with the â€˜-Eâ€™ option to â€˜completeâ€™.  The â€˜-Iâ€™
option to â€˜completeâ€™ indicates that the command word is the first
non-assignment word on the line, or after a command delimiter such as
â€˜;â€™ or â€˜|â€™.  This usually indicates command name completion.

   If the command word is a full pathname, Bash searches for a compspec
for the full pathname first.  If there is no compspec for the full
pathname, Bash attempts to find a compspec for the portion following the
final slash.  If those searches do not result in a compspec, or if there
is no compspec for the command word, Bash uses any compspec defined with
the â€˜-Dâ€™ option to â€˜completeâ€™ as the default.  If there is no default
compspec, Bash performs alias expansion on the command word as a final
resort, and attempts to find a compspec for the command word resulting
from any successful expansion.

   If a compspec is not found, Bash performs its default completion
described above (see Commands For Completion).  Otherwise, once a
compspec has been found, Bash uses it to generate the list of matching
words.

   First, Bash performs the ACTIONS specified by the compspec.  This
only returns matches which are prefixes of the word being completed.
When the â€˜-fâ€™ or â€˜-dâ€™ option is used for filename or directory name
completion, Bash uses shell the variable â€˜FIGNOREâ€™ to filter the
matches.  See Bash Variables, for a description of â€˜FIGNOREâ€™.

   Next, programmable completion generates matches specified by a
pathname expansion pattern supplied as an argument to the â€˜-Gâ€™ option.
The words generated by the pattern need not match the word being
completed.  Bash uses the â€˜FIGNOREâ€™ variable to filter the matches, but
does not use the â€˜GLOBIGNOREâ€™ shell variable.

   Next, completion considers the string specified as the argument to
the â€˜-Wâ€™ option.  The string is first split using the characters in the
â€˜IFSâ€™ special variable as delimiters.  This honors shell quoting within
the string, in order to provide a mechanism for the words to contain
shell metacharacters or characters in the value of â€˜IFSâ€™.  Each word is
then expanded using brace expansion, tilde expansion, parameter and
variable expansion, command substitution, and arithmetic expansion, as
described above (see Shell Expansions).  The results are split using the
rules described above (see Word Splitting).  The results of the
expansion are prefix-matched against the word being completed, and the
matching words become possible completions.

   After these matches have been generated, Bash executes any shell
function or command specified with the â€˜-Fâ€™ and â€˜-Câ€™ options.  When the
command or function is invoked, Bash assigns values to the â€˜COMP_LINEâ€™,
â€˜COMP_POINTâ€™, â€˜COMP_KEYâ€™, and â€˜COMP_TYPEâ€™ variables as described above
(see Bash Variables).  If a shell function is being invoked, Bash also
sets the â€˜COMP_WORDSâ€™ and â€˜COMP_CWORDâ€™ variables.  When the function or
command is invoked, the first argument ($1) is the name of the command
whose arguments are being completed, the second argument ($2) is the
word being completed, and the third argument ($3) is the word preceding
the word being completed on the current command line.  There is no
filtering of the generated completions against the word being completed;
the function or command has complete freedom in generating the matches
and they do not need to match a prefix of the word.

   Any function specified with â€˜-Fâ€™ is invoked first.  The function may
use any of the shell facilities, including the â€˜compgenâ€™ and â€˜compoptâ€™
builtins described below (see Programmable Completion Builtins), to
generate the matches.  It must put the possible completions in the
â€˜COMPREPLYâ€™ array variable, one per array element.

   Next, any command specified with the â€˜-Câ€™ option is invoked in an
environment equivalent to command substitution.  It should print a list
of completions, one per line, to the standard output.  Backslash will
escape a newline, if necessary.  These are added to the set of possible
completions.

   After generating all of the possible completions, Bash applies any
filter specified with the â€˜-Xâ€™ option to the completions in the list.
The filter is a pattern as used for pathname expansion; a â€˜&â€™ in the
pattern is replaced with the text of the word being completed.  A
literal â€˜&â€™ may be escaped with a backslash; the backslash is removed
before attempting a match.  Any completion that matches the pattern is
removed from the list.  A leading â€˜!â€™ negates the pattern; in this case
Bash removes any completion that does not match the pattern.  If the
â€˜nocasematchâ€™ shell option is enabled (see the description of â€˜shoptâ€™ in
The Shopt Builtin), Bash performs the match without regard to the case
of alphabetic characters.

   Finally, programmable completion adds any prefix and suffix specified
with the â€˜-Pâ€™ and â€˜-Sâ€™ options, respectively, to each completion, and
returns the result to Readline as the list of possible completions.

   If the previously-applied actions do not generate any matches, and
the â€˜-o dirnamesâ€™ option was supplied to â€˜completeâ€™ when the compspec
was defined, Bash attempts directory name completion.

   If the â€˜-o plusdirsâ€™ option was supplied to â€˜completeâ€™ when the
compspec was defined, Bash attempts directory name completion and adds
any matches to the set of possible completions.

   By default, if a compspec is found, whatever it generates is returned
to the completion code as the full set of possible completions.  The
default Bash completions and the Readline default of filename completion
are disabled.  If the â€˜-o bashdefaultâ€™ option was supplied to â€˜completeâ€™
when the compspec was defined, and the compspec generates no matches,
Bash attempts its default completions.  If the compspec and, if
attempted, the default Bash completions generate no matches, and the â€˜-o
defaultâ€™ option was supplied to â€˜completeâ€™ when the compspec was
defined, programmable completion performs Readline's default completion.

   The options supplied to â€˜completeâ€™ and â€˜compoptâ€™ can control how
Readline treats the completions.  For instance, the â€˜-o fullquoteâ€™
option tells Readline to quote the matches as if they were filenames.
See the description of â€˜completeâ€™ (see Programmable Completion Builtins)
for details.

   When a compspec indicates that it wants directory name completion,
the programmable completion functions force Readline to append a slash
to completed names which are symbolic links to directories, subject to
the value of the MARK-DIRECTORIES Readline variable, regardless of the
setting of the MARK-SYMLINKED-DIRECTORIES Readline variable.

   There is some support for dynamically modifying completions.  This is
most useful when used in combination with a default completion specified
with â€˜-Dâ€™.  It's possible for shell functions executed as completion
functions to indicate that completion should be retried by returning an
exit status of 124.  If a shell function returns 124, and changes the
compspec associated with the command on which completion is being
attempted (supplied as the first argument when the function is
executed), programmable completion restarts from the beginning, with an
attempt to find a new compspec for that command.  This can be used to
build a set of completions dynamically as completion is attempted,
rather than loading them all at once.

   For instance, assuming that there is a library of compspecs, each
kept in a file corresponding to the name of the command, the following
default completion function would load completions dynamically:

     _completion_loader()
     {
         . "/etc/bash_completion.d/$1.sh" >/dev/null 2>&1 && return 124
     }
     complete -D -F _completion_loader -o bashdefault -o default

8.7 Programmable Completion Builtins
====================================

Three builtin commands are available to manipulate the programmable
completion facilities: one to specify how the arguments to a particular
command are to be completed, and two to modify the completion as it is
happening.

â€˜compgenâ€™
          compgen [-V VARNAME] [OPTION] [WORD]

     Generate possible completion matches for WORD according to the
     OPTIONs, which may be any option accepted by the â€˜completeâ€™ builtin
     with the exceptions of â€˜-pâ€™, â€˜-râ€™, â€˜-Dâ€™, â€˜-Eâ€™, and â€˜-Iâ€™, and write
     the matches to the standard output.

     If the â€˜-Vâ€™ option is supplied, â€˜compgenâ€™ stores the generated
     completions into the indexed array variable VARNAME instead of
     writing them to the standard output.

     When using the â€˜-Fâ€™ or â€˜-Câ€™ options, the various shell variables
     set by the programmable completion facilities, while available,
     will not have useful values.

     The matches will be generated in the same way as if the
     programmable completion code had generated them directly from a
     completion specification with the same flags.  If WORD is
     specified, only those completions matching WORD will be displayed
     or stored.

     The return value is true unless an invalid option is supplied, or
     no matches were generated.

â€˜completeâ€™
          complete [-abcdefgjksuv] [-o COMP-OPTION] [-DEI] [-A ACTION]
          [-G GLOBPAT] [-W WORDLIST] [-F FUNCTION] [-C COMMAND]
          [-X FILTERPAT] [-P PREFIX] [-S SUFFIX] NAME [NAME ...]
          complete -pr [-DEI] [NAME ...]

     Specify how arguments to each NAME should be completed.

     If the â€˜-pâ€™ option is supplied, or if no options or NAMEs are
     supplied, print existing completion specifications in a way that
     allows them to be reused as input.  The â€˜-râ€™ option removes a
     completion specification for each NAME, or, if no NAMEs are
     supplied, all completion specifications.

     The â€˜-Dâ€™ option indicates that other supplied options and actions
     should apply to the "default" command completion; that is,
     completion attempted on a command for which no completion has
     previously been defined.  The â€˜-Eâ€™ option indicates that other
     supplied options and actions should apply to "empty" command
     completion; that is, completion attempted on a blank line.  The
     â€˜-Iâ€™ option indicates that other supplied options and actions
     should apply to completion on the initial non-assignment word on
     the line, or after a command delimiter such as â€˜;â€™ or â€˜|â€™, which is
     usually command name completion.  If multiple options are supplied,
     the â€˜-Dâ€™ option takes precedence over â€˜-Eâ€™, and both take
     precedence over â€˜-Iâ€™.  If any of â€˜-Dâ€™, â€˜-Eâ€™, or â€˜-Iâ€™ are supplied,
     any other NAME arguments are ignored; these completions only apply
     to the case specified by the option.

     The process of applying these completion specifications when word
     completion is attempted is described above (see Programmable
     Completion).

     Other options, if specified, have the following meanings.  The
     arguments to the â€˜-Gâ€™, â€˜-Wâ€™, and â€˜-Xâ€™ options (and, if necessary,
     the â€˜-Pâ€™ and â€˜-Sâ€™ options) should be quoted to protect them from
     expansion before the â€˜completeâ€™ builtin is invoked.

     â€˜-o COMP-OPTIONâ€™
          The COMP-OPTION controls several aspects of the compspec's
          behavior beyond the simple generation of completions.
          COMP-OPTION may be one of:

          â€˜bashdefaultâ€™
               Perform the rest of the default Bash completions if the
               compspec generates no matches.

          â€˜defaultâ€™
               Use Readline's default filename completion if the
               compspec generates no matches.

          â€˜dirnamesâ€™
               Perform directory name completion if the compspec
               generates no matches.

          â€˜filenamesâ€™
               Tell Readline that the compspec generates filenames, so
               it can perform any filename-specific processing (such as
               adding a slash to directory names, quoting special
               characters, or suppressing trailing spaces).  This option
               is intended to be used with shell functions specified
               with â€˜-Fâ€™.

          â€˜fullquoteâ€™
               Tell Readline to quote all the completed words even if
               they are not filenames.

          â€˜noquoteâ€™
               Tell Readline not to quote the completed words if they
               are filenames (quoting filenames is the default).

          â€˜nosortâ€™
               Tell Readline not to sort the list of possible
               completions alphabetically.

          â€˜nospaceâ€™
               Tell Readline not to append a space (the default) to
               words completed at the end of the line.

          â€˜plusdirsâ€™
               After generating any matches defined by the compspec,
               attempt directory name completion and add any matches to
               the results of the other actions.

     â€˜-A ACTIONâ€™
          The ACTION may be one of the following to generate a list of
          possible completions:

          â€˜aliasâ€™
               Alias names.  May also be specified as â€˜-aâ€™.

          â€˜arrayvarâ€™
               Array variable names.

          â€˜bindingâ€™
               Readline key binding names (see Bindable Readline
               Commands).

          â€˜builtinâ€™
               Names of shell builtin commands.  May also be specified
               as â€˜-bâ€™.

          â€˜commandâ€™
               Command names.  May also be specified as â€˜-câ€™.

          â€˜directoryâ€™
               Directory names.  May also be specified as â€˜-dâ€™.

          â€˜disabledâ€™
               Names of disabled shell builtins.

          â€˜enabledâ€™
               Names of enabled shell builtins.

          â€˜exportâ€™
               Names of exported shell variables.  May also be specified
               as â€˜-eâ€™.

          â€˜fileâ€™
               File and directory names, similar to Readline's filename
               completion.  May also be specified as â€˜-fâ€™.

          â€˜functionâ€™
               Names of shell functions.

          â€˜groupâ€™
               Group names.  May also be specified as â€˜-gâ€™.

          â€˜helptopicâ€™
               Help topics as accepted by the â€˜helpâ€™ builtin (see Bash
               Builtins).

          â€˜hostnameâ€™
               Hostnames, as taken from the file specified by the
               â€˜HOSTFILEâ€™ shell variable (see Bash Variables).

          â€˜jobâ€™
               Job names, if job control is active.  May also be
               specified as â€˜-jâ€™.

          â€˜keywordâ€™
               Shell reserved words.  May also be specified as â€˜-kâ€™.

          â€˜runningâ€™
               Names of running jobs, if job control is active.

          â€˜serviceâ€™
               Service names.  May also be specified as â€˜-sâ€™.

          â€˜setoptâ€™
               Valid arguments for the â€˜-oâ€™ option to the â€˜setâ€™ builtin
               (see The Set Builtin).

          â€˜shoptâ€™
               Shell option names as accepted by the â€˜shoptâ€™ builtin
               (see Bash Builtins).

          â€˜signalâ€™
               Signal names.

          â€˜stoppedâ€™
               Names of stopped jobs, if job control is active.

          â€˜userâ€™
               User names.  May also be specified as â€˜-uâ€™.

          â€˜variableâ€™
               Names of all shell variables.  May also be specified as
               â€˜-vâ€™.

     â€˜-C COMMANDâ€™
          COMMAND is executed in a subshell environment, and its output
          is used as the possible completions.  Arguments are passed as
          with the â€˜-Fâ€™ option.

     â€˜-F FUNCTIONâ€™
          The shell function FUNCTION is executed in the current shell
          environment.  When it is executed, the first argument ($1) is
          the name of the command whose arguments are being completed,
          the second argument ($2) is the word being completed, and the
          third argument ($3) is the word preceding the word being
          completed, as described above (see Programmable Completion).
          When â€˜functionâ€™ finishes, programmable completion retrieves
          the possible completions from the value of the â€˜COMPREPLYâ€™
          array variable.

     â€˜-G GLOBPATâ€™
          Expand the filename expansion pattern GLOBPAT to generate the
          possible completions.

     â€˜-P PREFIXâ€™
          Add PREFIX to the beginning of each possible completion after
          all other options have been applied.

     â€˜-S SUFFIXâ€™
          Append SUFFIX to each possible completion after all other
          options have been applied.

     â€˜-W WORDLISTâ€™
          Split the WORDLIST using the characters in the â€˜IFSâ€™ special
          variable as delimiters, and expand each resulting word.  Shell
          quoting is honored within WORDLIST in order to provide a
          mechanism for the words to contain shell metacharacters or
          characters in the value of â€˜IFSâ€™.  The possible completions
          are the members of the resultant list which match a prefix of
          the word being completed.

     â€˜-X FILTERPATâ€™
          FILTERPAT is a pattern as used for filename expansion.  It is
          applied to the list of possible completions generated by the
          preceding options and arguments, and each completion matching
          FILTERPAT is removed from the list.  A leading â€˜!â€™ in
          FILTERPAT negates the pattern; in this case, any completion
          not matching FILTERPAT is removed.

     The return value is true unless an invalid option is supplied, an
     option other than â€˜-pâ€™, â€˜-râ€™, â€˜-Dâ€™, â€˜-Eâ€™, or â€˜-Iâ€™ is supplied
     without a NAME argument, an attempt is made to remove a completion
     specification for a NAME for which no specification exists, or an
     error occurs adding a completion specification.

â€˜compoptâ€™
          compopt [-o OPTION] [-DEI] [+o OPTION] [NAME]
     Modify completion options for each NAME according to the OPTIONs,
     or for the currently-executing completion if no NAMEs are supplied.
     If no OPTIONs are given, display the completion options for each
     NAME or the current completion.  The possible values of OPTION are
     those valid for the â€˜completeâ€™ builtin described above.

     The â€˜-Dâ€™ option indicates that other supplied options should apply
     to the "default" command completion; the â€˜-Eâ€™ option indicates that
     other supplied options should apply to "empty" command completion;
     and the â€˜-Iâ€™ option indicates that other supplied options should
     apply to completion on the initial word on the line.  These are
     determined in the same way as the â€˜completeâ€™ builtin.

     If multiple options are supplied, the â€˜-Dâ€™ option takes precedence
     over â€˜-Eâ€™, and both take precedence over â€˜-Iâ€™

     The return value is true unless an invalid option is supplied, an
     attempt is made to modify the options for a NAME for which no
     completion specification exists, or an output error occurs.

8.8 A Programmable Completion Example
=====================================

The most common way to obtain additional completion functionality beyond
the default actions â€˜completeâ€™ and â€˜compgenâ€™ provide is to use a shell
function and bind it to a particular command using â€˜complete -Fâ€™.

   The following function provides completions for the â€˜cdâ€™ builtin.  It
is a reasonably good example of what shell functions must do when used
for completion.  This function uses the word passed as â€˜$2â€™ to determine
the directory name to complete.  You can also use the â€˜COMP_WORDSâ€™ array
variable; the current word is indexed by the â€˜COMP_CWORDâ€™ variable.

   The function relies on the â€˜completeâ€™ and â€˜compgenâ€™ builtins to do
much of the work, adding only the things that the Bash â€˜cdâ€™ does beyond
accepting basic directory names: tilde expansion (see Tilde Expansion),
searching directories in $CDPATH, which is described above (see Bourne
Shell Builtins), and basic support for the â€˜cdable_varsâ€™ shell option
(see The Shopt Builtin).  â€˜_comp_cdâ€™ modifies the value of IFS so that
it contains only a newline to accommodate file names containing spaces
and tabs - â€˜compgenâ€™ prints the possible completions it generates one
per line.

   Possible completions go into the COMPREPLY array variable, one
completion per array element.  The programmable completion system
retrieves the completions from there when the function returns.

     # A completion function for the cd builtin
     # based on the cd completion function from the bash_completion package
     _comp_cd()
     {
         local IFS=$' \t\n'    # normalize IFS
         local cur _skipdot _cdpath
         local i j k

         # Tilde expansion, which also expands tilde to full pathname
         case "$2" in
         \~*)    eval cur="$2" ;;
         *)      cur=$2 ;;
         esac

         # no cdpath or absolute pathname -- straight directory completion
         if [[ -z "${CDPATH:-}" ]] || [[ "$cur" == @(./*|../*|/*) ]]; then
             # compgen prints paths one per line; could also use while loop
             IFS=$'\n'
             COMPREPLY=( $(compgen -d -- "$cur") )
             IFS=$' \t\n'
         # CDPATH+directories in the current directory if not in CDPATH
         else
             IFS=$'\n'
             _skipdot=false
             # preprocess CDPATH to convert null directory names to .
             _cdpath=${CDPATH/#:/.:}
             _cdpath=${_cdpath//::/:.:}
             _cdpath=${_cdpath/%:/:.}
             for i in ${_cdpath//:/$'\n'}; do
                 if [[ $i -ef . ]]; then _skipdot=true; fi
                 k="${#COMPREPLY[@]}"
                 for j in $( compgen -d -- "$i/$cur" ); do
                     COMPREPLY[k++]=${j#$i/}        # cut off directory
                 done
             done
             $_skipdot || COMPREPLY+=( $(compgen -d -- "$cur") )
             IFS=$' \t\n'
         fi

         # variable names if appropriate shell option set and no completions
         if shopt -q cdable_vars && [[ ${#COMPREPLY[@]} -eq 0 ]]; then
             COMPREPLY=( $(compgen -v -- "$cur") )
         fi

         return 0
     }

   We install the completion function using the â€˜-Fâ€™ option to
â€˜completeâ€™:

     # Tell readline to quote appropriate and append slashes to directories;
     # use the bash default completion for other arguments
     complete -o filenames -o nospace -o bashdefault -F _comp_cd cd

Since we'd like Bash and Readline to take care of some of the other
details for us, we use several other options to tell Bash and Readline
what to do.  The â€˜-o filenamesâ€™ option tells Readline that the possible
completions should be treated as filenames, and quoted appropriately.
That option will also cause Readline to append a slash to filenames it
can determine are directories (which is why we might want to extend
â€˜_comp_cdâ€™ to append a slash if we're using directories found via
CDPATH: Readline can't tell those completions are directories).  The â€˜-o
nospaceâ€™ option tells Readline to not append a space character to the
directory name, in case we want to append to it.  The â€˜-o bashdefaultâ€™
option brings in the rest of the "Bash default" completions - possible
completions that Bash adds to the default Readline set.  These include
things like command name completion, variable completion for words
beginning with â€˜$â€™ or â€˜${â€™, completions containing pathname expansion
patterns (see Filename Expansion), and so on.

   Once installed using â€˜completeâ€™, â€˜_comp_cdâ€™ will be called every time
we attempt word completion for a â€˜cdâ€™ command.

   Many more examples - an extensive collection of completions for most
of the common GNU, Unix, and Linux commands - are available as part of
the bash_completion project.  This is installed by default on many
GNU/Linux distributions.  Originally written by Ian Macdonald, the
project now lives at <https://github.com/scop/bash-completion/>.  There
are ports for other systems such as Solaris and Mac OS X.

   An older version of the bash_completion package is distributed with
bash in the â€˜examples/completeâ€™ subdirectory.

9 Using History Interactively
*****************************

This chapter describes how to use the GNU History Library interactively,
from a user's standpoint.  It should be considered a user's guide.  For
information on using the GNU History Library in other programs, see the
GNU Readline Library Manual.

9.1 Bash History Facilities
===========================

When the â€˜-o historyâ€™ option to the â€˜setâ€™ builtin is enabled (see The
Set Builtin), the shell provides access to the â€œcommand historyâ€, the
list of commands previously typed.  The value of the â€˜HISTSIZEâ€™ shell
variable is used as the number of commands to save in a history list:
the shell saves the text of the last â€˜$HISTSIZEâ€™ commands (default 500).
The shell stores each command in the history list prior to parameter and
variable expansion but after history expansion is performed, subject to
the values of the shell variables â€˜HISTIGNOREâ€™ and â€˜HISTCONTROLâ€™.

   When the shell starts up, Bash initializes the history list by
reading history entries from the file named by the â€˜HISTFILEâ€™ variable
(default â€˜~/.bash_historyâ€™).  This is referred to as the â€œhistory fileâ€.
The history file is truncated, if necessary, to contain no more than the
number of history entries specified by the value of the â€˜HISTFILESIZEâ€™
variable.  If â€˜HISTFILESIZEâ€™ is unset, or set to null, a non-numeric
value, or a numeric value less than zero, the history file is not
truncated.

   When the history file is read, lines beginning with the history
comment character followed immediately by a digit are interpreted as
timestamps for the following history entry.  These timestamps are
optionally displayed depending on the value of the â€˜HISTTIMEFORMATâ€™
variable (see Bash Variables).  When present, history timestamps delimit
history entries, making multi-line entries possible.

   When a shell with history enabled exits, Bash copies the last
â€˜$HISTSIZEâ€™ entries from the history list to the file named by
â€˜$HISTFILEâ€™.  If the â€˜histappendâ€™ shell option is set (see Bash
Builtins), Bash appends the entries to the history file, otherwise it
overwrites the history file.  If â€˜HISTFILEâ€™ is unset or null, or if the
history file is unwritable, the history is not saved.  After saving the
history, Bash truncates the history file to contain no more than
â€˜$HISTFILESIZEâ€™ lines as described above.

   If the â€˜HISTTIMEFORMATâ€™ variable is set, the shell writes the
timestamp information associated with each history entry to the history
file, marked with the history comment character, so timestamps are
preserved across shell sessions.  When the history file is read, lines
beginning with the history comment character followed immediately by a
digit are interpreted as timestamps for the following history entry.  As
above, when using â€˜HISTTIMEFORMATâ€™, the timestamps delimit multi-line
history entries.

   The â€˜fcâ€™ builtin command will list or edit and re-execute a portion
of the history list.  The â€˜historyâ€™ builtin can display or modify the
history list and manipulate the history file.  When using command-line
editing, search commands are available in each editing mode that provide
access to the history list (see Commands For History).

   The shell allows control over which commands are saved on the history
list.  The â€˜HISTCONTROLâ€™ and â€˜HISTIGNOREâ€™ variables are used to save
only a subset of the commands entered.  If the â€˜cmdhistâ€™ shell option is
enabled, the shell attempts to save each line of a multi-line command in
the same history entry, adding semicolons where necessary to preserve
syntactic correctness.  The â€˜lithistâ€™ shell option modifies â€˜cmdhistâ€™ by
saving the command with embedded newlines instead of semicolons.  The
â€˜shoptâ€™ builtin is used to set these options.  See The Shopt Builtin,
for a description of â€˜shoptâ€™.

9.2 Bash History Builtins
