=========================

Bash provides two builtin commands which manipulate the history list and
history file.

â€˜fcâ€™
          fc [-e ENAME] [-lnr] [FIRST] [LAST]
          fc -s [PAT=REP] [COMMAND]

     The first form selects a range of commands from FIRST to LAST from
     the history list and displays or edits and re-executes them.  Both
     FIRST and LAST may be specified as a string (to locate the most
     recent command beginning with that string) or as a number (an index
     into the history list, where a negative number is used as an offset
     from the current command number).

     When listing, a FIRST or LAST of 0 is equivalent to -1 and -0 is
     equivalent to the current command (usually the â€˜fcâ€™ command);
     otherwise 0 is equivalent to -1 and -0 is invalid.

     If LAST is not specified, it is set to the current command for
     listing and to FIRST otherwise.  If FIRST is not specified, it is
     set to the previous command for editing and âˆ’16 for listing.

     If the â€˜-lâ€™ flag is supplied, the commands are listed on standard
     output.  The â€˜-nâ€™ flag suppresses the command numbers when listing.
     The â€˜-râ€™ flag reverses the order of the listing.

     Otherwise, â€˜fcâ€™ invokes the editor named by ENAME on a file
     containing those commands.  If ENAME is not supplied, â€˜fcâ€™ uses the
     value of the following variable expansion:
     â€˜${FCEDIT:-${EDITOR:-vi}}â€™.  This says to use the value of the
     â€˜FCEDITâ€™ variable if set, or the value of the â€˜EDITORâ€™ variable if
     that is set, or â€˜viâ€™ if neither is set.  When editing is complete,
     â€˜fcâ€™ reads the file of edited commands and echoes and executes
     them.

     In the second form, â€˜fcâ€™ re-executes COMMAND after replacing each
     instance of PAT in the selected command with REP.  COMMAND is
     interpreted the same as FIRST above.

     A useful alias to use with the â€˜fcâ€™ command is â€˜r='fc -s'â€™, so that
     typing â€˜r ccâ€™ runs the last command beginning with â€˜ccâ€™ and typing
     â€˜râ€™ re-executes the last command (see Aliases).

     If the first form is used, the return value is zero unless an
     invalid option is encountered or FIRST or LAST specify history
     lines out of range.  When editing and re-executing a file of
     commands, the return value is the value of the last command
     executed or failure if an error occurs with the temporary file.  If
     the second form is used, the return status is that of the
     re-executed command, unless COMMAND does not specify a valid
     history entry, in which case â€˜fcâ€™ returns a non-zero status.

â€˜historyâ€™
          history [N]
          history -c
          history -d OFFSET
          history -d START-END
          history [-anrw] [FILENAME]
          history -ps ARG

     With no options, display the history list with numbers.  Entries
     prefixed with a â€˜*â€™ have been modified.  An argument of N lists
     only the last N entries.  If the shell variable â€˜HISTTIMEFORMATâ€™ is
     set and not null, it is used as a format string for â€˜strftimeâ€™(3)
     to display the time stamp associated with each displayed history
     entry.  If â€˜historyâ€™ uses â€˜HISTTIMEFORMATâ€™, it does not print an
     intervening space between the formatted time stamp and the history
     entry.

     Options, if supplied, have the following meanings:

     â€˜-câ€™
          Clear the history list.  This may be combined with the other
          options to replace the history list.

     â€˜-d OFFSETâ€™
          Delete the history entry at position OFFSET.  If OFFSET is
          positive, it should be specified as it appears when the
          history is displayed.  If OFFSET is negative, it is
          interpreted as relative to one greater than the last history
          position, so negative indices count back from the end of the
          history, and an index of â€˜-1â€™ refers to the current â€˜history
          -dâ€™ command.

     â€˜-d START-ENDâ€™
          Delete the range of history entries between positions START
          and END, inclusive.  Positive and negative values for START
          and END are interpreted as described above.

     â€˜-aâ€™
          Append the "new" history lines to the history file.  These are
          history lines entered since the beginning of the current Bash
          session, but not already appended to the history file.

     â€˜-nâ€™
          Read the history lines not already read from the history file
          and add them to the current history list.  These are lines
          appended to the history file since the beginning of the
          current Bash session.

     â€˜-râ€™
          Read the history file and append its contents to the history
          list.

     â€˜-wâ€™
          Write the current history list to the history file,
          overwriting the history file.

     â€˜-pâ€™
          Perform history substitution on the ARGs and display the
          result on the standard output, without storing the results in
          the history list.

     â€˜-sâ€™
          Add the ARGs to the end of the history list as a single entry.
          The last command in the history list is removed before adding
          the ARGs.

     If a FILENAME argument is supplied with any of the â€˜-wâ€™, â€˜-râ€™,
     â€˜-aâ€™, or â€˜-nâ€™ options, Bash uses FILENAME as the history file.  If
     not, it uses the value of the â€˜HISTFILEâ€™ variable.  If â€˜HISTFILEâ€™
     is unset or null, these options have no effect.

     If the â€˜HISTTIMEFORMATâ€™ variable is set, â€˜historyâ€™ writes the time
     stamp information associated with each history entry to the history
     file, marked with the history comment character as described above.
     When the history file is read, lines beginning with the history
     comment character followed immediately by a digit are interpreted
     as timestamps for the following history entry.

     The return value is 0 unless an invalid option is encountered, an
     error occurs while reading or writing the history file, an invalid
     OFFSET or range is supplied as an argument to â€˜-dâ€™, or the history
     expansion supplied as an argument to â€˜-pâ€™ fails.

9.3 History Expansion
=====================

The shell provides a history expansion feature that is similar to the
history expansion provided by â€˜cshâ€™ (also referred to as history
substitution where appropriate).  This section describes the syntax used
to manipulate the history information.

   History expansion is enabled by default for interactive shells, and
can be disabled using the â€˜+Hâ€™ option to the â€˜setâ€™ builtin command (see
The Set Builtin).  Non-interactive shells do not perform history
expansion by default, but it can be enabled with â€˜set -Hâ€™.

   History expansions introduce words from the history list into the
input stream, making it easy to repeat commands, insert the arguments to
a previous command into the current input line, or fix errors in
previous commands quickly.

   History expansion is performed immediately after a complete line is
read, before the shell breaks it into words, and is performed on each
line individually.  Bash attempts to inform the history expansion
functions about quoting still in effect from previous lines.

   History expansion takes place in two parts.  The first is to
determine which entry from the history list should be used during
substitution.  The second is to select portions of that entry to include
into the current one.

   The entry selected from the history is called the â€œeventâ€, and the
portions of that entry that are acted upon are â€œwordsâ€.  Various
â€œmodifiersâ€ are available to manipulate the selected words.  The entry
is split into words in the same fashion that Bash does when reading
input, so that several words surrounded by quotes are considered one
word.  The â€œevent designatorâ€ selects the event, the optional â€œword
designatorâ€ selects words from the event, and various optional
â€œmodifiersâ€ are available to manipulate the selected words.

   History expansions are introduced by the appearance of the history
expansion character, which is â€˜!â€™ by default.  History expansions may
appear anywhere in the input, but do not nest.

   History expansion implements shell-like quoting conventions: a
backslash can be used to remove the special handling for the next
character; single quotes enclose verbatim sequences of characters, and
can be used to inhibit history expansion; and characters enclosed within
double quotes may be subject to history expansion, since backslash can
escape the history expansion character, but single quotes may not, since
they are not treated specially within double quotes.

   When using the shell, only â€˜\â€™ and â€˜'â€™ may be used to escape the
history expansion character, but the history expansion character is also
treated as quoted if it immediately precedes the closing double quote in
a double-quoted string.

   Several characters inhibit history expansion if found immediately
following the history expansion character, even if it is unquoted:
space, tab, newline, carriage return, â€˜=â€™, and the other shell
metacharacters.

   There is a special abbreviation for substitution, active when the
QUICK SUBSTITUTION character (described above under â€˜histcharsâ€™) is the
first character on the line.  It selects the previous history list
entry, using an event designator equivalent to â€˜!!â€™, and substitutes one
string for another in that entry.  It is described below (see Event
Designators).  This is the only history expansion that does not begin
with the history expansion character.

   Several shell options settable with the â€˜shoptâ€™ builtin (see The
Shopt Builtin) modify history expansion behavior If the â€˜histverifyâ€™
shell option is enabled, and Readline is being used, history
substitutions are not immediately passed to the shell parser.  Instead,
the expanded line is reloaded into the Readline editing buffer for
further modification.  If Readline is being used, and the â€˜histreeditâ€™
shell option is enabled, a failed history expansion is reloaded into the
Readline editing buffer for correction.

   The â€˜-pâ€™ option to the â€˜historyâ€™ builtin command shows what a history
expansion will do before using it.  The â€˜-sâ€™ option to the â€˜historyâ€™
builtin may be used to add commands to the end of the history list
without actually executing them, so that they are available for
subsequent recall.  This is most useful in conjunction with Readline.

   The shell allows control of the various characters used by the
history expansion mechanism with the â€˜histcharsâ€™ variable, as explained
above (see Bash Variables).  The shell uses the history comment
character to mark history timestamps when writing the history file.

9.3.1 Event Designators
-----------------------

An event designator is a reference to an entry in the history list.  The
event designator consists of the portion of the word beginning with the
history expansion character, and ending with the word designator if one
is present, or the end of the word.  Unless the reference is absolute,
events are relative to the current position in the history list.

â€˜!â€™
     Start a history substitution, except when followed by a space, tab,
     the end of the line, â€˜=â€™, or the rest of the shell metacharacters
     defined above (see Definitions).

â€˜!Nâ€™
     Refer to history list entry N.

â€˜!-Nâ€™
     Refer to the history entry minus N.

â€˜!!â€™
     Refer to the previous entry.  This is a synonym for â€˜!-1â€™.

â€˜!STRINGâ€™
     Refer to the most recent command preceding the current position in
     the history list starting with STRING.

â€˜!?STRING[?]â€™
     Refer to the most recent command preceding the current position in
     the history list containing STRING.  The trailing â€˜?â€™ may be
     omitted if the STRING is followed immediately by a newline.  If
     STRING is missing, this uses the string from the most recent
     search; it is an error if there is no previous search string.

â€˜^STRING1^STRING2^â€™
     Quick Substitution.  Repeat the last command, replacing STRING1
     with STRING2.  Equivalent to â€˜!!:s^STRING1^STRING2^â€™.

â€˜!#â€™
     The entire command line typed so far.

9.3.2 Word Designators
----------------------

Word designators are used to select desired words from the event.  They
are optional; if the word designator isn't supplied, the history
expansion uses the entire event.  A â€˜:â€™ separates the event
specification from the word designator.  It may be omitted if the word
designator begins with a â€˜^â€™, â€˜$â€™, â€˜*â€™, â€˜-â€™, or â€˜%â€™.  Words are numbered
from the beginning of the line, with the first word being denoted by 0
(zero).  That first word is usually the command word, and the arguments
begin with the second word.  Words are inserted into the current line
separated by single spaces.

   For example,

â€˜!!â€™
     designates the preceding command.  When you type this, the
     preceding command is repeated in toto.

â€˜!!:$â€™
     designates the last word of the preceding command.  This may be
     shortened to â€˜!$â€™.

â€˜!fi:2â€™
     designates the second argument of the most recent command starting
     with the letters â€˜fiâ€™.

   Here are the word designators:

â€˜0 (zero)â€™
     The â€˜0â€™th word.  For the shell, and many other, applications, this
     is the command word.

â€˜Nâ€™
     The Nth word.

â€˜^â€™
     The first argument: word 1.

â€˜$â€™
     The last word.  This is usually the last argument, but expands to
     the zeroth word if there is only one word in the line.

â€˜%â€™
     The first word matched by the most recent â€˜?STRING?â€™ search, if the
     search string begins with a character that is part of a word.  By
     default, searches begin at the end of each line and proceed to the
     beginning, so the first word matched is the one closest to the end
     of the line.

â€˜X-Yâ€™
     A range of words; â€˜-Yâ€™ abbreviates â€˜0-Yâ€™.

â€˜*â€™
     All of the words, except the â€˜0â€™th.  This is a synonym for â€˜1-$â€™.
     It is not an error to use â€˜*â€™ if there is just one word in the
     event; it expands to the empty string in that case.

â€˜X*â€™
     Abbreviates â€˜X-$â€™.

â€˜X-â€™
     Abbreviates â€˜X-$â€™ like â€˜X*â€™, but omits the last word.  If â€˜xâ€™ is
     missing, it defaults to 0.

   If a word designator is supplied without an event specification, the
previous command is used as the event, equivalent to â€˜!!â€™.

9.3.3 Modifiers
---------------

After the optional word designator, you can add a sequence of one or
more of the following modifiers, each preceded by a â€˜:â€™.  These modify,
or edit, the word or words selected from the history event.

â€˜hâ€™
     Remove a trailing filename component, leaving only the head.

â€˜tâ€™
     Remove all leading filename components, leaving the tail.

â€˜râ€™
     Remove a trailing suffix of the form â€˜.SUFFIXâ€™, leaving the
     basename.

â€˜eâ€™
     Remove all but the trailing suffix.

â€˜pâ€™
     Print the new command but do not execute it.

â€˜qâ€™
     Quote the substituted words, escaping further substitutions.

â€˜xâ€™
     Quote the substituted words as with â€˜qâ€™, but break into words at
     spaces, tabs, and newlines.  The â€˜qâ€™ and â€˜xâ€™ modifiers are mutually
     exclusive; expansion uses the last one supplied.

â€˜s/OLD/NEW/â€™
     Substitute NEW for the first occurrence of OLD in the event line.
     Any character may be used as the delimiter in place of â€˜/â€™.  The
     delimiter may be quoted in OLD and NEW with a single backslash.  If
     â€˜&â€™ appears in NEW, it is replaced with OLD.  A single backslash
     quotes the â€˜&â€™ in OLD and NEW.  If OLD is null, it is set to the
     last OLD substituted, or, if no previous history substitutions took
     place, the last STRING in a !?STRINGâ€˜[?]â€™ search.  If NEW is null,
     each matching OLD is deleted.  The final delimiter is optional if
     it is the last character on the input line.

â€˜&â€™
     Repeat the previous substitution.

â€˜gâ€™
â€˜aâ€™
     Cause changes to be applied over the entire event line.  This is
     used in conjunction with â€˜sâ€™, as in â€˜gs/OLD/NEW/â€™, or with â€˜&â€™.

â€˜Gâ€™
     Apply the following â€˜sâ€™ or â€˜&â€™ modifier once to each word in the
     event.

10 Installing Bash
******************

This chapter provides basic instructions for installing Bash on the
various supported platforms.  The distribution supports the GNU
operating systems, nearly every version of Unix, and several non-Unix
systems such as BeOS and Interix.  Other independent ports exist for
Windows platforms.

10.1 Basic Installation
=======================

These are installation instructions for Bash.

   The simplest way to compile Bash is:

  1. â€˜cdâ€™ to the directory containing the source code and type
     â€˜./configureâ€™ to configure Bash for your system.  If you're using
     â€˜cshâ€™ on an old version of System V, you might need to type â€˜sh
     ./configureâ€™ instead to prevent â€˜cshâ€™ from trying to execute
     â€˜configureâ€™ itself.

     Running â€˜configureâ€™ takes some time.  While running, it prints
     messages telling which features it is checking for.

  2. Type â€˜makeâ€™ to compile Bash and build the â€˜bashbugâ€™ bug reporting
     script.

  3. Optionally, type â€˜make testsâ€™ to run the Bash test suite.

  4. Type â€˜make installâ€™ to install â€˜bashâ€™ and â€˜bashbugâ€™.  This will
     also install the manual pages and Info file, message translation
     files, some supplemental documentation, a number of example
     loadable builtin commands, and a set of header files for developing
     loadable builtins.  You may need additional privileges to install
     â€˜bashâ€™ to your desired destination, which may require â€˜sudo make
     installâ€™.  More information about controlling the locations where
     â€˜bashâ€™ and other files are installed is below (see Installation
     Names).

   The â€˜configureâ€™ shell script attempts to guess correct values for
various system-dependent variables used during compilation.  It uses
those values to create a â€˜Makefileâ€™ in each directory of the package
(the top directory, the â€˜builtinsâ€™, â€˜docâ€™, â€˜poâ€™, and â€˜supportâ€™
directories, each directory under â€˜libâ€™, and several others).  It also
creates a â€˜config.hâ€™ file containing system-dependent definitions.
Finally, it creates a shell script named â€˜config.statusâ€™ that you can
run in the future to recreate the current configuration, a file
â€˜config.cacheâ€™ that saves the results of its tests to speed up
reconfiguring, and a file â€˜config.logâ€™ containing compiler output
(useful mainly for debugging â€˜configureâ€™).  If at some point
â€˜config.cacheâ€™ contains results you don't want to keep, you may remove
or edit it.

   To find out more about the options and arguments that the â€˜configureâ€™
script understands, type

     bash-4.2$ ./configure --help

at the Bash prompt in your Bash source directory.

   If you want to build Bash in a directory separate from the source
directory - to build for multiple architectures, for example - just use
the full path to the configure script.  The following commands will
build Bash in a directory under â€˜/usr/local/buildâ€™ from the source code
in â€˜/usr/local/src/bash-4.4â€™:

     mkdir /usr/local/build/bash-4.4
     cd /usr/local/build/bash-4.4
     bash /usr/local/src/bash-4.4/configure
     make

   See Compiling For Multiple Architectures for more information about
building in a directory separate from the source.

   If you need to do unusual things to compile Bash, please try to
figure out how â€˜configureâ€™ could check whether or not to do them, and
mail diffs or instructions to <bash-maintainers@gnu.org> so they can be
considered for the next release.

   The file â€˜configure.acâ€™ is used to create â€˜configureâ€™ by a program
called Autoconf.  You only need â€˜configure.acâ€™ if you want to change it
or regenerate â€˜configureâ€™ using a newer version of Autoconf.  If you do
this, make sure you are using Autoconf version 2.69 or newer.

   You can remove the program binaries and object files from the source
code directory by typing â€˜make cleanâ€™.  To also remove the files that
â€˜configureâ€™ created (so you can compile Bash for a different kind of
computer), type â€˜make distcleanâ€™.

10.2 Compilers and Options
==========================

Some systems require unusual options for compilation or linking that the
â€˜configureâ€™ script does not know about.  You can give â€˜configureâ€™
initial values for variables by setting them in the environment.  Using
a Bourne-compatible shell, you can do that on the command line like
this:

     CC=c89 CFLAGS=-O2 LIBS=-lposix ./configure

   On systems that have the â€˜envâ€™ program, you can do it like this:

     env CPPFLAGS=-I/usr/local/include LDFLAGS=-s ./configure

   The configuration process uses GCC to build Bash if it is available.

10.3 Compiling For Multiple Architectures
=========================================

You can compile Bash for more than one kind of computer at the same
time, by placing the object files for each architecture in their own
directory.  To do this, you must use a version of â€˜makeâ€™ that supports
the â€˜VPATHâ€™ variable, such as GNU â€˜makeâ€™.  â€˜cdâ€™ to the directory where
you want the object files and executables to go and run the â€˜configureâ€™
script from the source directory (see Basic Installation).  You may need
to supply the â€˜--srcdir=PATHâ€™ argument to tell â€˜configureâ€™ where the
source files are.  â€˜configureâ€™ automatically checks for the source code
in the directory that â€˜configureâ€™ is in and in â€˜..â€™.

   If you have to use a â€˜makeâ€™ that does not support the â€˜VPATHâ€™
variable, you can compile Bash for one architecture at a time in the
source code directory.  After you have installed Bash for one
architecture, use â€˜make distcleanâ€™ before reconfiguring for another
architecture.

   Alternatively, if your system supports symbolic links, you can use
the â€˜support/mkcloneâ€™ script to create a build tree which has symbolic
links back to each file in the source directory.  Here's an example that
creates a build directory in the current directory from a source
directory â€˜/usr/gnu/src/bash-2.0â€™:

     bash /usr/gnu/src/bash-2.0/support/mkclone -s /usr/gnu/src/bash-2.0 .

The â€˜mkcloneâ€™ script requires Bash, so you must have already built Bash
for at least one architecture before you can create build directories
for other architectures.

10.4 Installation Names
=======================

By default, â€˜make installâ€™ will install into â€˜/usr/local/binâ€™,
â€˜/usr/local/manâ€™, etc.; that is, the â€œinstallation prefixâ€ defaults to
â€˜/usr/localâ€™.  You can specify an installation prefix other than
â€˜/usr/localâ€™ by giving â€˜configureâ€™ the option â€˜--prefix=PATHâ€™, or by
specifying a value for the â€˜prefixâ€™ â€˜makeâ€™ variable when running â€˜make
installâ€™ (e.g., â€˜make install prefix=PATHâ€™).  The â€˜prefixâ€™ variable
provides a default for â€˜exec_prefixâ€™ and other variables used when
installing Bash.

   You can specify separate installation prefixes for
architecture-specific files and architecture-independent files.  If you
give â€˜configureâ€™ the option â€˜--exec-prefix=PATHâ€™, â€˜make installâ€™ will
use PATH as the prefix for installing programs and libraries.
Documentation and other data files will still use the regular prefix.

   If you would like to change the installation locations for a single
run, you can specify these variables as arguments to â€˜makeâ€™: â€˜make
install exec_prefix=/â€™ will install â€˜bashâ€™ and â€˜bashbugâ€™ into â€˜/binâ€™
instead of the default â€˜/usr/local/binâ€™.

   If you want to see the files Bash will install and where it will
install them without changing anything on your system, specify the
variable â€˜DESTDIRâ€™ as an argument to â€˜makeâ€™.  Its value should be the
absolute directory path you'd like to use as the root of your sample
installation tree.  For example,

     mkdir /fs1/bash-install
     make install DESTDIR=/fs1/bash-install

will install â€˜bashâ€™ into â€˜/fs1/bash-install/usr/local/bin/bashâ€™, the
documentation into directories within
â€˜/fs1/bash-install/usr/local/shareâ€™, the example loadable builtins into
â€˜/fs1/bash-install/usr/local/lib/bashâ€™, and so on.  You can use the
usual â€˜exec_prefixâ€™ and â€˜prefixâ€™ variables to alter the directory paths
beneath the value of â€˜DESTDIRâ€™.

   The GNU Makefile standards provide a more complete description of
these variables and their effects.

10.5 Specifying the System Type
===============================

There may be some features â€˜configureâ€™ can not figure out automatically,
but needs to determine by the type of host Bash will run on.  Usually
â€˜configureâ€™ can figure that out, but if it prints a message saying it
can not guess the host type, give it the â€˜--host=TYPEâ€™ option.  â€˜TYPEâ€™
can either be a short name for the system type, such as â€˜sun4â€™, or a
canonical name with three fields: â€˜CPU-COMPANY-SYSTEMâ€™ (e.g.,
â€˜i386-unknown-freebsd4.2â€™).

   See the file â€˜support/config.subâ€™ for the possible values of each
field.

10.6 Sharing Defaults
=====================

If you want to set default values for â€˜configureâ€™ scripts to share, you
can create a site shell script called â€˜config.siteâ€™ that gives default
values for variables like â€˜CCâ€™, â€˜cache_fileâ€™, and â€˜prefixâ€™.  â€˜configureâ€™
looks for â€˜PREFIX/share/config.siteâ€™ if it exists, then
â€˜PREFIX/etc/config.siteâ€™ if it exists.  Or, you can set the
â€˜CONFIG_SITEâ€™ environment variable to the location of the site script.
A warning: the Bash â€˜configureâ€™ looks for a site script, but not all
â€˜configureâ€™ scripts do.

10.7 Operation Controls
=======================

â€˜configureâ€™ recognizes the following options to control how it operates.

â€˜--cache-file=FILEâ€™
     Use and save the results of the tests in FILE instead of
     â€˜./config.cacheâ€™.  Set FILE to â€˜/dev/nullâ€™ to disable caching, for
     debugging â€˜configureâ€™.

â€˜--helpâ€™
     Print a summary of the options to â€˜configureâ€™, and exit.

â€˜--quietâ€™
â€˜--silentâ€™
â€˜-qâ€™
     Do not print messages saying which checks are being made.

â€˜--srcdir=DIRâ€™
     Look for the Bash source code in directory DIR.  Usually
     â€˜configureâ€™ can determine that directory automatically.

â€˜--versionâ€™
     Print the version of Autoconf used to generate the â€˜configureâ€™
     script, and exit.

   â€˜configureâ€™ also accepts some other, not widely used, boilerplate
options.  â€˜configure --helpâ€™ prints the complete list.

10.8 Optional Features
======================

The Bash â€˜configureâ€™ has a number of â€˜--enable-FEATUREâ€™ options, where
FEATURE indicates an optional part of Bash.  There are also several
â€˜--with-PACKAGEâ€™ options, where PACKAGE is something like â€˜bash-mallocâ€™
or â€˜afsâ€™.  To turn off the default use of a package, use
â€˜--without-PACKAGEâ€™.  To configure Bash without a feature that is
enabled by default, use â€˜--disable-FEATUREâ€™.

   Here is a complete list of the â€˜--enable-â€™ and â€˜--with-â€™ options that
the Bash â€˜configureâ€™ recognizes.

â€˜--with-afsâ€™
     Define if you are using the Andrew File System from Transarc.

â€˜--with-bash-mallocâ€™
     Use the Bash version of â€˜mallocâ€™ in the directory â€˜lib/mallocâ€™.
     This is not the same â€˜mallocâ€™ that appears in GNU libc, but a
     custom version originally derived from the 4.2 BSD â€˜mallocâ€™.  This
     â€˜mallocâ€™ is very fast, but wastes some space on each allocation,
     though it uses several techniques to minimize the waste.  This
     option is enabled by default.  The â€˜NOTESâ€™ file contains a list of
     systems for which this should be turned off, and â€˜configureâ€™
     disables this option automatically for a number of systems.

â€˜--with-cursesâ€™
     Use the curses library instead of the termcap library.  â€˜configureâ€™
     usually chooses this automatically, since most systems include the
     termcap functions in the curses library.

â€˜--with-gnu-mallocâ€™
     A synonym for â€˜--with-bash-mallocâ€™.

â€˜--with-installed-readline[=PREFIX]â€™
     Define this to make Bash link with a locally-installed version of
     Readline rather than the version in â€˜lib/readlineâ€™.  This works
     only with Readline 5.0 and later versions.  If PREFIX is â€˜yesâ€™ or
     not supplied, â€˜configureâ€™ uses the values of the make variables
     â€˜includedirâ€™ and â€˜libdirâ€™, which are subdirectories of â€˜prefixâ€™ by
     default, to find the installed version of Readline if it is not in
     the standard system include and library directories.  If PREFIX is
     â€˜noâ€™, Bash links with the version in â€˜lib/readlineâ€™.  If PREFIX is
     set to any other value, â€˜configureâ€™ treats it as a directory
     pathname and looks for the installed version of Readline in
     subdirectories of that directory (include files in PREFIX/â€˜includeâ€™
     and the library in PREFIX/â€˜libâ€™).  The Bash default is to link with
     a static library built in the â€˜lib/readlineâ€™ subdirectory of the
     build directory.

â€˜--with-libintl-prefix[=PREFIX]â€™
     Define this to make Bash link with a locally-installed version of
     the libintl library instead of the version in â€˜lib/intlâ€™.

â€˜--with-libiconv-prefix[=PREFIX]â€™
     Define this to make Bash look for libiconv in PREFIX instead of the
     standard system locations.  The Bash distribution does not include
     this library.

â€˜--enable-minimal-configâ€™
     This produces a shell with minimal features, closer to the
     historical Bourne shell.

   There are several â€˜--enable-â€™ options that alter how Bash is
compiled, linked, and installed, rather than changing run-time features.

â€˜--enable-largefileâ€™
     Enable support for large files
     (http://www.unix.org/version2/whatsnew/lfs20mar.html) if the
     operating system requires special compiler options to build
     programs which can access large files.  This is enabled by default,
     if the operating system provides large file support.

â€˜--enable-profilingâ€™
     This builds a Bash binary that produces profiling information to be
     processed by â€˜gprofâ€™ each time it is executed.

â€˜--enable-separate-helpfilesâ€™
     Use external files for the documentation displayed by the â€˜helpâ€™
     builtin instead of storing the text internally.

â€˜--enable-static-linkâ€™
     This causes Bash to be linked statically, if â€˜gccâ€™ is being used.
     This could be used to build a version to use as root's shell.

   The â€˜minimal-configâ€™ option can be used to disable all of the
following options, but it is processed first, so individual options may
be enabled using â€˜enable-FEATUREâ€™.

   All of the following options except for â€˜alt-array-implementationâ€™,
â€˜disabled-builtinsâ€™, â€˜direxpand-defaultâ€™, â€˜strict-posix-defaultâ€™, and
â€˜xpg-echo-defaultâ€™ are enabled by default, unless the operating system
does not provide the necessary support.

â€˜--enable-aliasâ€™
     Allow alias expansion and include the â€˜aliasâ€™ and â€˜unaliasâ€™
     builtins (see Aliases).

â€˜--enable-alt-array-implementationâ€™
     This builds Bash using an alternate implementation of arrays (see
     Arrays) that provides faster access at the expense of using more
     memory (sometimes many times more, depending on how sparse an array
     is).

â€˜--enable-arith-for-commandâ€™
     Include support for the alternate form of the â€˜forâ€™ command that
     behaves like the C language â€˜forâ€™ statement (see Looping
     Constructs).

â€˜--enable-array-variablesâ€™
     Include support for one-dimensional array shell variables (see
     Arrays).

â€˜--enable-bang-historyâ€™
     Include support for â€˜cshâ€™-like history substitution (see History
     Interaction).

â€˜--enable-bash-source-fullpath-defaultâ€™
     Set the default value of the â€˜bash_source_fullpathâ€™ shell option
     described above under The Shopt Builtin to be enabled.  This
     controls how filenames are assigned to the â€˜BASH_SOURCEâ€™ array
     variable.

â€˜--enable-brace-expansionâ€™
     Include â€˜cshâ€™-like brace expansion ( â€˜b{a,b}câ€™ â†¦ â€˜bac bbcâ€™ ).  See
     Brace Expansion, for a complete description.

â€˜--enable-casemod-attributesâ€™
     Include support for case-modifying attributes in the â€˜declareâ€™
     builtin and assignment statements.  Variables with the â€˜uppercaseâ€™
     attribute, for example, will have their values converted to
     uppercase upon assignment.

â€˜--enable-casemod-expansionâ€™
     Include support for case-modifying word expansions.

â€˜--enable-command-timingâ€™
     Include support for recognizing â€˜timeâ€™ as a reserved word and for
     displaying timing statistics for the pipeline following â€˜timeâ€™ (see
     Pipelines).  This allows timing pipelines, shell compound commands,
     shell builtins, and shell functions, which an external command
     cannot do easily.

â€˜--enable-cond-commandâ€™
     Include support for the â€˜[[â€™ conditional command.  (see Conditional
     Constructs).

â€˜--enable-cond-regexpâ€™
     Include support for matching POSIX regular expressions using the
     â€˜=~â€™ binary operator in the â€˜[[â€™ conditional command.  (see
     Conditional Constructs).

â€˜--enable-coprocessesâ€™
     Include support for coprocesses and the â€˜coprocâ€™ reserved word (see
     Pipelines).

â€˜--enable-debuggerâ€™
     Include support for the Bash debugger (distributed separately).

â€˜--enable-dev-fd-stat-brokenâ€™
     If calling â€˜statâ€™ on /dev/fd/N returns different results than
     calling â€˜fstatâ€™ on file descriptor N, supply this option to enable
     a workaround.  This has implications for conditional commands that
     test file attributes.

â€˜--enable-direxpand-defaultâ€™
     Cause the â€˜direxpandâ€™ shell option (see The Shopt Builtin) to be
     enabled by default when the shell starts.  It is normally disabled
     by default.

â€˜--enable-directory-stackâ€™
     Include support for a â€˜cshâ€™-like directory stack and the â€˜pushdâ€™,
     â€˜popdâ€™, and â€˜dirsâ€™ builtins (see The Directory Stack).

â€˜--enable-disabled-builtinsâ€™
     Allow builtin commands to be invoked via â€˜builtin xxxâ€™ even after
     â€˜xxxâ€™ has been disabled using â€˜enable -n xxxâ€™.  See Bash Builtins,
     for details of the â€˜builtinâ€™ and â€˜enableâ€™ builtin commands.

â€˜--enable-dparen-arithmeticâ€™
     Include support for the â€˜((...))â€™ command (see Conditional
     Constructs).

â€˜--enable-extended-globâ€™
     Include support for the extended pattern matching features
     described above under Pattern Matching.

â€˜--enable-extended-glob-defaultâ€™
     Set the default value of the â€˜extglobâ€™ shell option described above
     under The Shopt Builtin to be enabled.

â€˜--enable-function-importâ€™
     Include support for importing function definitions exported by
     another instance of the shell from the environment.  This option is
     enabled by default.

â€˜--enable-glob-asciiranges-defaultâ€™
     Set the default value of the â€˜globasciirangesâ€™ shell option
     described above under The Shopt Builtin to be enabled.  This
     controls the behavior of character ranges when used in pattern
     matching bracket expressions.

â€˜--enable-help-builtinâ€™
     Include the â€˜helpâ€™ builtin, which displays help on shell builtins
     and variables (see Bash Builtins).

â€˜--enable-historyâ€™
     Include command history and the â€˜fcâ€™ and â€˜historyâ€™ builtin commands
     (see Bash History Facilities).

â€˜--enable-job-controlâ€™
     This enables the job control features (see Job Control), if the
     operating system supports them.

â€˜--enable-multibyteâ€™
     This enables support for multibyte characters if the operating
     system provides the necessary support.

â€˜--enable-net-redirectionsâ€™
     This enables the special handling of filenames of the form
     â€˜/dev/tcp/HOST/PORTâ€™ and â€˜/dev/udp/HOST/PORTâ€™ when used in
     redirections (see Redirections).

â€˜--enable-process-substitutionâ€™
     This enables process substitution (see Process Substitution) if the
     operating system provides the necessary support.

â€˜--enable-progcompâ€™
     Enable the programmable completion facilities (see Programmable
     Completion).  If Readline is not enabled, this option has no
     effect.

â€˜--enable-prompt-string-decodingâ€™
     Turn on the interpretation of a number of backslash-escaped
     characters in the â€˜$PS0â€™, â€˜$PS1â€™, â€˜$PS2â€™, and â€˜$PS4â€™ prompt
     strings.  See Controlling the Prompt, for a complete list of prompt
     string escape sequences.

â€˜--enable-readlineâ€™
     Include support for command-line editing and history with the Bash
     version of the Readline library (see Command Line Editing).

â€˜--enable-restrictedâ€™
     Include support for a â€œrestricted shellâ€.  If this is enabled, Bash
     enters a restricted mode when called as â€˜rbashâ€™.  See The
     Restricted Shell, for a description of restricted mode.

â€˜--enable-selectâ€™
     Include the â€˜selectâ€™ compound command, which allows generation of
     simple menus (see Conditional Constructs).

â€˜--enable-single-help-stringsâ€™
     Store the text displayed by the â€˜helpâ€™ builtin as a single string
     for each help topic.  This aids in translating the text to
     different languages.  You may need to disable this if your compiler
     cannot handle very long string literals.

â€˜--enable-strict-posix-defaultâ€™
     Make Bash POSIX-conformant by default (see Bash POSIX Mode).

â€˜--enable-translatable-stringsâ€™
     Enable support for â€˜$"STRING"â€™ translatable strings (see Locale
     Translation).

â€˜--enable-usg-echo-defaultâ€™
     A synonym for â€˜--enable-xpg-echo-defaultâ€™.

â€˜--enable-xpg-echo-defaultâ€™
     Make the â€˜echoâ€™ builtin expand backslash-escaped characters by
     default, without requiring the â€˜-eâ€™ option.  This sets the default
     value of the â€˜xpg_echoâ€™ shell option to â€˜onâ€™, which makes the Bash
     â€˜echoâ€™ behave more like the version specified in the Single Unix
     Specification, version 3.  See Bash Builtins, for a description of
     the escape sequences that â€˜echoâ€™ recognizes.

   The file â€˜config-top.hâ€™ contains C Preprocessor â€˜#defineâ€™ statements
for options which are not settable from â€˜configureâ€™.  Some of these are
not meant to be changed; beware of the consequences if you do.  Read the
comments associated with each definition for more information about its
effect.

Appendix A Reporting Bugs
*************************

Please report all bugs you find in Bash.  But first, you should make
sure that it really is a bug, and that it appears in the latest version
of Bash.  The latest released version of Bash is always available for
FTP from <ftp://ftp.gnu.org/pub/gnu/bash/> and from
<http://git.savannah.gnu.org/cgit/bash.git/snapshot/bash-master.tar.gz>.

   Once you have determined that a bug actually exists, use the
â€˜bashbugâ€™ command to submit a bug report or use the form at the Bash
project page (https://savannah.gnu.org/projects/bash/).  If you have a
fix, you are encouraged to submit that as well!  Suggestions and
'philosophical' bug reports may be mailed to <bug-bash@gnu.org> or
<help-bash@gnu.org>.

   All bug reports should include:
   â€¢ The version number of Bash.
   â€¢ The hardware and operating system.
   â€¢ The compiler used to compile Bash.
   â€¢ A description of the bug behavior.
   â€¢ A short script or 'recipe' which exercises the bug and may be used
     to reproduce it.

â€˜bashbugâ€™ inserts the first three items automatically into the template
it provides for filing a bug report.

   Please send all reports concerning this manual to <bug-bash@gnu.org>.

Appendix B Major Differences From The Bourne Shell
**************************************************

Bash implements essentially the same grammar, parameter and variable
expansion, redirection, and quoting as the Bourne Shell.  Bash uses the
POSIX standard as the specification of how these features are to be
implemented and how they should behave.  There are some differences
between the traditional Bourne shell and Bash; this section quickly
details the differences of significance.  A number of these differences
are explained in greater depth in previous sections.  This section uses
the version of â€˜shâ€™ included in SVR4.2 (the last version of the
historical Bourne shell) as the baseline reference.

   â€¢ Bash is POSIX-conformant, even where the POSIX specification
     differs from traditional â€˜shâ€™ behavior (see Bash POSIX Mode).

   â€¢ Bash has multi-character invocation options (see Invoking Bash).

   â€¢ The Bash restricted mode is more useful (see The Restricted Shell);
     the SVR4.2 shell restricted mode is too limited.

   â€¢ Bash has command-line editing (see Command Line Editing) and the
     â€˜bindâ€™ builtin.

   â€¢ Bash provides a programmable word completion mechanism (see
     Programmable Completion), and builtin commands â€˜completeâ€™,
     â€˜compgenâ€™, and â€˜compoptâ€™, to manipulate it.

   â€¢ Bash decodes a number of backslash-escape sequences in the prompt
     string variables (â€˜PS0â€™, â€˜PS1â€™, â€˜PS2â€™, and â€˜PS4â€™) (see Controlling
     the Prompt).

   â€¢ Bash expands and displays the â€˜PS0â€™ prompt string variable.

   â€¢ Bash runs commands from the â€˜PROMPT_COMMANDâ€™ array variable before
     issuing each primary prompt.

   â€¢ Bash has command history (see Bash History Facilities) and the
     â€˜historyâ€™ and â€˜fcâ€™ builtins to manipulate it.  The Bash history
     list maintains timestamp information and uses the value of the
     â€˜HISTTIMEFORMATâ€™ variable to display it.

   â€¢ Bash implements â€˜cshâ€™-like history expansion (see History
     Interaction).

   â€¢ Bash supports the â€˜$'...'â€™ quoting syntax, which expands ANSI-C
     backslash-escaped characters in the text between the single quotes
     (see ANSI-C Quoting).

   â€¢ Bash supports the â€˜$"..."â€™ quoting syntax and performs
     locale-specific translation of the characters between the double
     quotes.  The â€˜-Dâ€™, â€˜--dump-stringsâ€™, and â€˜--dump-po-stringsâ€™
     invocation options list the translatable strings found in a script
     (see Locale Translation).

   â€¢ Bash includes brace expansion (see Brace Expansion) and tilde
     expansion (see Tilde Expansion).

   â€¢ Bash implements command aliases and the â€˜aliasâ€™ and â€˜unaliasâ€™
     builtins (see Aliases).

   â€¢ Bash implements the â€˜!â€™ reserved word to negate the return value of
     a pipeline (see Pipelines).  This is very useful when an â€˜ifâ€™
     statement needs to act only if a test fails.  The Bash â€˜-o
     pipefailâ€™ option to â€˜setâ€™ will cause a pipeline to return a failure
     status if any command fails (see The Set Builtin).

   â€¢ Bash has the â€˜timeâ€™ reserved word and command timing (see
     Pipelines).  The display of the timing statistics may be controlled
     with the â€˜TIMEFORMATâ€™ variable.

   â€¢ Bash provides coprocesses and the â€˜coprocâ€™ reserved word (see
     Coprocesses).

   â€¢ Bash implements the â€˜for (( EXPR1 ; EXPR2 ; EXPR3 ))â€™ arithmetic
     for command, similar to the C language (see Looping Constructs).

   â€¢ Bash includes the â€˜selectâ€™ compound command, which allows the
     generation of simple menus (see Conditional Constructs).

   â€¢ Bash includes the â€˜[[â€™ compound command, which makes conditional
     testing part of the shell grammar (see Conditional Constructs),
     including optional regular expression matching.

   â€¢ Bash provides optional case-insensitive matching for the â€˜caseâ€™ and
     â€˜[[â€™ constructs (see Conditional Constructs).

   â€¢ Bash provides additional â€˜caseâ€™ statement action list terminators:
     â€˜;&â€™ and â€˜;;&â€™ (see Conditional Constructs).

   â€¢ Bash provides shell arithmetic, the â€˜((â€™ compound command (see
     Conditional Constructs), the â€˜letâ€™ builtin, and arithmetic
     expansion (see Shell Arithmetic).

   â€¢ Bash has one-dimensional array variables (see Arrays), and the
     appropriate variable expansions and assignment syntax to use them.
     Several of the Bash builtins take options to act on arrays.  Bash
     provides a number of built-in array variables.

   â€¢ Variables present in the shell's initial environment are
     automatically exported to child processes (see Command Execution
     Environment).  The Bourne shell does not normally do this unless
     the variables are explicitly marked using the â€˜exportâ€™ command.

   â€¢ Bash can expand positional parameters beyond â€˜$9â€™ using â€˜${NUM}â€™
     (see Shell Parameter Expansion).

   â€¢ Bash supports the â€˜+=â€™ assignment operator, which appends to the
     value of the variable named on the left hand side (see Shell
     Parameters).

   â€¢ Bash includes the POSIX pattern removal â€˜%â€™, â€˜#â€™, â€˜%%â€™ and â€˜##â€™
     expansions to remove leading or trailing substrings from variable
     values (see Shell Parameter Expansion).

   â€¢ The expansion â€˜${#xx}â€™, which returns the length of â€˜${xx}â€™, is
     supported (see Shell Parameter Expansion).

   â€¢ The expansion â€˜${var:â€™OFFSETâ€˜[:â€™LENGTHâ€˜]}â€™, which expands to the
     substring of â€˜varâ€™'s value of length LENGTH, beginning at OFFSET,
     is present (see Shell Parameter Expansion).

   â€¢ The expansion â€˜${VAR/[/]â€™PATTERNâ€˜[/â€™REPLACEMENTâ€˜]}â€™, which matches
     PATTERN and replaces it with REPLACEMENT in the value of VAR, is
     available (see Shell Parameter Expansion), with a mechanism to use
     the matched text in REPLACEMENT.

   â€¢ The expansion â€˜${!PREFIX*}â€™ expansion, which expands to the names
     of all shell variables whose names begin with PREFIX, is available
     (see Shell Parameter Expansion).

   â€¢ Bash has indirect variable expansion using â€˜${!word}â€™ (see Shell
     Parameter Expansion) and implements the â€˜namerefâ€™ variable
     attribute for automatic indirect variable expansion.

   â€¢ Bash includes a set of parameter transformation word expansions of
     the form â€˜${var@X}â€™, where â€˜Xâ€™ specifies the transformation (see
     Shell Parameter Expansion).

   â€¢ The POSIX â€˜$()â€™ form of command substitution is implemented (see
     Command Substitution), and preferred to the Bourne shell's â€˜``â€™
     (which is also implemented for backwards compatibility).

   â€¢ Bash implements a variant of command substitution that runs the
     enclosed command in the current shell execution environment: â€˜${
     COMMAND;}â€™ or â€˜${|COMMAND;}â€™ (see Command Substitution).

   â€¢ Bash has process substitution (see Process Substitution).

   â€¢ Bash automatically assigns variables that provide information about
     the current user (â€˜UIDâ€™, â€˜EUIDâ€™, and â€˜GROUPSâ€™), the current host
     (â€˜HOSTTYPEâ€™, â€˜OSTYPEâ€™, â€˜MACHTYPEâ€™, and â€˜HOSTNAMEâ€™), and the
     instance of Bash that is running (â€˜BASHâ€™, â€˜BASH_VERSIONâ€™, and
     â€˜BASH_VERSINFOâ€™).  See Bash Variables, for details.

   â€¢ Bash uses many variables to provide functionality and customize
     shell behavior that the Bourne shell does not.  Examples include
     â€˜RANDOMâ€™, â€˜SRANDOMâ€™, â€˜EPOCHSECONDSâ€™, â€˜EPOCHREALTIMEâ€™, â€˜TIMEFORMATâ€™,
     â€˜BASHPIDâ€™, â€˜BASH_XTRACEFDâ€™, â€˜GLOBIGNOREâ€™, â€˜HISTIGNOREâ€™, and
     â€˜BASH_VERSIONâ€™.  See Bash Variables, for a complete list.

   â€¢ Bash uses the â€˜GLOBSORTâ€™ shell variable to control how to sort the
     results of filename expansion (see Filename Expansion).

   â€¢ Bash uses the â€˜IFSâ€™ variable to split only the results of
     expansion, not all words (see Word Splitting).  This closes a
     longstanding shell security hole.

   â€¢ The filename expansion bracket expression code uses â€˜!â€™ and â€˜^â€™ to
     negate the set of characters between the brackets (see Filename
     Expansion).  The Bourne shell uses only â€˜!â€™.

   â€¢ Bash implements the full set of POSIX filename expansion operators,
     including character classes, equivalence classes, and collating
     symbols (see Filename Expansion).

   â€¢ Bash implements extended pattern matching features when the
     â€˜extglobâ€™ shell option is enabled (see Pattern Matching).

   â€¢ The â€˜globstarâ€™ shell option extends filename expansion to
     recursively scan directories and subdirectories for matching
     filenames (see Pattern Matching).

   â€¢ It is possible to have a variable and a function with the same
     name; â€˜shâ€™ does not separate the two name spaces.

   â€¢ Bash functions are permitted to have local variables using the
     â€˜localâ€™ builtin, and thus users can write useful recursive
     functions (see Bash Builtins).

   â€¢ Bash performs filename expansion on filenames specified as operands
     to input and output redirection operators (see Redirections).

   â€¢ Bash contains the â€˜<>â€™ redirection operator, allowing a file to be
     opened for both reading and writing, and the â€˜&>â€™ redirection
     operator, for directing standard output and standard error to the
     same file (see Redirections).

   â€¢ Bash includes the â€˜<<<â€™ redirection operator, allowing a string to
     be used as the standard input to a command (see Redirections).

   â€¢ Bash implements the â€˜[n]<&WORDâ€™ and â€˜[n]>&WORDâ€™ redirection
     operators, which move one file descriptor to another.

   â€¢ Bash treats a number of filenames specially when they are used in
     redirection operators (see Redirections).

   â€¢ Bash provides the {VAR}<WORD capability to have the shell allocate
     file descriptors for redirections and assign them to VAR (see
     Redirections).  This works with multiple redirection operators.

   â€¢ Bash can open network connections to arbitrary machines and
     services with the redirection operators (see Redirections).

   â€¢ The â€˜noclobberâ€™ option is available to avoid overwriting existing
     files with output redirection (see The Set Builtin).  The â€˜>|â€™
     redirection operator may be used to override â€˜noclobberâ€™.

   â€¢ Variable assignments preceding commands affect only that command,
     even builtins and functions (see Environment).  In â€˜shâ€™, all
     variable assignments preceding commands are global unless the
     command is executed from the file system.

   â€¢ Bash includes a number of features to support a separate debugger
     for shell scripts: variables (â€˜BASH_ARGCâ€™, â€˜BASH_ARGVâ€™,
     â€˜BASH_LINENOâ€™, â€˜BASH_SOURCEâ€™), the â€˜DEBUGâ€™, â€˜RETURNâ€™, and â€˜ERRâ€™
     traps, â€˜declare -Fâ€™, and the â€˜callerâ€™ builtin.

   â€¢ Bash implements a â€˜cshâ€™-like directory stack, and provides the
     â€˜pushdâ€™, â€˜popdâ€™, and â€˜dirsâ€™ builtins to manipulate it (see The
     Directory Stack).  Bash also makes the directory stack visible as
     the value of the â€˜DIRSTACKâ€™ shell variable.

   â€¢ Bash allows a function to override a builtin with the same name,
     and provides access to that builtin's functionality within the
     function via the â€˜builtinâ€™ and â€˜commandâ€™ builtins (see Bash
     Builtins).

   â€¢ Bash includes the â€˜callerâ€™ builtin (see Bash Builtins), which
     displays the context of any active subroutine call (a shell
     function or a script executed with the â€˜.â€™ or â€˜sourceâ€™ builtins).
     This supports the Bash debugger.

   â€¢ The Bash â€˜cdâ€™ and â€˜pwdâ€™ builtins (see Bourne Shell Builtins) each
     take â€˜-Lâ€™ and â€˜-Pâ€™ options to switch between logical and physical
     modes.

   â€¢ The â€˜commandâ€™ builtin allows selectively skipping shell functions
     when performing command lookup (see Bash Builtins).

   â€¢ Bash uses the â€˜declareâ€™ builtin to modify the full set of variable
     and function attributes, and to assign values to variables.

   â€¢ The â€˜disownâ€™ builtin can remove a job from the internal shell job
     table (see Job Control Builtins) or suppress sending â€˜SIGHUPâ€™ to a
     job when the shell exits as the result of a â€˜SIGHUPâ€™.

   â€¢ The â€˜enableâ€™ builtin (see Bash Builtins) can enable or disable
     individual builtins and implements support for dynamically loading
     builtin commands from shared objects.

   â€¢ The Bash â€˜execâ€™ builtin takes additional options that allow users
     to control the contents of the environment passed to the executed
     command, and what the zeroth argument to the command is to be (see
     Bourne Shell Builtins).

   â€¢ Shell functions may be exported to children via the environment
     using â€˜export -fâ€™ (see Shell Functions).

   â€¢ The Bash â€˜exportâ€™ and â€˜readonlyâ€™ builtins (see Bourne Shell
     Builtins can take a â€˜-fâ€™ option to act on shell functions, a â€˜-pâ€™
     option to display variables with various attributes set in a format
     that can be used as shell input, a â€˜-nâ€™ option to remove various
     variable attributes, and â€˜name=valueâ€™ arguments to set variable
     attributes and values simultaneously.

   â€¢ The Bash â€˜hashâ€™ builtin allows a name to be associated with an
     arbitrary filename, even when that filename cannot be found by
     searching the â€˜$PATHâ€™, using â€˜hash -pâ€™ (see Bourne Shell Builtins).

   â€¢ Bash includes a â€˜helpâ€™ builtin for quick reference to shell
     facilities (see Bash Builtins).

   â€¢ Bash includes the â€˜mapfileâ€™ builtin to quickly read the contents of
     a file into an indexed array variable (see Bash Builtins).

   â€¢ The â€˜printfâ€™ builtin is available to display formatted output (see
     Bash Builtins), and has additional custom format specifiers and an
     option to assign the formatted output directly to a shell variable.

   â€¢ The Bash â€˜readâ€™ builtin (see Bash Builtins) will read a line ending
     in â€˜\â€™ with the â€˜-râ€™ option, and will use the â€˜REPLYâ€™ variable as a
     default if no non-option arguments are supplied.

   â€¢ The â€˜readâ€™ builtin (see Bash Builtins) accepts a prompt string with
     the â€˜-pâ€™ option and will use Readline to obtain the line when given
     the â€˜-eâ€™ or â€˜-Eâ€™ options, with the ability to insert text into the
     line using the â€˜-iâ€™ option.  The â€˜readâ€™ builtin also has additional
     options to control input: the â€˜-sâ€™ option will turn off echoing of
     input characters as they are read, the â€˜-tâ€™ option will allow
     â€˜readâ€™ to time out if input does not arrive within a specified
     number of seconds, the â€˜-nâ€™ option will allow reading only a
     specified number of characters rather than a full line, and the
     â€˜-dâ€™ option will read until a particular character rather than
     newline.

   â€¢ The â€˜returnâ€™ builtin may be used to abort execution of scripts
     executed with the â€˜.â€™ or â€˜sourceâ€™ builtins (see Bourne Shell
     Builtins).

   â€¢ Bash has much more optional behavior controllable with the â€˜setâ€™
     builtin (see The Set Builtin).

   â€¢ The â€˜-xâ€™ (â€˜xtraceâ€™) option displays commands other than simple
     commands when performing an execution trace (see The Set Builtin).

   â€¢ Bash includes the â€˜shoptâ€™ builtin, for finer control of shell
     optional capabilities (see The Shopt Builtin), and allows these
     options to be set and unset at shell invocation (see Invoking
     Bash).

   â€¢ The â€˜testâ€™ builtin (see Bourne Shell Builtins) is slightly
     different, as it implements the POSIX algorithm, which specifies
     the behavior based on the number of arguments.

   â€¢ The â€˜trapâ€™ builtin (see Bourne Shell Builtins) allows a â€˜DEBUGâ€™
     pseudo-signal specification, similar to â€˜EXITâ€™.  Commands specified
     with a â€˜DEBUGâ€™ trap are executed before every simple command, â€˜forâ€™
     command, â€˜caseâ€™ command, â€˜selectâ€™ command, every arithmetic â€˜forâ€™
     command, and before the first command executes in a shell function.
     The â€˜DEBUGâ€™ trap is not inherited by shell functions unless the
     function has been given the â€˜traceâ€™ attribute or the â€˜functraceâ€™
     option has been enabled using the â€˜shoptâ€™ builtin.  The â€˜extdebugâ€™
     shell option has additional effects on the â€˜DEBUGâ€™ trap.

     The â€˜trapâ€™ builtin (see Bourne Shell Builtins) allows an â€˜ERRâ€™
     pseudo-signal specification, similar to â€˜EXITâ€™ and â€˜DEBUGâ€™.
     Commands specified with an â€˜ERRâ€™ trap are executed after a simple
     command fails, with a few exceptions.  The â€˜ERRâ€™ trap is not
     inherited by shell functions unless the â€˜-o errtraceâ€™ option to the
     â€˜setâ€™ builtin is enabled.

     The â€˜trapâ€™ builtin (see Bourne Shell Builtins) allows a â€˜RETURNâ€™
     pseudo-signal specification, similar to â€˜EXITâ€™ and â€˜DEBUGâ€™.
     Commands specified with a â€˜RETURNâ€™ trap are executed before
     execution resumes after a shell function or a shell script executed
     with â€˜.â€™ or â€˜sourceâ€™ returns.  The â€˜RETURNâ€™ trap is not inherited
     by shell functions unless the function has been given the â€˜traceâ€™
     attribute or the â€˜functraceâ€™ option has been enabled using the
     â€˜shoptâ€™ builtin.

   â€¢ The Bash â€˜typeâ€™ builtin is more extensive and gives more
     information about the names it finds (see Bash Builtins).

   â€¢ The â€˜ulimitâ€™ builtin provides control over many more per-process
     resources (see Bash Builtins).

   â€¢ The Bash â€˜umaskâ€™ builtin uses the â€˜-pâ€™ option to display the output
     in the form of a â€˜umaskâ€™ command that may be reused as input (see
     Bourne Shell Builtins).

   â€¢ The Bash â€˜waitâ€™ builtin has a â€˜-nâ€™ option to wait for the next
     child to exit, possibly selecting from a list of supplied jobs, and
     the â€˜-pâ€™ option to store information about a terminated child
     process in a shell variable.

   â€¢ The SVR4.2 shell behaves differently when invoked as â€˜jshâ€™ (it
     turns on job control).

   â€¢ The SVR4.2 shell has two privilege-related builtins (â€˜mldmodeâ€™ and
     â€˜privâ€™) not present in Bash.

   â€¢ Bash does not have the â€˜stopâ€™ or â€˜newgrpâ€™ builtins.

   â€¢ Bash does not use the â€˜SHACCTâ€™ variable or perform shell
     accounting.

   â€¢ The SVR4.2 â€˜shâ€™ uses a â€˜TIMEOUTâ€™ variable like Bash uses â€˜TMOUTâ€™.

More features unique to Bash may be found in Bash Features.

B.1 Implementation Differences From The SVR4.2 Shell
====================================================

Since Bash is a completely new implementation, it does not suffer from
many of the limitations of the SVR4.2 shell.  For instance:

   â€¢ Bash does not fork a subshell when redirecting into or out of a
     shell control structure such as an â€˜ifâ€™ or â€˜whileâ€™ statement.

   â€¢ Bash does not allow unbalanced quotes.  The SVR4.2 shell will
     silently insert a needed closing quote at â€˜EOFâ€™ under certain
     circumstances.  This can be the cause of some hard-to-find errors.

   â€¢ The SVR4.2 shell uses a baroque memory management scheme based on
     trapping â€˜SIGSEGVâ€™.  If the shell is started from a process with
     â€˜SIGSEGVâ€™ blocked (e.g., by using the â€˜system()â€™ C library function
     call), it misbehaves badly.

   â€¢ In a questionable attempt at security, the SVR4.2 shell, when
     invoked without the â€˜-pâ€™ option, will alter its real and effective
     UID and GID if they are less than some magic threshold value,
     commonly 100.  This can lead to unexpected results.

   â€¢ The SVR4.2 shell does not allow users to trap â€˜SIGSEGVâ€™, â€˜SIGALRMâ€™,
     or â€˜SIGCHLDâ€™.

   â€¢ The SVR4.2 shell does not allow the â€˜IFSâ€™, â€˜MAILCHECKâ€™, â€˜PATHâ€™,
     â€˜PS1â€™, or â€˜PS2â€™ variables to be unset.

   â€¢ The SVR4.2 shell treats â€˜^â€™ as the undocumented equivalent of â€˜|â€™.

   â€¢ Bash allows multiple option arguments when it is invoked (â€˜-x -vâ€™);
     the SVR4.2 shell allows only one option argument (â€˜-xvâ€™).  In fact,
     some versions of the shell dump core if the second argument begins
     with a â€˜-â€™.

   â€¢ The SVR4.2 shell exits a script if any builtin fails; Bash exits a
     script only if one of the POSIX special builtins fails, and only
     for certain failures, as enumerated in the POSIX standard.

   â€¢ If the â€˜lastpipeâ€™ option is enabled, and job control is not active,
     Bash runs the last element of a pipeline in the current shell
     execution environment.

     texts or invariant sections, and (2) were thus incorporated prior
     to November 1, 2008.

     The operator of an MMC Site may republish an MMC contained in the
     site under CC-BY-SA on the same site at any time before August 1,
     2009, provided the MMC is eligible for relicensing.

ADDENDUM: How to use this License for your documents
====================================================

To use this License in a document you have written, include a copy of
the License in the document and put the following copyright and license
notices just after the title page:

       Copyright (C)  YEAR  YOUR NAME.
       Permission is granted to copy, distribute and/or modify this document
       under the terms of the GNU Free Documentation License, Version 1.3
       or any later version published by the Free Software Foundation;
       with no Invariant Sections, no Front-Cover Texts, and no Back-Cover
       Texts.  A copy of the license is included in the section entitled ``GNU
       Free Documentation License''.

   If you have Invariant Sections, Front-Cover Texts and Back-Cover
Texts, replace the "with...Texts."  line with this:

         with the Invariant Sections being LIST THEIR TITLES, with
         the Front-Cover Texts being LIST, and with the Back-Cover Texts
         being LIST.

   If you have Invariant Sections without Cover Texts, or some other
combination of the three, merge those two alternatives to suit the
situation.

   If your document contains nontrivial examples of program code, we
recommend releasing these examples in parallel under your choice of free
software license, such as the GNU General Public License, to permit
their use in free software.
